Plabic tangles and cluster promotion maps
Chaim Even-Zohar, Matteo Parisi, Melissa Sherman-Bennett, Ran Tessler, Lauren Williams

Abstract
. Inspired by the BCFW recurrence for tilings of the amplituhedron, we introduce the general framework of plabic tangles that utilizes plabic graphs to define rational maps between products of Grassmannians called promotions . The central conjecture of the paper is that promotion maps are quasi-cluster homomorphisms, which we prove for several classes of promotions. In order to define promotion maps, we utilize m m -vector-relation configurations ( m m -VRCs) on plabic graphs. We relate m m -VRCs to the degree (a.k.a ‘intersection number’) of the amplituhedron map on positroid varieties and characterize all plabic trees with intersection number one and their VRCs. Finally, we show that promotion maps admit an operad structure and, supported by the class of 4 4 -mass box promotion, we point at new positivity properties for non-rational maps beyond cluster algebras. Promotion maps have important connections to the geometry and cluster structure of the amplituhedron and singularities of scattering amplitudes in planar 𝒩 = 4 \mathcal{N}=4 super Yang–Mills theory.



1.
Introduction

The
positive Grassmannian

\Gr_{k,n}^{\scriptscriptstyle\geq 0}
is the subset of the real Grassmannian in which all Plücker coordinates are nonnegative. It has a decomposition into

positroid cells

[
Pos06
]
indexed by
plabic graphs
of type
(k,n)
, planar bicolored graphs which are embedded in a disk.
The (tree)
amplituhedron

\mathcal{A}_{n,k,m}(Z)
is the image of the
positive Grassmannian
\Gr_{k,n}^{\scriptscriptstyle\geq 0}
under the

amplituhedron map

{\tilde{Z}:\Gr_{k,n}^{\scriptscriptstyle\geq 0}\to\Gr_{k,k+m}}
.
It was introduced by
Arkani-Hamed and Trnka
[
AHT14
]
in order to give a
geometric interpretation of the
BCFW recurrence

[
BCFW05
]
for

scattering amplitudes
in
\mathcal{N}=4
super Yang Mills theory (SYM).
In particular, each way of iterating the BCFW recurrence gives rise
to a collection of positroid cells of
\Gr_{k,n}^{\scriptscriptstyle\geq 0}
. Arkani-Hamed and Trnka conjectured that the amplituhedron map
\tilde{Z}
is injective on these cells, that the
\tilde{Z}
-images of these cells
‘tile’ the
m=4
amplituhedron
\mathcal{A}_{n,k,4}(Z)
, and that one can compute
scattering amplitudes by
summing the ‘volumes’ of the tiles.

In our previous work
[
ELP+23
]
, which was joint with Lakrec,
we proved the above
BCFW tiling conjecture
of Arkani-Hamed and Trnka. We also proved
the
cluster adjacency conjecture
, which says that
facets of tiles are cut out by collections of
compatible cluster variables
. To prove these conjectures, we used a graphical recurrence
[
BH15
, (3.3)]
on plabic graphs that involves inserting two smaller plabic graphs into faces of a fixed “core” plabic graph, see
Figure

3
. In particular, we showed that this graphical recurrence gives rise to a
quasi-cluster homomorphism

\mathbb{C}(\widehat{\Gr}_{4,N_{L}})\otimes\mathbb{C}(\widehat{\Gr}_{4,N_{R}})\to\mathbb{C}(\widehat{\Gr}_{4,n}),

that is, a map that respects the
cluster algebras structure
on the Grassmannian, and in particular takes cluster variables to cluster variables.
1
1

1

up to a Laurent monomial in frozen variables.

The goal of this paper is to illustrate that the particular graphical recurrence and quasi-cluster homomorphism that we used to prove the BCFW tiling and cluster adjacency conjectures is part of a much vaster framework.
In particular, in
Section

4
, we introduce the notion of
plabic tangle
, which is the data of a plabic graph “core”
G
drawn inside an
outer disk
, together with
\ell

inner disks
, each of which lies in a face of
G
. The definition of plabic tangle is inspired by the notion of
planar tangle

[
Jon22
]
, and we show that similarly to the case of planar tangles, there is an
operad structure
on plabic tangles, see
Section

9
. The central construction of this paper
is the association of a map between Grassmannians to each (sufficiently nice)
plabic tangle.
We note that while plabic graphs have been previously used to define subsets of the Grassmannian

[
Pos06
]
,
or to define functions on Grassmannians or rings of invariants
[
Kup96
,
FP16
]
, this is to our knowledge the first time that plabic graphs have been used to define
maps
between (products of) Grassmannians or between their coordinate rings.

In order to associate maps to plabic tangles, we start with the framework of
vector relation configurations
(see
Section

3
), inspired by
[
AGPR24
]
2
2

2

our definitions and results are rather different than those of
[
AGPR24
]
; see
Remark

3.5
for a comparison.
.
An
m
-vector-relation configuration
(
m
-VRC) on a bipartite plabic graph
G
(with
n
boundary vertices, colored black) is an assignment of a vector
v_{b}\in\mathbb{C}^{m}
to each black vertex and a scalar
r_{e}\in\mathbb{C}^{*}
to each edge such that the
boundary vectors

v_{1},\dots,v_{n}
on vertices
1,\dots n
span
\mathbb{C}^{m}
, and for each white vertex
w
, we have the linear relation

\sum_{e=\{b,w\}}r_{e}v_{b}=0.

Suppose
G
is
(
m
-generically) solvable
, that is, a generic configuration of
n
vectors in
\mathbb{C}^{m}
on the outer boundary of
G
can be extended to a unique VRC (modulo gauge) on
G
. If we have a plabic tangle with core
G
, where each inner disk
D
is connected to some black vertices of
G
, and we choose a scaling for our internal vectors
v_{b}
, we can use the VRC to define a rational map from the Grassmannian
\Gr_{m,n}

associated to the outer boundary of
G
, to the product of Grassmannians
\Gr_{m,D}

associated to the inner disks of
G
. The pullback of this map is a map on the level of coordinate rings; we refer to these two maps as
geometric
and
algebraic promotion
.
We will be particularly interested in when geometric promotion is a dominant map
3
3

3

i.e. its image is Zariski-dense in the codomain.
, in which case we call the tangle
dominant
; see
Theorem

4.13
for a combinatorial characterization.

We believe that dominant solvable plabic tangles are the appropriate setting for generalizing our results on the BCFW story. In particular,
we believe that they give rise to quasi-cluster homomorphisms.

Conjecture

(
4.16
)
.

Let
(G,\mathbf{D})
be a dominant solvable plabic tangle. Then there exists a normalization of the vectors in the
m
-VRCs on
G
such that the following hold.

(1)

Geometric promotion by
(G,\mathbf{D})

\psi:\Gr_{m,n}\;\dashrightarrow\;\Gr_{m,D^{(1)}}\times\dots\times\Gr_{m,D^{(\ell)}}

sends totally positive elements to totally positive elements.

(2)

Algebraic promotion by
(G,\mathbf{D})

\Psi=\psi^{*}:\mathbb{C}({\Gr}_{m,D^{(1)}})\otimes\dots\otimes\mathbb{C}({\Gr}_{m,D^{(\ell)}})\;\to\;\mathbb{C}({\Gr}_{m,n})

is a quasi-cluster homomorphism,
after we freeze some variables on the right-hand side.

4.16
generalizes the BCFW quasi-cluster homomorphism (reviewed in
Section

5.2
), and asserts a deep connection between
m
-VRCs on solvable plabic graphs and the cluster structure on the Grassmannian. Moreover,
we prove it for several interesting infinite families of promotion maps.

Theorem
.

4.16
is true in the case of:
star promotion
(
k=1
, any
m,n
);
spurion promotion
(
k=2
,
m=4
, any
n
);
chain-tree promotion
(
k=3
,
m=4
, any
n
); and
forest promotion
(
k=2
,
m=3
, any
n
). For each promotion, the core is of type
(k,n)
, see
Section

4
.

The second part of our paper shows that
m
-VRCs on solvable graphs
G
are also closely related to inverting the amplituhedron map. Let
\Pi_{G}\subset\Gr_{k,n}
be the
positroid variety
of
G
, that is, the Zariski closure of the
positroid cell
S_{G}
. We say that

G
and

\Pi_{G}
have
m
-intersection number
d

if for generic
Z\in\Mat_{n,k+m}
,
the (rational) amplituhedron map
\tilde{Z}:\Gr_{k,n}\dashedrightarrow\Gr_{k,k+m}
is generically
d
-to-1 on
\Pi_{G}.
We prove that
G
is
m
-generically solvable if and only if
\Pi_{G}
has
m
-intersection number 1 and
\dim\Pi_{G}=km
(see
Corollary

6.12
). In this situation, the
m
-VRCs on
G
can be used to generically invert the amplituhedron map on
\Pi_{G}
(see
Remark

6.16
). More generally, we show that the intersection number can be computed in terms of
m
-VRCs (see
Corollary

6.10
).

In the third part of our paper, we turn our attention to
plabic trees
. This special class already demonstrates the range of promotion maps in our conjecture and the power of the
m
-VRC formulation of intersection number. We characterize the plabic trees which have
m
-intersection number 1, or equivalently, the
m
-generically solvable plabic trees.

We say that a bipartite plabic tree
G
is
m
-balanced
if
for each edge
e
of
G
, if we write

G\setminus\{e\}=G_{1}\sqcup G_{2}
(giving “half” the edge
e
to each
G_{i}
), then for each
i=1,2
, we have

m(k_{G_{i}}-1)<\dim\Pi_{G_{i}}\leq mk_{G_{i}},

where
k_{G_{i}}
is the
k
-statistic of the tree
G_{i}
.
We have the following result, proved using
m
-VRCs.

Theorem

(
Theorem

7.23
)
.

Let
G
be a bipartite plabic tree of type
(k,km+1)
. Then

G
has
m
-intersection number 1
if and only if
G
is
m
-balanced. If
G
is not
m
-balanced, then
G
has
m
-intersection number
0
.

We note that using this characterization, one can produce for each
m
various infinite families of solvable plabic trees, and hence infinite families of promotion maps.

Finally, we explore to what extent our constructions, such as the operad framework and the notion of promotion, make sense when we work with a plabic graph
G
of higher intersection number. In
Section

10
we study an intersection number
2
cell called the

4
-mass box
, which has previously arisen in the physics literature
4
4

4

It associated to a Feynman diagram with one cycle (‘loop’) and
4
vertices - hence a ‘box’. At each vertex a ‘massive’ momentum flows - hence ‘
4
-mass’
[
tV79
]
. The
leading singularity
associated to the diagram is encoded by the positroid cell labelled by the plabic graph
G

[
AHBC+16b
]
.
.
We obtain two “promotion” maps from this cell, which cannot be quasi-cluster homomorphisms, because they involve a square root. On the other hand, these maps still possess an intriguing positivity property, namely
(1) of
4.16
above. This could point to a new algebraic structure beyond the framework of cluster algebras.

Theorem

(
Theorem

10.10
)
.

The
4
-mass box promotion maps
\Psi_{+},\Psi_{-}
preserves positivity of cluster variables for
\Gr_{4,N^{\prime}}
, i.e. if
x
is a cluster variable for
\Gr_{4,N^{\prime}}
, then
\Psi_{+}(x),\Psi_{-}(x)
are positive on
\Gr^{>0}_{4,n}
.

On the physics side, we expect singularities of
Yangian invariants
- building blocks of scattering amplitudes in
\mathcal{N}=4
SYM - to be images of algebraic promotion maps, generalizing what we have seen with BCFW promotions. Our framework should thus shed light on the conjectural cluster structures and positivity pheonomena that arise in the study of scattering amplitudes, see
Remarks

2.29
and
10.14
. Promotions maps should also play an important role in describing the geometry and the cluster structure of amplituhedron tiles and, more generally, images of positroid cells under the amplituhedron map. This will be the subject of a future work.

2.
Background

2.1.
The Grassmannian and the configuration space

We start by defining the Grass- mannian and the configuration space.
In this paper we will usually be working with these spaces defined over the
complex numbers
\mathbb{C}
.

Definition 2.1
.

The
Grassmannian

\Gr_{k,n}=\Gr_{k,n}(\mathbb{F}):=\{V\ |\ V\subseteq\mathbb{F}^{n},\ \dim V=k\}

is the space of all
k
-dimensional subspaces of
an
n
-dimensional vector space
\mathbb{F}^{n}
.
We let
\Gr^{\circ}_{k,n}
denote the subset of the Grassmannian where no Plücker coordinates vanish, where Plücker coordinates
are defined below.

Let
[n]
denote
\{1,\dots,n\}
, and
\binom{[n]}{k}
denote the set of all
k
-element
subsets of
[n]
.
We can use full rank
k\times n
matrices
C
to represent points of
\Gr_{k,n}
.
Recall that the
Plücker coordinates
give an embedding of the
Grassmannian into projective space. More specifically, for
I=\{i_{1}<\dots<i_{k}\}\in\binom{[n]}{k}
, we let
\langle I\rangle_{V}=\langle i_{1}\,i_{2}\,\dots\,i_{k}\rangle_{V}
be the
k\times k
minor of
C
using the columns
I
.
The
\langle I\rangle_{V}

are called the
Plücker coordinates
of
V
, and are independent of the choice of matrix
representative
C
(up to common rescaling). The
Plücker embedding

V\mapsto\{\langle I\rangle_{V}\}_{I\in\binom{[n]}{k}}

embeds
\Gr_{k,n}
into
projective space. When it does not cause confusion, we will identify
C
with its row-span and drop the subscript
V
on Plücker coordinates.
If
C
has columns
v_{1},\dots,v_{n}
, we may also identify
\langle i_{1}\,i_{2}\,\dots\,i_{k}\rangle_{V}
with the element
v_{i_{1}}\wedge v_{i_{2}}\wedge\dots\wedge v_{i_{k}}
, hence the Plücker coordinates are
alternating
in the indices, e.g.
\langle i_{1}\,i_{2}\,\dots\,i_{k}\rangle=-\langle i_{2}\,i_{1}\,\dots\,i_{k}\rangle
.

The torus
(\mathbb{C}^{*})^{n}
acts on
\Gr_{k,n}
by scaling the
i
th column of
a representative matrix
C
. Because the element
(t,\dots,t)\in(\mathbb{C}^{*})^{n}

scales all Plücker coordinates by
t^{k}
, the action of
(\mathbb{C}^{*})^{n}
factors
through the torus
T:=(\mathbb{C}^{*})^{n}/\mathbb{C}^{*}\cong(\mathbb{C}^{*})^{n-1}
.

Definition 2.2
.

The
configuration space

\Conf_{k,n}:=\GL_{k}\setminus\{(v_{1},\dots,v_{n})\ |\ v_{i}\in\mathbb{P}^{k-1}\}
parameterizes the space of
n
ordered points in
\mathbb{P}^{k-1}
, considered up to the action of
\GL_{k}
.
We let
\Conf^{\circ}_{k,n}
denote the subset of the configuration space where any
k
of the points
v_{1},\dots,v_{n}
affinely
span a subspace of
\mathbb{P}^{k-1}
of dimension
k-1
.

Note that there is an isomorphism
\Gr^{\circ}_{k,n}/T\cong\Conf^{\circ}_{k,n}
.

If
D
is an index set with a total order, with
|D|=n
, we will also use

\Gr_{k,D}
and
\Conf_{k,D}
to denote
the Grassmannian and configuration spaces
\Gr_{k,n}
and
\Conf_{k,n}
,
where the columns of a representing matrix are indexed by
D

(and similarly for
\Gr^{\circ}_{k,D}
and
\Conf^{\circ}_{k,D}
).

Definition 2.3

(Positive Grassmannian and positroids)
.

[
Lus94
,
Pos06
]

We say that
V\in\Gr_{k,n}(\mathbb{R})
is
totally nonnegative

if (up to a global change of sign)

\langle I\rangle_{V}\geq 0
for all
I\in\binom{[n]}{k}
.
Similarly,
V
is
totally positive
if
\langle I\rangle_{V}>0
for all
I\in\binom{[n]}{k}
.
We let
\Gr_{k,n}^{\geq 0}
and
\Gr_{k,n}^{>0}
denote the set of
totally nonnegative and totally positive elements of
\Gr_{k,n}(\mathbb{R})
, respectively.

\Gr_{k,n}^{\geq 0}
is called the
totally nonnegative

Grassmannian
, or
sometimes just the
positive Grassmannian
.

If we partition
\Gr_{k,n}^{\geq 0}
into strata based on which Plücker coordinates are strictly
positive and which are
0
, we obtain a cell decomposition of
\Gr_{k,n}^{\geq 0}

into
positroid cells

S_{G}
, which can be indexed by
(equivalence classes of) plabic graphs
G

[
Pos06
]
,
see
Appendix

A
for background.
Each positroid cell
S_{G}
gives rise to a matroid
\mathcal{M}_{G}
, whose bases are precisely
the
k
-element subsets
I
such that the Plücker coordinate

\langle I\rangle
does not vanish on
S_{G}
; this matroid
is called a
positroid
. The bases of
\mathcal{M}_{G}
are exactly the source sets of
perfect orientations
of
G
, see
Definition

A.4
.

The Zariski closure of
S_{G}
in
\Gr_{k,n}(\mathbb{C})

is called
the
positroid variety

\Pi_{G}
. The positroid variety is cut out by setting to
0
all Plücker coordinates
\langle I\rangle
that vanish on
S_{G}
, or equivalently, all
\langle I\rangle
such that
I\notin\mathcal{M}_{G}

[
KLS13
]
. There is a map
\mathbb{B}_{G}
called the
boundary measurement map
which is an isomorphism from
(\mathbb{C}^{*})^{\dim\Pi_{G}}
to a subset of
\Pi_{G}
; see
Definition

A.10
. We use
T_{G}
to denote the image of
\mathbb{B}_{G}
, and call
T_{G}
the
boundary measurement torus
.

The following lemma will be useful to us. We use the notation
V^{\perp}
for the orthogonal complement of a subspace
V
.

Lemma 2.4
.

Let
G
be a
(k,n)
-plabic graph and
G^{op}
the plabic graph obtained by switching the colors of all vertices. Then

\displaystyle\Pi_{G}

\displaystyle\to\Pi_{G^{op}}

\displaystyle V

\displaystyle\mapsto V^{\perp}

is an involutive isomorphism of varieties.

Proof.

For
I\in\binom{[n]}{k}
, and
I^{c}=[n]\setminus I
its complement, we have

\langle I\rangle_{V}=\pm\langle I^{c}\rangle_{V^{\perp}}

where the sign is determined by
I
(see the discussion around Lemma 1.11 in
[
Kar17
]
). Thus,
\langle I\rangle_{V}=0
if and only if
\langle I^{c}\rangle_{V^{\perp}}=0
. The matroids
\mathcal{M}_{G}
and
\mathcal{M}_{G^{op}}
are duals of each other, meaning that
I\in\mathcal{M}_{G}
if and only if
I^{c}\in\mathcal{M}_{G^{op}}
. This shows that the map is well-defined. It is easy to see that it is regular, with regular inverse.
∎

2.2.
Cluster algebras

Cluster algebras were introduced by Fomin and Zelevinsky in
[
FZ02
]
; see
[
FWZc
]
for
an introduction.
We give a quick definition of cluster algebras from quivers here.

A
quiver
is a finite directed graph. A
loop
of a quiver is an edge whose
source and target coincide.

Definition 2.5

(
Quiver Mutation
)
.

Let
Q
be a quiver without loops or oriented
2
-cycles.
Let
k
be a vertex of
Q
.
Following
[
FZ02
]
, we define the

mutated quiver

\mu_{k}(Q)
as follows:
it has the same set of vertices as
Q
,
and its set of arrows is obtained by the following procedure:

(1)

for each subquiver
i\to k\to j
, add a new arrow
i\to j
;

(2)

reverse all arrows with source or target
k
;

(3)

remove the arrows in a maximal set of pairwise
disjoint oriented
2
-cycles.

It is not hard to check that mutation is an involution, that is,

\mu_{k}^{2}(Q)=Q
for each vertex
k
.

Definition 2.6

(
Seeds
)
.

Choose
s\geq r
positive integers.
Let
\mathcal{F}
be an
ambient field

of rational functions
in
r
independent
variables
over

\mathbb{C}(x_{r+1},\dots,x_{s})
.
A
seed
in
\mathcal{F}
is
a pair
(\widetilde{\mathbf{x}},Q)
, where

•

\widetilde{\mathbf{x}}=(x_{1},\dots,x_{s})
forms a free generating
set for

\mathcal{F}
,
and

•

Q
is a quiver on vertices

1,2,\dots,r,r+1,\dots,s
,
whose vertices
1,2,\dots,r
are called

mutable
, and whose vertices
r+1,\dots,s
are called
frozen
.

We call
\widetilde{\mathbf{x}}
the

cluster
of a seed
(\widetilde{\mathbf{x}},Q)
, and its elements are called
cluster variables
.
The variables
c=\{x_{r+1},\dots,x_{s}\}
are called

frozen
(or
coefficient variables
).
We let
\mathbb{M}
denote the group of Laurent monomials in the frozen variables, which we call the
frozen group
.

Definition 2.7

(
Seed mutations
)
.

Let
(\widetilde{\mathbf{x}},Q)
be a seed in
\mathcal{F}
,
and let
k
be a mutable vertex of
Q
.
The
seed mutation

\mu_{k}
in direction
k
transforms

(\widetilde{\mathbf{x}},Q)
into the seed

\mu_{k}(\widetilde{\mathbf{x}},Q)=(\widetilde{\mathbf{x}}^{\prime},\mu_{k}(Q))
, where the cluster

\widetilde{\mathbf{x}}^{\prime}=(x^{\prime}_{1},\dots,x^{\prime}_{s})
is defined as follows:

x_{j}^{\prime}=x_{j}
for
j\neq k
,
and
x^{\prime}_{k}\in\mathcal{F}
is determined
by the
exchange relation

(1)

x^{\prime}_{k}\ x_{k}=\ \prod_{\begin{subarray}{c}i\to k\end{subarray}}x_{i}+\ \prod_{\begin{subarray}{c}k\to i\end{subarray}}x_{i}\,.

Note that arrows between two frozen vertices of a quiver do not
affect seed mutation; therefore we often omit
arrows between two frozen vertices.

Definition 2.8

(
Cluster algebra
)
.

Given an initial labeled seed
(\widetilde{\mathbf{x}},Q)
, we let
\mathcal{X}
denote the union of all cluster variables obtained performing all possible mutation sequences from the initial seed.
Let
\mathbb{C}[c^{\pm 1}]
be the
ground ring
consisting
of Laurent polynomials in the frozen variables. The

cluster algebra

\mathcal{A}=\mathcal{A}(\widetilde{\mathbf{x}},Q)
associated with a
given pattern is the
\mathbb{C}[c^{\pm 1}]
-subalgebra of the ambient field
\mathcal{F}

generated by all cluster variables,
with coefficients which are Laurent polynomials
in the frozen variables:
\mathcal{A}=\mathbb{C}[c^{\pm 1}][\mathcal{X}]
.
We say that
\mathcal{A}
has
rank
r
because each cluster contains

r
cluster variables. Cluster (or frozen) variables that belong to a common cluster are said to be
compatible
.

Remark 2.9
.

Another common convention is to choose the ring

\mathbb{C}[c]
of polynomials in the frozen variables as the ground ring,
and define the cluster algebra
as
\overline{\mathcal{A}}:=\mathbb{C}[c][\mathcal{X}]
.

The Grassmannian
\Gr_{m,n}(\mathbb{C})
has a natural cluster algebra structure, generated by the Plücker coordinates. A standard seed for this cluster structure is the
rectangles seed

\Sigma_{m,n}
, with
m(n-m)+1
variables,
n
of which are frozen, as demonstrated in
8.2
below. The cluster algebra
\overline{\mathcal{A}}(\Sigma_{m,n})
is the homogeneous coordinate ring
\mathbb{C}[\Gr_{m,n}]
of the complex Grassmannian
[
Sco06
,
FWZa
]
. We refer to any seed for this cluster algebra as a
seed for

\Gr_{m,n}
.

2.3.
Quasi-cluster homomorphisms of cluster algebras

In this section we define quasi- cluster homomorphisms of cluster
algebras, following
[
Fra16
]
and
[
Fra20
]
.

Definition 2.10

(Exchange ratios)
.

Given a seed
\Sigma=((x_{1},\dots,x_{s}),Q)
for a cluster algebra of rank
r\leq s
, and a mutable variable
x_{i}
(so that
1\leq i\leq r
), the
exchange ratio
of
x_{i}
(with respect to
\Sigma
) is

\hat{y}_{\Sigma}(x_{i})\;=\;\frac{\prod_{j:i\to j}x_{j}^{\#\arr(i\to j)}}{\prod_{j:j\to i}x_{j}^{\#\arr(j\to i)}}

where
\arr(i\to j)
denotes the number of arrows from
i
to
j
in the quiver
Q
.

Given a cluster algebra
\mathcal{A}
, we let
\mathbb{M}
denote its frozen group, that is the group of Laurent monomials
in the frozen variables. For elements
x,y\in\mathcal{A}
, we say that

x
is proportional to
y
, writing
x\propto y
, if
x=My
for some Laurent monomial

M\in\mathbb{M}
. We then refer to
M
as a
frozen factor
.

Definition 2.11

(Quasi-cluster homomorphism)
.

[
Fra16
, Definition 3.1 and Proposition 3.2]

Let
\mathcal{A}
and
\overline{\mathcal{A}}
be two cluster algebras of the same rank
r
,
and with respective frozen groups
\mathbb{M}
and
\overline{\mathbb{M}}
.
Then an algebra homomorphism
f:\mathcal{A}\to\overline{\mathcal{A}}
that satisfies

f(\mathbb{M})\subseteq\overline{\mathbb{M}}
is called a
quasi-cluster homomorphism

from
\mathcal{A}
to
\overline{\mathcal{A}}

if there are seeds

\Sigma=((x_{1},\dots,x_{s}),Q)
and
\overline{\Sigma}=((\bar{x}_{\bar{1}},\dots,\bar{x}_{\bar{s}}),\bar{Q})

for
\mathcal{A}
and
\overline{\mathcal{A}}
, such that

(1)

f(x_{i})\propto\bar{x}_{\bar{i}}
for
1\leq i\leq r

(2)

f(\hat{y}_{\Sigma}(x_{i}))=\hat{y}_{\overline{\Sigma}}(\bar{x}_{\bar{i}})
for
1\leq i\leq r
.

If conditions (1) and (2) hold,
we write
f(\Sigma)\propto\overline{\Sigma}
.

Note that conditions (1) and (2) imply that the map
i\mapsto\bar{i}
of mutable nodes in
Q
and
\bar{Q}
extends to an isomorphism of the corresponding induced subquivers. If we want to show
f(\Sigma)\propto\overline{\Sigma}
, we often start by finding an isomorphism between the mutable nodes of
Q
and
\bar{Q}
and then check that (1) and (2) are satisfied.

Proposition 2.12
.

[
Fra16
, Proposition 3.2]

If

\Sigma\propto\overline{\Sigma}
, then

\mu_{k}(\Sigma)
is similar to
\mu_{k}(\overline{\Sigma})
.

2.4.
Background on the Grassmann-Cayley algebra

The
Grassmann-Cayley algebra
, the exterior algebra endowed with an additional
shuffle product
,
is the natural setting for many of our computations. We recall the definitions and basic facts here. Let

S_{n}^{r}:=\{w\in S_{n}:w(1)<\cdots<w(r),w(r+1)<\cdots<w(n)\}.

For vectors
v_{1},\dots,v_{m}\in\mathbb{C}^{m}
, we use the notation
\langle v_{1}\cdots v_{m}\rangle:=\det[v_{1}\dots v_{m}]
.

Definition 2.13
.

Let
A=a_{1}\wedge\dots\wedge a_{p}\in\bigwedge^{p}\mathbb{C}^{m}
and
B=b_{1}\wedge\dots\wedge b_{q}\in\bigwedge^{q}\mathbb{C}^{m}
and set
s:=p+q-m
. The
shuffle

A*B
of
A
and
B
is
0
if
s<0
. If
s\geq 0
, then we define

\displaystyle A*B:=

\displaystyle\sum_{w\in S_{p}^{m-q}}\sign(w)~\langle a_{w(1)}\cdots a_{w(m-q)}b_{1}\cdots b_{q}\rangle~a_{w(m-q+1)}\wedge\dots\wedge a_{w(p)}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\sum_{w\in S_{q}^{s}}\sign(w)~\langle a_{1}\cdots a_{p}b_{w(s+1)}\cdots b_{w(q)}\rangle~b_{w(1)}\wedge\dots\wedge b_{w(s)}.

In the degenerate case where
s\geq 0
and
p=0
, so
A
is the scalar
a
, we have
q=m
and define
A*B:=a\langle b_{1}\cdots b_{q}\rangle
; analogously, if
s\geq 0
and
q=0
so
B
is the scalar
b
,
A*B:=b\langle a_{1}\cdots a_{p}\rangle
.
Note that if
s=0
, then

A*B
is the determinant
\langle a_{1}\cdots a_{p}b_{1}\cdots b_{q}\rangle
.

We extend the operation
*
to arbitrary elements of the exterior algebra
\bigwedge\mathbb{C}^{m}
by linearity. In compound expressions, we adopt the order of operations precedence rule that the
\wedge
product is performed before the
*
product.

Lemma 2.14
.

The operation
*
has the following properties.

•

If
A\in\bigwedge^{p}\mathbb{C}^{m}
and
B\in\bigwedge^{q}\mathbb{C}^{m}
, then
A*B=(-1)^{(m-p)(m-q)}B*A
.

•

The operation
*
is associative:
(A*B)*C=A*(B*C)
.

Definition 2.15
.

The
Grassmann-Cayley algebra

\GC(m)
is the exterior algebra
\bigwedge\mathbb{C}^{m}
endowed with the additional
shuffle product

*:\bigwedge\mathbb{C}^{m}\times\bigwedge\mathbb{C}^{m}\to\bigwedge\mathbb{C}^{m}
, which is associative.

We summarize some additional basic properties of
\wedge
and
*
, and their relation to intersections and sums of subspaces. For
A=a_{1}\wedge\dots\wedge a_{p}\in\bigwedge\mathbb{C}^{m}
, write
\overline{A}
for
\spn({a_{1},\dots,a_{p}})
. We say
A

represents

\overline{A}
.

Lemma 2.16
.

Let
A=a_{1}\wedge\dots\wedge a_{p}
and
B=b_{1}\wedge\dots\wedge b_{q}
be decomposable elements of
\GC(m)
.

•

We have
A\wedge B\neq 0
if and only if
\overline{A}\cap\overline{B}=\{0\}
, and then
A\wedge B
represents
\overline{A}+\overline{B}=\overline{A}\oplus\overline{B}.

•

If
\overline{A}+\overline{B}\neq\mathbb{C}^{m}
, then
A*B=0
.

•

If
\overline{A}+\overline{B}=\mathbb{C}^{m}
and
\overline{A}\cap\overline{B}=\{0\}
, then
A*B=\langle a_{1},\dots,a_{p},b_{1},\dots,b_{q}\rangle
is a scalar.

•

If
\overline{A}+\overline{B}=\mathbb{C}^{m}
and
\overline{A}\cap\overline{B}\neq\{0\}
, then
A*B
is again decomposable and represents
\overline{A}\cap\overline{B}
.

Using these properties, it is straightforward to see that, under some constraints on dimension, wedge and shuffle products represent sums and intersections, respectively, of subspaces.

Lemma 2.17
.

Suppose
A_{1},\dots,A_{p}\in\bigwedge\mathbb{C}^{m}
are decomposable.

•

{A_{1}\wedge\dots\wedge A_{p}}
represents
\overline{A_{1}}+\dots+\overline{A_{p}}
if

\dim\overline{A_{1}+\dots+A_{p}}=\sum_{i}\dim\overline{A_{i}}

and otherwise
{A_{1}\wedge\dots\wedge A_{p}}=0
.

•

{A_{1}*\dots*A_{p}}
represents
\bigcap_{i}\overline{A_{i}}
if

\dim\left(\bigcap_{i}\overline{A_{i}}\right)=\left(\sum_{i}\dim\overline{A_{i}}\right)-(p-1)m\geq 1

and otherwise
{A_{1}*\dots*A_{p}}
is a scalar.

The following relation, which is easy to verify, will also be useful to us.

Lemma 2.18
.

Let
A_{1}\in\bigwedge^{d_{1}}\mathbb{C}^{m},\dots,A_{p}\in\bigwedge^{d_{p}}\mathbb{C}^{m}
be decomposable, and suppose
d_{1}+\dots+d_{p}=m+1
. Then

\sum_{i=1}^{p}(-1)^{d_{i}(d_{i}+d_{i+1}+\dots+d_{p})}~(A_{1}\wedge\dots\wedge\widehat{A_{i}}\wedge\dots\wedge A_{p})*A_{i}=0

We will often use the Grassmann-Cayley algebra
\GC(m)
to define regular functions on the Grassmannian
\Gr_{m,n}
, or functions from one Grassmannian to another. If
V\in\Gr_{m,n}
is represented by the matrix
[v_{1}\cdots v_{n}]
, we often write
v_{i}
as just
i
. We also often omit the symbol
\wedge
, writing
i_{1}\dots i_{j}
for
v_{i_{1}}\wedge\cdots\wedge v_{i_{j}}
.

Notation 2.19
.

If
F\in\GC(m)
is a scalar, we sometimes write
\langle F\rangle
for
F
, to emphasize that it is a scalar. As is consistent with our previous convention with Plücker coordinates, if
F=v_{1}\wedge\cdots\wedge v_{m}\in\bigwedge^{m}\mathbb{C}^{m}
, we write
\langle F\rangle:=\det[v_{1}\dots v_{n}]
.

Definition 2.20
.

Consider
\GC(m)
and
V\in\Gr_{m,n}
represented by
[v_{1}\cdots v_{n}]
, and let
A=\{a_{1},\dots,a_{i}\}
,
B=\{b_{1},\dots,b_{j}\}
and
C=\{c_{1},\dots,c_{k}\}
be ordered sets of indices in
[n]
such that
i+j\geq m
,
i+k\geq m
,
j+k\geq m
, and
i+j+k=2m
. Then

A*B*C\;=\;({a_{1}}\cdots{a_{i}})*({b_{1}}\cdots{b_{j}})*({c_{1}}\cdots{c_{k}})\;=\;(v_{a_{1}}\wedge\cdots\wedge v_{a_{i}})*(v_{b_{1}}\wedge\cdots\wedge v_{b_{j}})*(v_{c_{1}}\wedge\cdots\wedge v_{c_{k}})

is a scalar, and we use the notation
\langle A*B*C\rangle:=A*B*C
.
We call
\langle A*B*C\rangle
a
chain polynomial
.

Remark 2.21
.

For
m=4
,
[
ELP+23
, Definition 2.5]
defined a “chain polynomial” using the notation
\langle abc\,|\,de\,|\,fgh\rangle
. We have
\langle abc*de*fgh\rangle=-\langle abc\,|\,de\,|\,fgh\rangle.

Example 2.22
.

On the Grassmannian
\Gr_{m,n}
the chain polynomials of
Definition

2.20
are quadratic polynomials in the Plücker coordinates.
For example, let
m=3
, and
i=j=k=2
.
Let
A=\{a_{1},a_{2}\}
,
B=\{b_{1},b_{2}\}
, and
C=\{c_{1},c_{2}\}

be ordered sets of indices in
[n]
.
Then

\displaystyle\langle A*B*C\rangle

\displaystyle=(A*B)*C=A*(B*C)

\displaystyle=\langle{a_{1}}{b_{1}}{b_{2}}\rangle\langle{a_{2}}{c_{1}}{c_{2}}\rangle-\langle{a_{2}}{b_{1}}{b_{2}}\rangle\langle{a_{1}}{c_{1}}{c_{2}}\rangle

\displaystyle=\langle{a_{1}}{a_{2}}{b_{2}}\rangle\langle{b_{1}}{c_{1}}{c_{2}}\rangle-\langle{a_{1}}{a_{2}}{b_{1}}\rangle\langle{b_{2}}{c_{1}}{c_{2}}\rangle.

Example 2.23
.

Let
A=\{a_{1},a_{2},a_{3}\},B=\{b_{1},b_{2},b_{3}\},C=\{c_{1},c_{2},c_{3}\}
be ordered sets of indices in
[n]
.
For
V\in\Gr_{4,n}
represented by
[v_{1}\cdots v_{n}]
, consider the element

a_{3}(B*C)\;:=\;v_{a_{3}}\wedge((v_{b_{1}}\wedge v_{b_{2}}\wedge v_{b_{3}})*(v_{c_{1}}\wedge v_{c_{2}}\wedge v_{c_{3}}))\;\in\;\GC(m).

•

By definition, we have

\displaystyle a_{3}(B*C)

\displaystyle=a_{3}(\langle b_{1}b_{2}b_{3}c_{3}\rangle c_{1}c_{2}-\langle b_{1}b_{2}b_{3}c_{2}\rangle c_{1}c_{3}+\langle b_{1}b_{2}b_{3}c_{1}\rangle c_{2}c_{3})

\displaystyle=\langle b_{1}b_{2}b_{3}c_{3}\rangle a_{3}c_{1}c_{2}-\langle b_{1}b_{2}b_{3}c_{2}\rangle a_{3}c_{1}c_{3}+\langle b_{1}b_{2}b_{3}c_{1}\rangle a_{3}c_{2}c_{3}

\displaystyle=\langle b_{1}c_{1}c_{2}c_{3}\rangle a_{3}b_{2}b_{3}-\langle b_{2}c_{1}c_{2}c_{3}\rangle a_{3}b_{1}b_{3}+\langle b_{3}c_{1}c_{2}c_{3}\rangle a_{3}b_{1}b_{2}.

Provided that all vectors involved are sufficiently generic,
a_{3}(B*C)
represents the 3-plane spanned by
v_{a_{3}}
and the 2-plane

\spn(v_{b_{1}},v_{b_{2}},v_{b_{3}})\cap\spn(v_{c_{1}},v_{c_{2}},v_{c_{3}})
.

•

We also have

\displaystyle(a_{1}a_{2})*(a_{3}(B*C))\;

\displaystyle=\;\langle a_{1}a_{3}*B*C\rangle a_{2}-\langle a_{2}a_{3}*B*C\rangle a_{1}

\displaystyle=\;\langle a_{1}a_{2}*B*C\rangle a_{3}-\langle A*B*c_{2}c_{3}\rangle c_{1}+\langle A*B*c_{1}c_{3}\rangle c_{2}-\langle A*B*c_{1}c_{2}\rangle c_{3}

Generically, this element represents the intersection of the 2-plane
\spn(v_{a_{1}},v_{a_{2}})
with the 3-plane represented by
a_{3}(B*C)
.

•

Similarly,

\displaystyle A*B*C\;

\displaystyle=\;\langle a_{1}a_{2}*B*C\rangle a_{3}-\langle a_{1}a_{3}*B*C\rangle a_{2}+\langle a_{2}a_{3}*B*C\rangle a_{1}

\displaystyle=\;\langle A*B*c_{1}c_{2}\rangle c_{3}-\langle A*B*c_{1}c_{3}\rangle c_{2}+\langle A*B*c_{2}c_{3}\rangle c_{1}

represents the line obtained by intersecting three 3-planes:
\spn(v_{a_{1}},v_{a_{2}},v_{a_{3}})
,
\spn(v_{b_{1}},v_{b_{2}},v_{b_{3}})
, and
\spn(v_{c_{1}},v_{c_{2}},v_{c_{3}})
, provided the vectors involved are sufficiently generic.

2.5.
The amplituhedron map

Motivated by the desire to give a geometric understanding of the
BCFW recurrence
for computing scattering amplitudes in
\mathcal{N}=4
super Yang Mills theory, and building on the Grassmannian formulation of
[
AHBC+16a
]
,
Arkani-Hamed and Trnka

[
AHT14
]

introduced
the
(tree) amplituhedron
, which they defined as
the image of the positive Grassmannian under a positive linear map. While the amplituhedron is not a central focus of this paper, the desire to understand it was a hidden motivation of this work. The amplituhedron map, defined below, and its degree on positroid varieties, is very closely related to our constructions.

Let
\Mat_{n,p}^{\circ}
denote the set of full-rank
n\times p
matrices, and
\Mat_{n,p}^{>0}\subset\Mat_{n,p}^{\circ}
be the subset of matrices whose maximal minors are positive.

Definition 2.24

(The amplituhedron map)
.

Let
Z\in\Mat_{n,k+m}^{\circ}
, where
k+m\leq n
.
The
amplituhedron map

\tilde{Z}:\Gr_{k,n}\dashedrightarrow\Gr_{k,k+m}

is the rational map defined by

\tilde{Z}(C):=CZ
,
where

C
is a
k\times n
matrix representing an element of

\Gr_{k,n}
, and
CZ
is a
k\times(k+m)
matrix representing an
element of
\Gr_{k,k+m}
.
The
exceptional locus
of
\tilde{Z}
, denoted by
E_{Z}
, is the subset of
\Gr_{k,n}
where
\tilde{Z}
is not defined.

If
Z\in\Mat_{n,k+m}^{>0}
,
\tilde{Z}
is well-defined when
restricted to the positive Grassmannian

\Gr_{k,n}^{\geq 0}
, and the
tree amplituhedron

\mathcal{A}_{n,k,m}(Z)\subset\Gr_{k,k+m}
is the image

\tilde{Z}(\Gr_{k,n}^{\geq 0})
.

It will be convenient for us compose the amplituhedron map with an embedding into
\Gr_{m,n}
, which also depends on
Z
.

Definition 2.25
.

Let
Z\in\Mat_{n,k+m}^{\circ}
. The
twistor map
is the map

\displaystyle\upsilon_{Z}:\Gr_{k,k+m}

\displaystyle\hookrightarrow\Gr_{m,n}

\displaystyle Y

\displaystyle\mapsto Y^{\perp}Z^{T}

where
Y^{\perp}\subset\mathbb{C}^{k+m}
denotes the subspace orthogonal to
Y
.

The twistor map
\upsilon_{Z}
is injective, and if we set
W=\colsp Z
, the image of
\upsilon_{Z}
is
\Gr_{m}(W):=\{X\in\Gr_{m,n}:X\subset W\}
, which is closed in
\Gr_{m,n}
. In terms of functions, the pullback
\upsilon_{Z}^{*}\langle I\rangle=\langle I\rangle_{\upsilon_{Z}(Y)}
of a Plücker coordinate is the
twistor coordinate

\det\begin{bmatrix}-Y-\\
-Z_{I}-\end{bmatrix}

where
Z_{I}
is the submatrix of
Z
using rows
I
.

Let
\Upsilon_{Z}:=\upsilon_{Z}\circ\tilde{Z}
. We use the notation
\mathcal{Y}_{G}:=\overline{\Upsilon_{Z}(\Pi_{G}\setminus E_{Z})}
for the (Zariski) closure of the image of
\Pi_{G}
under
\Upsilon_{Z}
. We define

(2)

\Upsilon_{Z,G}:=\Upsilon_{Z}|_{\Pi_{G}}:\Pi_{G}\dashrightarrow\mathcal{Y}_{G}

to be the restriction of
\Upsilon_{Z}
to
\Pi_{G}
.

Definition 2.26
.

Let
\Pi_{G}\subset\Gr_{k,n}
.
The
m
-intersection number
of
\Pi_{G}
, denoted
\operatorname{IN}_{m}(G)
, is defined as

\operatorname{IN}_{m}(G):=\begin{cases}0&\text{ if for generic }Z\in\Mat_{n,k+m}^{\circ},~\dim\mathcal{Y}_{G}<\dim\Pi_{G}\\
d&\text{ if for generic }Z\in\Mat_{n,k+m}^{\circ},~\Upsilon_{Z,G}\text{ is generically $d$-to-1.}\end{cases}

If
\Pi_{G}\subset\Gr_{k,n}
is dimension
km
and has
m
-intersection number 1, then
\mathcal{Y}_{G}=\Gr_{m}(W)
and we call
\Pi_{G}
an
algebraic pre-tile
, or just a
pre-tile
.

Remark 2.27
.

The amplituhedron literature often considers a semi-algebraic version of the objects above. For a positroid cell
S_{G}=\Pi_{G}\cap\Gr_{k,n}^{\geq 0}
, one can consider
Z_{G}:=\overline{\tilde{Z}(S_{G})}
, the closure of the image of the positroid cell. We call
Z_{G}
a
tile
if
\tilde{Z}
is injective on
S_{G}
and
\dim\Pi_{G}=km
. Note that
\Pi_{G}
being a pre-tile
does not
imply that
Z_{G}
is a tile, and
Z_{G}
being a tile
does not
imply that
\Pi_{G}
is a pre-tile. We record the following observations regarding tiles and pre-tiles:

•

For all known tiles
Z_{G}
,
\Pi_{G}
is a pre-tile.

•

For
m=1
: there are pre-tiles
\Pi_{G}
such that
Z_{G}
is not a tile. This behavior is expected to persist for odd
m
.

•

For
m=2
:
\Pi_{G}
is a pre-tile if and only if
Z_{G}
is a tile.

The following result of Lam gives a
Z
-independent characterization of
m
-intersection number and shows that it is always finite.

Proposition 2.28

(
[
Lam16a
, Proposition 4.8]
)
.

Suppose
\Pi_{G}\subset\Gr_{k,n}
has dimension
km
. Then
\operatorname{IN}_{m}(G)
is the coefficient of the Schur polynomial
s_{(n-k-m)^{k}}
in the cohomology class
[\Pi_{G}]\in H^{*}(\Gr_{k,n})
.

Equivalently, fix
X\in\Gr_{n-m,n}
and let
\Gr_{k}(X):=\{V\in\Gr_{k,n}:V\subset X\}.
Then in
H^{*}(\Gr_{k,n})
,

[\Gr_{k}(X)]\cap[\Pi_{G}]=\operatorname{IN}_{m}(G)\cdot[\operatorname{pt}].

Remark 2.29
.

Scattering amplitudes in planar
\mathcal{N}=4
super Yang-Mills (SYM) have fundamental building blocks called
Yangian invariants
, which are functions on
\Gr_{4,n}
of external momenta of scattering particles and encode the
leading singularities
of the theory. Each Yangian invariant
\mathcal{Y}_{G}
corresponds to a
4k
-dimensional positroid cell
S_{G}\subseteq\Gr^{\geq 0}_{k,n}

[
AHBC+16b
]
.
If
\mbox{IN}_{4}(G)=1
,
\mathcal{Y}_{G}
is a rational function whose poles correspond to facets of
\tilde{Z}(S_{G})
. If
\mbox{IN}_{4}(G)>1
,
\mathcal{Y}_{G}
can be expressed as a sum of
d
functions which have
algebraic
singularities.

3.
Vector-relation configurations

In this section we introduce
vector relation configurations
, essentially inspired by
[
AGPR24
]
.
We note that related frameworks had previously appeared in
[
AHBC+16b
]
,
[
Pos18
]
and
[
Lam16b
]
.

Throughout this section we will work with bipartite
plabic graphs as in
3.1
. For background, see
Appendix

A
, more specifically
Definition

A.1
and
Remark

A.3
.

Notation 3.1
.

Let
G=((B,W),E)

be a bipartite plabic graph, where

B
and
W
denote the set of black and white
vertices and
E
denotes the set of edges. We require
that all boundary vertices are black:
we write

B=B_{int}\sqcup B_{bd}
, where
B_{int}
is the
black interior vertices, and
B_{bd}=\{1,2,\dots,n\}

is the set of boundary vertices, labeled in increasing order clockwise. We use the notation
G^{op}
for the graph obtained from
G
by changing the color of every vertex (including the boundary vertices).
We will assume that all our plabic graphs are leafless, as in
Definition

A.1
. Unless otherwise mentioned,
G
is assumed to be reduced.

Definition 3.2
.

Let
G
be a bipartite plabic graph as in
3.1
.
Let
m\geq 1
be a positive integer. An
m
-vector-relation configuration
(
m
-VRC) on
G
is an assignment of vectors
v_{b}\in\mathbb{C}^{m}

to each black vertex of
G
and edge weights
r_{e}\in\mathbb{C}^{*}
to each edge of
G
, such that

(1)

the
boundary vectors

v_{1},\dots,v_{n}
on vertices
1,\dots n
span
\mathbb{C}^{m}
;

(2)

for each white vertex
w
, the following relation holds

\sum_{\{w,b\}=e\in E}r_{e}v_{b}=0.

We write
(\mathbf{v},\mathbf{R}):=(\{v_{b}\}_{b\in B},\{r_{e}\}_{e\in E})
for an
m
-VRC. The
boundary
of
(\mathbf{v},\mathbf{R})
is the
m\times n
matrix of boundary vectors

\partial(\mathbf{v},\mathbf{R}):=\begin{bmatrix}v_{1}\cdots v_{n}\end{bmatrix}.

An
m
-VRC is
non-degenerate
if all vectors on internal vertices are nonzero, and otherwise is
degenerate
.

Remark 3.3
.

We note that our conventions are somewhat different than those of
[
AGPR24
]
. We swap the roles of black and white relative to
[
AGPR24
]
; and in contrast to
[
AGPR24
]
, we allow our vectors
v_{b}
to be zero, and we require our edge weights to be nonzero.

We note that
GL_{m}(\mathbb{C})
acts on
(\mathbf{v},\mathbf{R})
by left multiplication on all vectors. We often consider
(\mathbf{v},\mathbf{R})
up to this action, in which case the boundary is a well-defined element of
\Gr_{m,n}
. We also have the following action of
(\mathbb{C}^{*})^{|W|+|B_{int}|}
on
m
-VRCs, via gauge transformations.

Definition 3.4
.

Let
(\mathbf{v},\mathbf{R})
be an
m
-VRC on a plabic graph
G
. For an internal vertex
x
of
G
and
t\in\mathbb{C}^{*}
,
gauge transformation by
t
at
x
changes
r_{e}\mapsto tr_{e}
for all edges
e
adjacent to
x
and, if
x=b
is black, additionally changes
v_{b}\mapsto(1/t)v_{b}
. We write

\mathcal{C}^{m}_{G}
for
m
-VRCs on
G
modulo gauge transformations and
GL_{m}
-action. If
(\mathbf{v},\mathbf{R})
is an
m
-VRC on
G
, we write
[\mathbf{v},\mathbf{R}]
for the corresponding element of
\mathcal{C}^{m}_{G}
and
\partial[\mathbf{v},\mathbf{R}]\in\Gr_{m,n}
for its boundary, which is well-defined. We write
\mathcal{C}^{\partial=\mathbf{z}}_{G}
for the subset of
\mathcal{C}^{m}_{G}
with boundary
\mathbf{z}\in\Gr_{m,n}
.

Note that gauge transformations commute with
GL_{m}
-action. It will sometimes also be useful to allow gauge transformations at the boundary vertices of
G
. With this enlarged gauge group,
\partial[\mathbf{v},\mathbf{R}]
is an element of
\Conf_{m,n}
.

Remark 3.5
.

One of our main results about vector relation configurations is

Corollary

6.12
, which gives a characterization of when a collection of generic boundary vectors can be uniquely extended to a vector relation configuration. This should be compared to
[
AGPR24
, Theorem 1.1]
.
Let
G
be a reduced plabic graph of type
(k,n)
. Then we have the following results:

•

(
Corollary

6.12
): A configuration of
n
generic boundary vectors in
\mathbb{C}^{m}
can be extended to a vector relation configuration on
G
that is unique up to gauge, if and only if the positroid variety
\Pi_{G}
has
m
-intersection number

1
and
\Pi_{G}
has dimension
km
.

•

(
[
AGPR24
, Theorem 1.1]
) A configuration of
n
boundary vectors in
\mathbb{C}^{k}
that represent a point of
\Pi_{G}
can generically be extended to a vector relation configuration on
G
that is unique up to gauge.

The next lemma gives a formula for the vectors in a VRC in terms of certain boundary vectors.
Recall from
Definition

A.4
that a
reverse perfect orientation
of
G
is an orientation of the edges such that every internal white vertex has a unique outgoing edge, and every internal black vertex has a unique incoming edge. Every reduced plabic graph has an acyclic reverse perfect orientation (cf.
Lemma

A.6
).

Lemma 3.6
.

Suppose
G
is a (leafless) plabic graph with an acyclic reverse perfect orientation
\mathcal{O}
.
Let
I
be the source set of
\mathcal{O}
. Let
(\mathbf{v},\mathbf{R})=(\{v_{b}\},\{r_{e}\})
be an
m
-VRC on
G
.

Then for any black vertex
b
,

(3)

v_{b}=\sum_{i\in I}\left(\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}P:i\to b\\
\text{ path in }\mathcal{O}\end{subarray}}\frac{\prod_{e}r_{e}}{\prod_{e^{\prime}}(-r_{e^{\prime}})}\right)v_{i}

where the product in the numerator is over the edges
e\in P
which are oriented black-to-white in
\mathcal{O}
and the product in the denominator is over edges
e^{\prime}\in P
oriented white-to-black.

Proof.

We induct on
q_{b}=\max_{i\in I}(\text{length of longest path }i\to b\text{ in }\mathcal{O})
. Since
\mathcal{O}
is acyclic and
G
is leafless, every internal vertex can be reached from a source: every internal vertex has degree at least two, so has some incoming edge. If we walk backwards along the incoming edges, we must eventually reach a source, as
\mathcal{O}
has no oriented cycles. So
q_{b}
is well-defined and nonnegative for all black vertices
b
.

If
q_{b}=0
, then
b=i
for some
i\in I
.
In that case, there are no paths
j\to b
unless
j=b
, so all terms on the right of (
3
) are zero except the one indexed by
b
. There is a single path
b\to b
which consists of no edges. So the right-hand side of (
3
) is
v_{i}
.

Now, assume
q_{b}>0
and that (
3
) holds for
b^{\prime}
with
q_{b^{\prime}}<q_{b}
. This means
b
is either internal or a sink of
\mathcal{O}
. There is a unique edge which is directed towards
b
, say it is
f=(w,b)
. Say
b_{1},\dots,b_{p}
are the other neighbors of
w
; the edges
e_{j}=(b_{j},w)
are all directed towards
w
. We have
v_{b}=-r_{f}^{-1}\sum_{j}r_{e_{j}}v_{b_{j}}
. Any path from
i\in I
to
b
must go through some
b_{j}
and any path
i\to b_{j}
can be extended to a path
i\to b
, so we have
q_{b_{j}}<q_{b}
. Using the inductive hypothesis, we have

v_{b}=\sum_{j}\frac{r_{e_{j}}}{-r_{f}}\sum_{i\in I}v_{i}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}P:i\to b_{j}\\
\text{ path in }\mathcal{O}\end{subarray}}\frac{\prod_{e}r_{e}}{\prod_{e^{\prime}}(-r_{e^{\prime}})}=-\sum_{i\in I}v_{i}\sum_{j}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}P:i\to b_{j}\\
\text{ path in }\mathcal{O}\end{subarray}}\frac{r_{e_{j}}}{-r_{f}}\frac{\prod_{e}r_{e}}{\prod_{e^{\prime}}(-r_{e^{\prime}})}=\sum_{i\in I}v_{i}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}P:i\to b\\
\text{ path in }\mathcal{O}\end{subarray}}\frac{\prod_{e}r_{e}}{\prod_{e^{\prime}}(-r_{e^{\prime}})}

where each product is over
e\in P
oriented black-to-white. This shows (
3
) holds for
v_{b}
.
∎

Corollary 3.7
.

Suppose
G
is a (leafless) plabic graph which admits an acyclic reverse perfect orientation. Then for any
m
-VRC
(\mathbf{v},\mathbf{R})
on
G
,
\mathbf{v}
is determined by the boundary
\partial(\mathbf{v},\mathbf{R})
and the coefficients
\mathbf{R}
.

Moves on plabic graphs induce maps on
\mathcal{C}^{m}_{G}
. See
[
AGPR24
, Section 2]
for proofs.

Lemma 3.8
.

Suppose
G^{\prime}
is obtained from
G
by a black (resp. white) expand move. Then the map shown in
Figure

1
, top (resp. bottom), induces a bijection
\mathcal{C}^{m}_{G}\to\mathcal{C}^{m}_{G^{\prime}}
.

Suppose
G,G^{\prime}
are related by a square move as in
Figure

2
. The map shown in
Figure

2
induces a bijection between
\{[\mathbf{v},\mathbf{R}]\in\mathcal{C}^{m}_{G}:ce-af\neq 0\}
and
\{[\mathbf{v},\mathbf{R}]\in\mathcal{C}^{m}_{G^{\prime}}:c^{\prime}e^{\prime}-a^{\prime}f^{\prime}\neq 0\}
.

Figure 1.
Top: reading left to right, a black-expand move and its effect on
m
-VRCs. Reading right to left, a black-contract move and its effect on
m
-VRCs. Bottom: white-expand and white-contract moves and their effect on
m
-VRCs.

Figure 2.
A square move and its effect on
m
-VRCs when
ce-af\neq 0
. Note that if
ce-af=0
, there is no relation among
u,y,z
(resp.
v,y,z
) unless
u=0
(resp.
v=0
).

4.
Plabic tangles and promotion maps

In this section we use
m
-VRCs on plabic graphs to give rational maps between Grassmannians. To record the image of the map, we need to first enhance plabic graphs slightly to
plabic tangles
. We note that
the definition of plabic tangle is inspired by the notion of
planar tangle

[
Jon22
]
, and we will later show that similarly to the case of planar tangles, there is an
operad structure
on plabic tangles, see
Section

9
.

4.1.
Tangles and maps on configuration spaces

Definition 4.1
.

Let
\ell
be a positive integer.
A
plabic tangle

(G,\mathbf{D})
is the data of a plabic graph

G=((B,W),E)
, drawn inside a disk called an
outer disk
(with boundary vertices
B_{bd}=\{1,\dots,n\}
), together with
\ell

inner disks
,
each of which lies in a
face of
G
.
Each inner disk has boundary vertices
D^{(i)}
(for
i\in[\ell]
), and each vertex
u\in D^{(i)}
is connected to one element
b_{u}
of
B
via a segment such that the resulting graph is planar.
Each inner and outer disk contains one
\star
-marked interval
, and the boundary vertices of each disk are labeled clockwise in increasing order starting just after the
\star
.
We use the notation
\mathbf{D}:=\{D^{(i)}\}_{i\in[\ell]}
for the boundary vertices of the inner disks. We often refer to
G
as the
core

and the
inner disks as
blobs
, see
Figure

3
.

Figure 3.
A plabic tangle with two blobs; the core is shown in black, while the blobs (inner disks) with their attaching segments are shown in red.

We first use VRCs on the core
G
to define maps on configuration spaces. We will restrict our attention to those
G
for which the boundary generically determines the VRC. Here and throughout, we use the Zariski topology on
\Gr_{m,n}
and
\Conf^{\circ}_{m,n}
.

Definition 4.2
.

A plabic graph
G
is
(
m
-generically) solvable
if for all
\mathbf{z}
in an open dense subset of
\Conf^{\circ}_{m,n}
, or in an open dense subset of
\Gr^{\circ}_{m,n}
,
there is a unique
m
-VRC
[\mathbf{v},\mathbf{R}]\in\mathcal{C}^{m}_{G}
with boundary
\mathbf{z}
. We denote this
m
-VRC by
[\mathbf{v},\mathbf{R}]^{\partial=\mathbf{z}}.
We say a plabic tangle is
(
m
-generically) solvable
if its core
G
is and each blob
D\in\mathbf{D}
has size at least
m
.

Remark 4.3
.

Proposition

6.11
shows that if
G
is reduced of type
(k,n)
, there is only one
m
for which
G
can be
m
-generically solvable. Namely,
m=(1/k)\dim\Pi_{G}=(\#(\text{faces of }G)-1)/k
. For this reason, we will drop “
m
-generically” from now on. Being reduced and solvable is a property of the move-equivalence class of
G
, as is implied by
Corollary

6.12
. If
G
and
G^{\prime}
are solvable and related by an expand-contract or square move, then the unique VRCs on
G,G^{\prime}
with boundary
\mathbf{z}
are related by the maps in
Lemma

3.8
, as is implied by
Corollary

6.13
.

We now use
[\mathbf{v},\mathbf{R}]^{\partial=\mathbf{z}}
to define a rational map on configuration spaces.

Definition 4.4
.

Let
(G,\mathbf{D})

be a solvable
plabic tangle.

We define a map

(4)

\psi:\Conf^{\circ}_{m,n}\dashrightarrow\prod_{D\in\mathbf{D}}\Conf_{m,D}

by first mapping generic
\mathbf{z}\in\Conf^{\circ}_{m,n}
to
[\mathbf{v},\mathbf{R}]^{\partial=\mathbf{z}}\in\mathcal{C}^{m}_{G}
, the unique
m
-VRC with boundary
\mathbf{z}
; since
[\mathbf{v},\mathbf{R}]^{\partial=\mathbf{z}}
is a gauge-equivalence class of VRCs,
this associates a line
\mathbb{C}v_{b}
to each
b\in B
. Recall that for
D\in\mathbf{D}
, each vertex
u

in
D
is adjacent to a unique element
b_{u}
of
B
. We associate line
\mathbb{C}v_{b_{u}}
to
u
; by reading the vertices of
D
in clockwise order starting just after the
\star
, we obtain the
blob’s boundary lines
, a tuple of
|D|
lines which we identify with an element of
\Conf_{m,D}
.

It will frequently be useful for us to make the assumption that the blob’s boundary lines usually give an element of
\Conf^{\circ}_{m,D}
. This is formalized in the following definition.

Definition 4.5
.

Let
(G,\mathbf{D})
be a plabic tangle. An
m
-VRC
[\mathbf{v},\mathbf{R}]\in\mathcal{C}^{m}_{G}
is
rank-
m
regular
for
(G,\mathbf{D})
if for each blob
D\in\mathbf{D}
,
the boundary lines
v_{b_{u}}
of the blob
span
\mathbb{C}^{m}
. If
(G,\mathbf{D})
is solvable, we say that it is
(rank-
m
) regular
if for generic
\mathbf{z}\in\Conf^{\circ}_{m,n}
,
[\mathbf{v},\mathbf{R}]^{\partial=\mathbf{z}}
is rank-
m
regular.
More generally,
(G,\mathbf{D})
is
rank-
m
regular
if in the set

\{(\mathbf{z},[\mathbf{v},\mathbf{R}])\ \colon\ \mathbf{z}\in\Conf^{\circ}_{m,n},~\partial[\mathbf{v},\mathbf{R}]=\mathbf{z},~[\mathbf{v},\mathbf{R}]~\text{is nondegenerate}\},

the subset of points where
[\mathbf{v},\mathbf{R}]
is also rank-
m
regular is dense.

4.2.
Pinnings, brushings, and maps on Grassmannians

We would like to upgrade the map on configuration spaces
given in
Definition

4.4
to a rational
map on Grassmannians instead. In order to do so, we need to choose a specific representative for
[\mathbf{v},\mathbf{R}]^{\partial=\mathbf{z}}
, in such a way that the coefficients
r_{e}
are rational functions of
\mathbf{z}
. We formalize this with the notion of a
pinning
.

Definition 4.6
.

Let
G
be a solvable plabic graph.
A collection of rational functions
\{r_{e}\}_{e\in E(G)}\subset\mathbb{C}(\Gr_{m,n})
is called a
pinning
of
G
if for generic
\mathbf{z}
,
[\mathbf{v},\mathbf{R}]^{\partial=\mathbf{z}}
has a representative of the form
(\{v_{b}\},\{r_{e}(\mathbf{z})\})
. Abusing terminology, we may call the representative
(\{v_{b}\},\{r_{e}(\mathbf{z})\})
a pinning, and may write
(\{v_{b}(\mathbf{z})\},\{r_{e}(\mathbf{z})\})
, since the vectors
v_{b}
are determined by
\mathbf{z}
and
r_{e}(\mathbf{z})
via (
3
).

We will show in
Proposition

6.15
that many pinnings exist; one may take
r_{e}(\mathbf{z})=f_{e}(\mathbf{z})
as provided there, and then act by gauge by any rational function of
\mathbf{z}
at any vertex.

Definition 4.7
.

A
pinned plabic tangle

(G,\mathbf{D})
is a solvable, rank-
m
regular plabic tangle
together with a pinning
(\{v_{b}(\mathbf{z})\},\{r_{e}(\mathbf{z})\})
of
G
. Using this pinning, we define the following maps:

•

A rational
geometric promotion
map

(5)

\psi:\Gr_{m,n}\dashrightarrow\prod_{D\in\mathbf{D}}\Gr_{m,D}

is defined as follows. Fix generic
\mathbf{z}\in\Gr_{m,n}
and choose a blob
D\in\mathbf{D}
.
Since each vertex
u

in
D
is adjacent to a unique element
b_{u}
of
B
, we associate vector
v_{b_{u}}(\mathbf{z})
to
u
, which we call the
boundary vector
of
u
. By reading the vertices of
D
in clockwise order starting just after the
\star
, we obtain the
blob’s boundary vectors
, which we assemble into a full-rank
m\times|D|
matrix
M
, and identify with an element of the Grassmannian
\Gr_{m,D}
.

•

(Algebraic) promotion
by the pinned plabic tangle is the pullback
\Psi:=\psi^{*}
of geometric promotion
\psi
; that is, it is the map

(6)

\Psi:\mathbb{C}(\widehat{\Gr}_{m,D^{(1)}})\otimes\dots\otimes\mathbb{C}(\widehat{\Gr}_{m,D^{(\ell)}})\to\mathbb{C}(\widehat{\Gr}_{m,n}),

which acts on Plücker coordinates by substituting
the
u
th column vector with the associated vector
v_{b_{u}}(\mathbf{z})

as above.

We note that by
Lemma

3.6
, the vectors
v_{b}(\mathbf{z})
can be expressed as linear combinations of the columns of
\mathbf{z}
with coefficients that are rational functions of
\mathbf{z}
, so
\Psi
is indeed well-defined.

One particularly nice way to obtain a pinned tangle is to
brush
the tangle using collections of vertex-disjoint paths.

Definition 4.8
.

A
brushed plabic tangle
is a plabic tangle
(G,\mathbf{D})
, together with a choice for each
D\in\mathbf{D}
of:

•

a reverse acyclic perfect orientation
\mathcal{O}^{D}
of
G
(see
Definition

A.4
)

•

a collection of oriented vertex-disjoint paths
\{P_{u}\}_{u\in D}
in
\mathcal{O}^{D}
such that
P_{u}
goes from some boundary vertex

i_{u}\in B_{bd}

to the vertex
b_{u}
adjacent to
u\in D
.

We write
\mathcal{B}:=\{\mathcal{O}^{D},\{P_{u}\}_{u\in D}\}_{D\in\mathbf{D}}
for the data of the brushing. We will also need to choose a sign
\sigma_{b_{u}}\in\{\pm 1\}
for each
b_{u}\in B_{int}
adjacent to a boundary vertex of a blob. We denote the brushing together with this choice of signs by
\mathcal{B}^{\boldsymbol{\sigma}}.

See
Figure

4
for an example of a brushing.

Figure 4.
A brushing for the tangle in
Figure

3
. On the left, the reverse acyclic perfect orientation and paths for
D^{(1)}
, and on the right, those for
D^{(2)}
. The paths are highlighted in blue. Note that some paths in the brushing are trivial and consist of a single boundary vertex.

A brushed plabic tangle has a distinguished pinning, which can be used to define promotion.

Definition 4.9
.

Suppose
(G,\mathbf{D})
is solvable plabic tangle and let
\mathcal{B}^{\boldsymbol{\sigma}}
be a brushing of
(G,\mathbf{D})
.
Let
(\{v^{\prime}_{b}(\mathbf{z})\},\{r^{\prime}_{e}(\mathbf{z})\})
be an arbitrary pinning. For
D\in\mathbf{D}
and
u\in D
, set

\wt^{\prime}(P_{u}):=\frac{\displaystyle\prod_{e\in P_{u}}r^{\prime}_{e}(\mathbf{z})}{\displaystyle\prod_{e^{\prime}\in P_{u}}(-r^{\prime}_{e^{\prime}}(\mathbf{z}))}

where the product in the numerator is over edges
e\in P_{u}
which are oriented black-to-white in
\mathcal{O}^{D}
, and the product in the denominator is over edges
e^{\prime}\in P_{u}
which are oriented white-to-black in
\mathcal{O}^{D}
. The
\mathcal{B}^{\boldsymbol{\sigma}}
-pinning
is the pinning
(\{v_{b}(\mathbf{z})\},\{r_{e}(\mathbf{z})\})
obtained by applying the gauge transformations

v^{\prime}_{b_{u}}(\mathbf{z})\mapsto v_{b_{u}}(\mathbf{z}):=\sigma_{b_{u}}\frac{v^{\prime}_{b_{u}}(\mathbf{z})}{\wt^{\prime}(P_{u})}

at all boundary vectors of all blobs. By
Lemma

4.10
below, the
\mathcal{B}^{\boldsymbol{\sigma}}
-pinning does not depend on the choice of pinning
(\{v^{\prime}_{b}(\mathbf{z})\},\{r^{\prime}_{e}(\mathbf{z})\})
.

If
(G,\mathbf{D},\mathcal{B}^{\boldsymbol{\sigma}})
is a brushed tangle which is solvable and rank-
m
regular, then
algebraic (resp. geometric) promotion by
(G,\mathbf{D},\mathcal{B}^{\boldsymbol{\sigma}})
is algebraic (resp. geometric) promotion by
(G,\mathbf{D})
using the
\mathcal{B}^{\boldsymbol{\sigma}}
-pinning. That is, algebraic promotion is the map which acts on Plücker coordinates of
\Gr_{m,D}
by substituting the
u
th column vector with
v_{b_{u}}(\mathbf{z})
.

See
Section

5.2
for the promotion maps corresponding to the brushed tangle in
Figure

4
, with all signs chosen to be
1
.

Lemma 4.10
.

Let
\mathcal{B}
be a brushing of a solvable plabic tangle
(G,\mathbf{D})
, and let
(\{v^{\prime}_{b}(\mathbf{z})\},\{r^{\prime}_{e}(\mathbf{z})\})
,
(\{v^{\prime\prime}_{b}(\mathbf{z})\},\{r^{\prime\prime}_{e}(\mathbf{z})\})
be two pinnings of
G
. For any
D\in\mathbf{D}
and boundary vertex
u\in D
, we have

\frac{v^{\prime}_{b_{u}}(\mathbf{z})}{\wt^{\prime}(P_{u})}=\frac{v^{\prime\prime}_{b_{u}}(\mathbf{z})}{\wt^{\prime\prime}(P_{u})}

where
\wt^{\prime}(P_{u}),\wt^{\prime\prime}(P_{u})
are as in
Definition

4.8
.

Proof.

As
G
is solvable, the two pinnings are related to each other by gauge transformations. Fix
u\in D
for some
D\in\mathbf{D}
. Note that
\wt^{\prime}(P_{u})
is invariant under gauge transformations at
x\neq b_{u}
. So we may assume that the pinnings differ only by a gauge transformation at
b_{u}
. Say
v^{\prime}_{b_{u}}(\mathbf{z})=t(\mathbf{z})\cdot v^{\prime\prime}_{b_{u}}(\mathbf{z})
, and for any edge
e
adjacent to
b_{u}
,
r^{\prime}_{e}(\mathbf{z})=r^{\prime\prime}_{e}(\mathbf{z})/t(\mathbf{z})
, where
t(\mathbf{z})
is some rational function of
\mathbf{z}
. Then we see that
\wt^{\prime}(P_{u})=t(\mathbf{z})\wt^{\prime\prime}(P_{u})
, and so the ratio
v^{\prime}_{b_{u}}(\mathbf{z})/\wt^{\prime}(P_{u})
is equal to
v^{\prime\prime}_{b_{u}}(\mathbf{z})/\wt^{\prime\prime}(P_{u})
.
∎

Remark 4.11
.

Brushed tangles whose cores are related by expand-contract or certain square moves give rise to the same promotion maps. See
Remarks

9.2
and
10
for more details.

Not every plabic tangle admits a brushing. In fact, as we will show, having a brushing is related to the promotion map being dominant.

Given a plabic tangle
(G,\mathbf{D})
, let
\pi^{D}:\prod_{D^{\prime}\in\mathbf{D}}\Conf_{m,D^{\prime}}\to\Conf_{m,D}
denote the projection map and let
\psi
be the map from (
4
) which assigns to each blob its collection of boundary lines.

Definition 4.12
.

A solvable plabic tangle
(G,\mathbf{D})
is
dominant
if it is rank-
m
regular and,
for each blob
D\in\mathbf{D}
, the image of the map
\pi^{D}\circ\psi

contains a Zariski dense subset of
\Conf^{\circ}_{m,D}
.

Theorem 4.13
.

A solvable plabic tangle
(G,\mathbf{D})
is dominant if and only if it admits a brushing.

The proof of this proposition is delayed until
Section

6.3
.

Note that we can compose tangles by inserting the outer disk of one into an inner disk of another, provided that the number of boundary vertices and the positions of the
\star
lines up. In
Section

9
we will show that with some care we can also compose brushed tangles. For now, we mention an application which allows us to produce many solvable graphs.

Corollary 4.14
.

Let
(G,\mathbf{D})
be a dominant plabic tangle, or equivalently a generically
m
-solvable plabic tangle which admits a brushing. For each
D\in\mathbf{D}
, choose a generically
m
-solvable plabic graph
G^{D}
with
|D|
boundary vertices. Let
\tilde{G}
denote the bipartite plabic graph obtained by identifying the boundary disk of each
G^{D}
with the corresponding inner disk
D
, then ignoring
D
and contracting the attaching segments. Then
\tilde{G}
is also generically
m
-solvable.

Proof.

Let
\phi
be the map from (
4
) and for
D\in\mathbf{D}
let
\pi^{D}
denote projection onto
\Conf_{m,D}
. Let
\mathcal{U}_{G}\subset\Conf^{\circ}_{m,n}
be an open dense subset such that for
\mathbf{z}\in\mathcal{U}_{G}
, there is a unique
[\mathbf{v},\mathbf{R}]\in\mathcal{C}^{m}_{G}
with boundary
\mathbf{z}
. Define
\mathcal{U}_{D}\subset\Conf^{\circ}_{m,D}
similarly, using
G^{D}
rather than
G
.

Since
(G,\mathbf{D})
is dominant, we have
\pi^{D}\circ\psi(\mathcal{U}_{G})
is dense in
\Conf^{\circ}_{m,D}
and so has nonempty intersection with
\mathcal{U}_{D}
, which is open and dense. Thus the preimage of
\mathcal{U}_{D}
under
\pi^{D}\circ\psi
is a nonempty open set
\mathcal{U}^{\prime}_{D}
in
\Conf^{\circ}_{m,N}
.

Let
\mathcal{U}:=\mathcal{U}_{G}\cap\bigcap_{D\in\mathbf{D}}\mathcal{U}^{\prime}_{D}
. For
\mathbf{z}\in\mathcal{U}
,
\mathbf{z}
uniquely determines the lines and relations for each vertex and edge of
G
. In particular, the boundary lines
\mathbb{C}v_{b_{u}}
of each blob
D
are uniquely determined. By construction, the boundary lines give an element of
\mathcal{U}_{D}
and so they in turn uniquely determine the lines and relations for each vertex and edge of
G^{D}
. This shows that for
\mathbf{z}\in\mathcal{U}
, there is a unique
[\mathbf{v},\mathbf{R}]\in\mathcal{C}^{m}_{\tilde{G}}
with boundary
\mathbf{z}
, as desired.
∎

Remark 4.15
.

Corollary

4.14
applied repeatedly to the BCFW core in
Figure

4
, together with its reflection and its cyclic shifts, shows that all
G
arising in the BCFW recurrence are generically
4
-solvable. That is,
\Pi_{G}
is an algebraic pre-tile for all
G
arising in the BCFW recurrence and
\tilde{Z}
is generically injective on
\Pi_{G}
. As mentioned in
Remark

2.27
, this does not immediately imply that
Z_{G}
is a tile, which was proved using different methods in
[
ELP+23
]
.

4.3.
Promotions from brushed tangles are conjecturally quasi-cluster

We make the following conjectures regarding promotions from brushed solvable plabic tangles. In what follows, let
\Sigma_{m,n}
denote a seed in the standard cluster structure on
\mathbb{C}[\widehat{\Gr}_{m,n}]
.

Conjecture 4.16
.

Let
(G,\mathbf{D})
be a dominant plabic tangle. Then there exists a brushing
\mathcal{B}
and a choice of signs
\boldsymbol{\sigma}
such that the following hold.

(1)

Geometric promotion by
(G,\mathbf{D},\mathcal{B}^{\boldsymbol{\sigma}})

sends totally positive elements to totally positive elements.

(2)

Algebraic promotion by
(G,\mathbf{D},\mathcal{B}^{\boldsymbol{\sigma}})

\Psi:\mathbb{C}(\widehat{\Gr}_{m,D^{(1)}})\otimes\dots\otimes\mathbb{C}(\widehat{\Gr}_{m,D^{(\ell)}})\to\mathbb{C}(\widehat{\Gr}_{m,n})

restricts to a quasi-cluster homomorphism from

\mathcal{A}(\Sigma_{m,D^{(1)}}\sqcup\cdots\sqcup\Sigma_{m,D^{(\ell)}})\to\mathcal{A}(\overline{\Sigma})

where
\overline{\Sigma}
is obtained from a seed
\Sigma_{m,n}
for
\widehat{\Gr}_{m,n}
by freezing some variables.

Remark 4.17
.

Let
(G,\mathbf{D})
be a dominant plabic tangle where
G
is a plabic tree (cf.
Section

7
). Then we expect that
4.16
holds for
all
brushings
\mathcal{B}
; that is, for all brushings, there is a choice of signs so that (1) and (2) of
4.16
hold.

5.
Examples of promotions from tangles

In this section, we illustrate how to
obtain promotion maps from various solvable plabic tangles, for cores with
k=1,2,3
.
We will show in
Section

8
that all of these maps
are quasi-cluster homomorphisms.
For these tangles,
Definitions

7.16
and
7.19
gives formulas for the vectors and coefficients in
[\mathbf{v},\mathbf{R}]^{\partial=\mathbf{z}}
; we use these without justification. We also choose the signs
\boldsymbol{\sigma}
to be 1 on all vertices.

5.1.
Star promotion (
m\geq 3
,
k=1
)

In this section we give a first example of how to use a vector relation configuration to get a map on Grassmannians which is a quasi-cluster homomorphism. In particular, we introduce the notion of
(unary) star promotion
, which uses the framework of
Definition

4.1
and
Definition

4.7
, to define
a homomorphism from

\mathbb{C}(\widehat{\Gr}_{m,n-1})

to
\mathbb{C}(\widehat{\Gr}_{m,n})
. We use a plabic tangle with a single inner disk (hence the word “unary”),
whose core graph is a tree on
m+1
leaves with only white vertices, see
Figure

5
. This core is move-equivalent to
a single white vertex with
m+1
legs emanating from it, that is, a “star graph.”

1
2
3
4
5
n
\star
\star
1
3
4
1
2
3
4
5
6
7
n
\star
\star
1
3
4
5
6

Figure 5.
Brushed tangles for unary star promotion for
m=3
and
m=5

. The labels of the inner disk vertices indicate the brushing: the paths go from the boundary vertex
i\in B_{bd}
to the (black vertex attached to the) inner disk vertex labeled
i
. These paths extend to a reverse perfect orientation.

We will prove in
Section

8
that unary star promotion is a
quasi-cluster homomorphism. Therefore if we apply unary star promotion to
a cluster variable (resp. subset of a cluster),
we obtain a cluster variable (resp. subset of a cluster) of

\Gr_{m,n}
, up to a Laurent monomial in
a certain set of distinguished Plücker coordinates.

In accordance with the brushed plabic tangles in
Figure

5
, we let
N^{\prime}=\{1,3,\dots,n\}
. We first apply
Definition

4.7

to the diagram at the left of
Figure

5
(with all signs equal to
1
), and obtain the
m=3

unary star promotion map
described in
Definition

5.1
.

Definition 5.1

(Unary star promotion for
m=3
)
.

Unary star promotion
(for
m=3
) is the homomorphism

\Psi_{3}:\mathbb{C}(\widehat{\Gr}_{3,N^{\prime}})\;\to\;\mathbb{C}(\widehat{\Gr}_{3,n})

induced
by the following substitution on vectors
1,3,4,\dots,n
in
\mathbb{C}^{3}
:

\displaystyle 3

\displaystyle\;\mapsto\;\frac{12*34}{\langle 124\rangle}\;=\;3-\frac{\langle 123\rangle}{\langle 124\rangle}\,4

\displaystyle j

\displaystyle\;\mapsto\;j\hskip 170.71652pt\text{ for }j=1,4,5,\dots,n.

Remark 5.2
.

We can give an equivalent definition of
\Psi_{3}
using Plücker coordinates.
Recall the chain polynomial notation from
Definition

2.20
. We have that

\displaystyle\Psi_{3}\left(\langle 3ab\rangle\right)

\displaystyle\;=\;\langle 3ab\rangle-\frac{\langle 123\rangle}{\langle 124\rangle}\langle 4ab\rangle\;=\;\frac{\langle 1\,2*3\,4*a\,b\rangle}{\langle 124\rangle}

\displaystyle\Psi_{3}\left(\langle abc\rangle\right)

\displaystyle\;=\;\langle a,b,c\rangle\hskip 199.16928pt\text{ if }3\notin\{abc\}.

We now give the generalization of
Definition

5.1
for general
m\geq 3.
This comes from star plabic tangles as in

Figure

5
, where the additional example
m=5
is shown on the right.

Definition 5.3

(Unary star promotion)
.

Fix a positive integer
m\geq 3
.

Unary star promotion
is the homomorphism

\Psi_{m}:\mathbb{C}(\widehat{\Gr}_{m,N^{\prime}})\;\to\;\mathbb{C}(\widehat{\Gr}_{m,n})

induced by the following substitution on vectors
1,3,4,\dots,n
in
\mathbb{C}^{m}
:

\displaystyle j

\displaystyle\;\mapsto\;\frac{(1\wedge 2\wedge\dots\wedge j{-}1)*(j\wedge j{+}1\wedge\dots\wedge m\wedge m{+}1))}{\langle 1,2,\dots,j{-}1,j{+}1,\dots,m,m{+}1\rangle}

\displaystyle\text{ for }3\leq j\leq m

\displaystyle j

\displaystyle\;\mapsto\;j

\displaystyle\text{ for }j=1\text{ and }m+1\leq j\leq n

5.2.
BCFW Promotion
(m=4,k=1)

This section discusses the promotion map coming from the brushed tangle in
Figure

4
. This promotion could be called
binary star promotion
.

Definition 5.4
.

(BCFW Promotion)

Let
N_{L}=\{1,2,\ldots,a,b,n\}
and
N_{R}=\{b,b+1,\ldots,c,d,n\}
,
where
a,b
and
c,d,n
are consecutive.
Let
F_{e}
denote the Plücker coordinate obtained by formally erasing the label
e
from
\langle abcdn\rangle
, e.g.

F_{b}=\langle acdn\rangle
and
F_{n}=\langle abcd\rangle
.

BCFW promotion
is the homomorphism

\Psi_{BCFW}:\mathbb{C}(\Gr_{4,N_{L}})\times\mathbb{C}(\Gr_{4,N_{R}})\;\rightarrow\;\mathbb{C}(\Gr_{4,n})

induced by the substitutions:

\displaystyle\mbox{on }\Gr_{4,N_{L}}:

\displaystyle\;\;\;\;\;\;b\mapsto\frac{ab*cdn}{F_{b}}

\displaystyle\mbox{on }\Gr_{4,N_{R}}:

\displaystyle\;\;\;\;\;\;d\mapsto\frac{cd*abn}{F_{d}}\;\;\;\;\;\;n\mapsto\frac{cdn*ab}{F_{n}}\;\;\;\;\;\;

and
j\mapsto j
otherwise.

The authors together with T. Lakrec proved that
\Psi_{BCFW}
is a quasi-cluster homomorphism in

[
ELP+23
, Theorem 4.7]
.

5.3.
Spurion promotion
(m=4,k=2)

The next example applies
Definition

4.7
to obtain the promotion map
\Psi_{sp}
by the plabic tangle
G_{sp}
in
Figure

6
. In this case, the core is the
spurion
5
5

5

See
[
EZLP+24
]
for etymology and the appearance of this graph in tilings of the amplituhedron.
, with
k=2
, and it is attached with
5
legs to one blob. The resulting promotion map is not obtainable by composing two star promotions.

Figure 6.
A plabic tangle
(G_{sp},\{1,2,7,8,9,\dots,n\})
for unary spurion promotion. The labels of the inner disk indicate the brushing: the paths go from the respective boundary vertices
1,2,7,8,9
to the (black vertex attached to the) blob vertex with the same label. These paths can be completed to a reverse perfect orientation.

Definition 5.5
.

(Unary spurion promotion)

Let
N^{\prime}=\{1,2,7,8,\ldots,n\}
.
Let
F_{i}
denote the expression obtained by formally erasing the label
i
from the expression
\langle 123*456*789\rangle
, e.g.

F_{2}=\langle 13*456*789\rangle
.

Unary spurion promotion
is the homomorphism

\Psi_{sp}:\mathbb{C}(\Gr_{4,N^{\prime}})\;\rightarrow\;\mathbb{C}(\Gr_{4,n})

induced by the substitutions:

2\mapsto\frac{12*3(456*789)}{F_{2}}\;\;\;\;\;\;7\mapsto\frac{123*456*789}{F_{7}}\;\;\;\;\;\;8\mapsto\frac{(123*456)7*89}{F_{8}}

and
j\mapsto j
otherwise.

Remark 5.6
.

By the identities in
Example

2.23
, the substitutions in upper spurion promotion can be written as follows:

\displaystyle 2\;\;\mapsto\;\;

\displaystyle 2-\frac{F_{1}}{F_{2}}1\;\;=\;\;\frac{F_{3}\cdot 3-F_{7}\cdot 7+F_{8}\cdot 8-F_{9}\cdot 9}{F_{2}}

\displaystyle 7\;\;\mapsto\;\;

\displaystyle 7-\frac{F_{8}}{F_{7}}8+\frac{F_{9}}{F_{7}}9\;\;=\;\;\frac{F_{1}\cdot 1-F_{2}\cdot 2+F_{3}\cdot 3}{F_{7}}

\displaystyle 8\;\;\mapsto\;\;

\displaystyle 8-\frac{F_{9}}{F_{8}}9\;\;=\;\;\frac{F_{7}\cdot 7-F_{1}\cdot 1+F_{2}\cdot 2-F_{3}\cdot 3}{F_{9}}

We note that all the chain polynomials appearing in this formula have sets of size
3=m-1
. Using
Corollary

8.3
with appropriate cyclic shifts, these polynomials are cluster variables and so are irreducible in
\mathbb{C}[\widehat{\Gr}_{4,n}]
by
[
GLS13
, Theorem 1.3]
.

5.4.
Chain-tree promotion (
m=4,k=3
)

An interesting class of examples are promotions induced by plabic tangles whose cores are
chain trees
. These trees let us generalize the promotions by the
k=1
star and the
k=2
spurion to higher values of
k
.

Definition 5.7
.

(Chain tree) A
(t_{1},t_{2},\dots,t_{2k-1})
-
chain tree
is a plabic graph of type
(k,\sum_{i}t_{i})
, which is a tree of the following form:
\dots
\dots
\dots
\dots
\dots
\dots
\dots
t_{1}
t_{2}
t_{3}
t_{4}
t_{2k-2}
t_{2k-1}

For example, the
(3,3,3)
-chain tree for
k=2
is the spurion discussed above. Other chain-trees of interest are the
(3,3,1,3,3)
-chain tree for
k=3
, the
(3,3,1,3,1,3,3)
-chain tree for
k=4
, and in general the
(3,3,1,3,1,\dots,3,1,3,3)
-chain tree for every
k
. This sequence of plabic graphs are
(k,4)
-amplitrees, cf
Definition

7.2
, and can be used to define new promotion maps for
m=4
.

The following example uses the
(3,3,1,3,3)
-chain tree and one blob to define the
k=3
chain-tree promotion map
\Psi_{ch}
, which is not obtainable by composing the previous promotions of
k=1
and
2
.

The plabic tangle for the

, the labels

in the blob indicate the brushing.

Definition 5.8
.

(Chain-tree Promotion)

Let
N^{\prime}=\{1,2,3,\mathrm{B},\mathrm{C},\mathrm{D},\ldots,n\}
where we extend the numerals by
\mathrm{A},\mathrm{B},\mathrm{C},\mathrm{D}
to abbreviate
10,11,12,13
. Let
F_{i}
denote the function obtained by “formally erasing the label
i
” from the expression
\langle(123*456)7(89\mathrm{A}*\mathrm{B}\mathrm{C}\mathrm{D})\rangle
, e.g.

F_{2}=\langle(13*456)7(89\mathrm{A}*\mathrm{B}\mathrm{C}\mathrm{D})\rangle,\;\;F_{7}=\langle 123*456*89\mathrm{A}*\mathrm{B}\mathrm{C}\mathrm{D}\rangle,\;\;F_{8}=\langle(123*456)7(9\mathrm{A}*\mathrm{B}\mathrm{C}\mathrm{D})\rangle.

Chain-tree promotion
is the homomorphism

\Psi_{ch}:\mathbb{C}(\Gr_{4,N^{\prime}})\;\rightarrow\;\mathbb{C}(\Gr_{4,n})

induced by the substitutions:

\displaystyle 2\;\mapsto\;\frac{12*(3(456*(7(89\mathrm{A}*\mathrm{B}\mathrm{C}\mathrm{D}))))}{F_{2}}

\displaystyle 3\;\mapsto\;\frac{123*456*(7(89\mathrm{A}*\mathrm{B}\mathrm{C}\mathrm{D}))}{F_{3}}

\displaystyle B\;\mapsto\;\frac{\mathrm{B}\mathrm{C}\mathrm{D}*89\mathrm{A}*(7(123*456))}{F_{B}}

\displaystyle C\;\mapsto\;\frac{\mathrm{C}\mathrm{D}*(\mathrm{B}(89\mathrm{A}*(7(456*123))}{F_{C}}

and
j\mapsto j
otherwise.

An interesting class of examples are promotions where the core has several connected components.

Definition 5.9

Choose

Forest promotion
is the homomorphism

\Psi_{fo}:\mathbb{C}(\Gr_{3,N^{\prime}})\;\rightarrow\;\mathbb{C}(\Gr_{3,n})

induced by the substitutions:

2\mapsto\frac{12*34}{\langle 134\rangle}\;\;\;\;\;\;b\mapsto\frac{ab*cd}{\langle acd\rangle}

and
j\mapsto j
otherwise.

6.
Vector-relation configurations and intersection number

In this section, we relate
m
-VRCs on
G
and the
m
-intersection number of the positroid variety
\Pi_{G}\subset\Gr_{k,n}
when
\dim\Pi_{G}=km
.
In
Corollary

6.10
, we show
the number of
m
-VRC’s with fixed generic boundary vectors is equal to the intersection number of
\Pi_{G}
. In particular,
G
is
m
-generically solvable if and only if

\Pi_{G}
has
m
-intersection number
1
and
\Pi_{G}
has dimension
km
(
Corollary

6.12
).
Throughout this section, we fix
k,m,n
such that
k+m\leq n
. The words “open”, “closed”, and “dense” all refer to the Zariski topology.

6.1.
Rephrasing intersection number

In this section we will translate the notion of
inter- section number (see

Definition

2.26
) in terms of
generic
Z\in\Mat_{n,k+m}^{\circ}
, to a condition about
generic
z\in\Gr_{m,n}
, see

Proposition

6.3
. In
Proposition

6.5
we will also give a combinatorial criterion on the plabic graph
G

for
verifying that a positroid variety
\Pi_{G}
has
intersection number
0
.

Recall the definition of
G^{op}
from
3.1
. Recall from
Lemma

2.4
that
V\mapsto V^{\perp}
is an involutive isomorphism from
\Pi_{H}
to
\Pi_{H^{op}}
.

Lemma 6.1
.

Fix
k,m
. Suppose
H
is a plabic graph of type
(k,n)
and set

Q_{H}:=\{\mathbf{z}\in\Gr_{m,n}:\mathbf{z}\subset V\text{ for some }V\in\Pi_{H^{op}}\}=\{\mathbf{z}\in\Gr_{m,n}:\mathbf{z}^{\perp}\supset C\text{ for some }C\in\Pi_{H}\}.

If
\dim\Pi_{H}<km
, then
Q_{H}
is an irreducible subvariety of
\Gr_{m,n}
of positive codimension.

Proof.

Consider the incidence variety

\{(\mathbf{z},V)\in\Gr_{m,n}\times\Pi_{H^{op}}:\mathbf{z}\subset V\}\subset\Gr_{m,n}\times\Gr_{n-k,n}.

This is a projective variety (cut out by the equations for
\Pi_{H^{op}}
and the incidence-Plücker relations) and is irreducible as
\Pi_{H^{op}}
is irreducible. The projection onto the first coordinate, which is a proper map, gives
Q_{H}
. This implies
Q_{H}
is closed and irreducible.

Now, we turn to dimension. There are
\dim\Pi_{H^{op}}=\dim\Pi_{H}
degrees of freedom to choose
V\in\Pi_{H^{op}}\subset\Gr_{n-k,n}
. Once
V
is chosen,
\mathbf{z}
can be any element of
\Gr_{m}(V)\cong\Gr_{m,n-k}
, which has dimension
m(n-k-m)
. So the incidence variety has dimension

\dim\Pi_{H}+m(n-k-m)<km+m(n-k-m)=m(n-m)=\dim\Gr_{m,n}.

Since
Q_{H}
is a projection of the incidence variety, its dimension is at most the dimension of the incidence variety, hence it has positive codimension in
\Gr_{m,n}
.

∎

Remark 6.2
.

Lemma

6.1
holds by the same proof if we replace
\Pi_{H^{op}}
with an irreducible subvariety
X\subset Gr_{n-k,n}
satisfying
\dim X<km
(and replace
\Pi_{H}
with the image of
X
under
V\mapsto V^{\perp}
).

In the next proposition, we use
T_{G}
to denote the image of the boundary measurement map (see
Definition

A.7
), which is an algebraic torus by
[
MS17
, Theorem 7.1]
.
The torus
T_{G}
is a Zariski-open, dense subset of
\Pi_{G}
.

Proposition 6.3
.

Suppose that
G
is a plabic graph of type
(k,n)
, and
\dim\Pi_{G}=km
. The following are equivalent:

(1)

\Pi_{G}
has
m
-intersection number
d
;

(2)

for an open dense set of
\mathbf{z}\in\Gr_{m,n}
, the set
\{C\in\Pi_{G}:C\subset\mathbf{z}^{\perp}\}
has cardinality
d
;

(3)

for an open dense set of
\mathbf{z}\in\Gr_{m,n}
, the set
\{C\in T_{G}:C\subset\mathbf{z}^{\perp}\}
has cardinality
d
.

Proof.

For
z\in\Gr_{m,n}
, let
P_{z}=\Gr_{k}(z^{\perp})
be the collection of
k
-planes
V\in\Gr_{k,n}
which are contained in
z^{\perp}
. This is a Schubert variety of dimension
k(n-m-k)
which is isomorphic to
\Gr_{k,n-m}
. Notice that
P_{z}\cap\Pi_{G}
and
P_{z}\cap T_{G}
are the sets mentioned in (2) and (3), respectively.

(1\Leftrightarrow 2)
:
By
Proposition

2.28
, in the cohomology ring
H^{*}(\Gr_{k,n})
, we have
[\Pi_{G}]\cap[P_{z}]=\operatorname{IN}_{m}(G)\cdot[\operatorname{pt}].
This means that
\Pi_{G}\cap P_{z}
will consist of exactly
\operatorname{IN}_{m}(G)
points, provided that the intersection is transverse. We now show that this intersection is transverse for a generic set of
z
. Since
GL_{n}
acts transitively on
\Gr_{m,n}
,
and
(P_{z})\cdot g
is equal to
P_{zg}
, by Kleiman’s theorem there’s an open dense subset of
g\in GL_{n}
such that

\Pi_{G}
and
P_{zg}
intersect transversely. Therefore for generic
z\in\Gr_{m,n}
,

\operatorname{IN}_{m}(G)=|P_{z}\cap\Pi_{G}|,
and hence
\operatorname{IN}_{m}(G)=|\{C\in\Pi_{G}:C\subset\mathbf{z}^{\perp}\}|
.

(2\Leftrightarrow 3)
: Consider
\Pi_{G}\setminus T_{G}
, which is a closed subvariety of
\Pi_{G}
of dimension less than
km
.
Lemmas

6.1
and
6.2
imply that
\{\mathbf{z}\in\Gr_{m,n}:\mathbf{z}^{\perp}\supset C\text{ for some }C\in\Pi_{G}\setminus T_{G}\}
is contained in a positive codimension subvariety
\mathcal{V}
of
\Gr_{m,n}
. For
\mathbf{z}
in the open dense set
\Gr_{m,n}\setminus\mathcal{V}
, any
C\in\Pi_{G}
contained in
\mathbf{z}^{\perp}
lies in
T_{G}
, and so

|P_{\mathbf{z}}\cap\Pi_{G}|=|P_{\mathbf{z}}\cap T_{G}|.

Therefore for
\mathbf{z}
in an open dense set of
\Gr_{m,n}
, the two sets
\{C\in\Pi_{G}:C\subset\mathbf{z}^{\perp}\}
and
\{C\in T_{G}:C\subset\mathbf{z}^{\perp}\}
have the same cardinality.
∎

Proposition

6.5
below gives a useful criterion for
verifying that a positroid variety
\Pi_{G}
has
intersection number
0
. We will use this in the next section in the proof of
Theorem

7.23
.

Lemma 6.4
.

Fix
\Pi_{G}
of dimension
km.
Suppose there is a variety
X\subset\Gr_{k^{\prime},n}
of dimension less than
k^{\prime}m
and for every point
V
in an open dense subset
\mathcal{U}
of
\Pi_{G}
, there exists
V^{\prime}\in X
such that
V^{\prime}\subset V
. Then
\operatorname{IN}_{m}(G)=0
.

Proof.

Assume for the sake of contradiction that
\Pi_{G}

has intersection number
d>0
. Repeating the proof of
Proposition

6.3
using
\mathcal{U}
rather than
T_{G}
, we obtain that there is an open dense set

S\subset\Gr_{m,n}
such that for

\mathbf{z}\in S
,

\{V\in\mathcal{U}:V\subset\mathbf{z}^{\perp}\}

consists of
d
points and in particular is nonempty. By construction of
\mathcal{U}
, we also have that all
V\in\mathcal{U}
contain an element of
X
. This means that

Y=\{\mathbf{z}\in\Gr_{m,n}:\text{for some }V^{\prime}\in X,~V^{\prime}\subset\mathbf{z}^{\perp}\}

contains
S
and so in particular is full-dimensional. But this contradicts
Lemma

6.1
, which states that
Y
is positive codimension.
∎

In the next proposition, we let
S(\mathcal{O})
denote the sources of a perfect orientation.

Proposition 6.5
.

Fix a positive integer
m
, and let

G
be a plabic graph of type
(k,n)
such that

\dim\Pi_{G}=km
. Suppose there is an acyclic perfect orientation
\mathcal{O}
of
G
and a subgraph
G^{\prime}
of
G
obtained by deleting some vertices and edges
\tilde{E}
of
G
such that:

(1)

\mathcal{O}
restricts to a perfect orientation
\mathcal{O}^{\prime}
of
G^{\prime}
;

(2)

the sources and sinks of
\mathcal{O}^{\prime}
are a subset of
B_{bd}
, and
S(\mathcal{O}^{\prime})=S(\mathcal{O})\cap\{a,a+1,\dots,b\}
for some
a<b
;

(3)

all vertices of
G^{\prime}
incident to edges of
\tilde{E}
are black;

(4)

G^{\prime}
has type
(k^{\prime},n^{\prime})
with
1\leq k^{\prime}<k
, and
\dim\Pi_{G^{\prime}}<k^{\prime}m
.

Then
\Pi_{G}
has
m
-intersection number 0.

Proof.

We would like to use
Lemma

6.4
on
\Pi_{G}
. Note that
\Pi_{G^{\prime}}
can be embedded into
\Gr_{k^{\prime},n}
by adding zero columns in positions
B_{bd}\setminus S
. We let
X
denote the image of
\Pi_{G^{\prime}}
under this embedding.
Item

4
implies that
\dim X<k^{\prime}m
.

We now show that every element of
T_{G}
contains an element of
X
. Let
B_{bd}^{\prime}
denote the sources and sinks of
