\mathcal{O}^{\prime}
, which are the boundary vertices of
G^{\prime}
.

Items

1
and
3
imply that if
e\in\tilde{E}
is incident to
v\in G^{\prime}
, then
e
is oriented towards
G^{\prime}
. This means that if
s
is a source of
\mathcal{O}^{\prime}
, then there are no paths in
\mathcal{O}
from
s
to
B_{bd}\setminus B_{bd}^{\prime}
. So in the path matrix
A(G,\mathcal{O})
, the row indexed by
s
has zeros in columns indexed by
B_{bd}\setminus B_{bd}^{\prime}
. Moreover, this together with
Item

2
implies the entry in row
s
and column
t\in B_{bd}^{\prime}
is the same as the entry of
A(G^{\prime},\mathcal{O})
in row
s
and column
t
, up to a sign
\sigma_{s,t}
. Because
S(\mathcal{O}^{\prime})
indexes a consecutive subset of the rows of
A(G,\mathcal{O})
,
\sigma_{s,t}
depends only on the column
t
.

This means that
A(G,\mathcal{O})
has a submatrix which, up to resigning columns, has the same span as
A(G^{\prime},\mathcal{O})
. As
\Pi_{G^{\prime}}
, and thus
X
, is preserved by resigning columns, this implies each element
V\in T_{G}
contains some element
V^{\prime}\in X
.

We have verified the assumptions of
Lemma

6.4
, and conclude that
\operatorname{IN}_{m}(G)=0
.
∎

Based on computations for
m\leq 8
, we conjecture that the sufficient condition for
\operatorname{IN}_{m}(G)=0
in
Proposition

6.5
is also necessary.

Conjecture 6.6
.

Suppose
\Pi_{G}\subset\Gr_{k,n}
has dimension
km
. The following are equivalent:

(1)

\Pi_{G}
has
m
-intersection number 0;

(2)

there is a plabic graph
G^{\prime}
of type
(k^{\prime},n^{\prime})
with
1\leq k^{\prime}<k
and
\dim\Pi_{G^{\prime}}<k^{\prime}m
such that every
V\in T_{G}
contains some
V^{\prime}\in\Pi_{G^{\prime}}
;

(3)

there is a graph move-equivalent to
G
with a subgraph
G^{\prime}
and perfect orientation
\mathcal{O}
satisfying the assumptions of
Proposition

6.5
.

6.2.
Relating VRCs to the intersection number

In this section we start by recalling results of
[
AGPR24
]
which relate the boundary measurement torus
T_{G}
to non-degenerate VRCs. We then show
in
Theorem

6.8
that the set of
m
-VRCs on
G
with boundary

\mathbf{z}
is in bijection with the set
\{C\in T_{G}:C\subset\mathbf{z}^{\perp}\}
, and
we show in
Corollary

6.10
that
the number of
m
-VRC’s with boundary
\mathbf{z}
is equal to the
intersection number of
\Pi_{G}
.

Recall from
3.1
that
G^{op}
is the graph obtained from
G
by switching the colors of every vertex. Recall from
Definition

3.2
that
\partial[\mathbf{v},\mathbf{R}]
denotes the boundary of the VRC
[\mathbf{v},\mathbf{R}]
.
Also recall the boundary measurement map

\mathbb{B}_{G}
from
Definition

A.7
.

Theorem 6.7

(
[
AGPR24
, Proposition 7.3, Theorem 7.8]
)
.

Let
G
be a reduced
(k,n)
-plabic graph. Choose any Kasteleyn signs
(\sigma_{e})_{e\in E(G)}
on
G
(see
[
AGPR24
, Section 7.1]
). Then we have isomorphisms
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\{[\mathbf{v},\mathbf{R}]\in\mathcal{C}^{n-k}_{G}\text{ non-degenerate}\}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces T_{G^{op}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(\mathbb{C}^{*})^{E(G)}/\text{gauge}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{[\{v_{b}\},\{r_{e}\}]}=[\mathbf{v},\mathbf{R}]}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\partial[\mathbf{v},\mathbf{R}]}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(\sigma_{e}r_{e})_{e\in E(G)}.}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\sim}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\partial}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\sim}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathbb{B}_{G^{op}}}

Theorem

6.7
implies that for any choice of
\mathbf{R}
(modulo gauge), there is a unique non-degenerate
[\mathbf{v},\mathbf{R}]\in\mathcal{C}^{n-k}_{G}
and its boundary is in
T_{G^{op}}
.

In the next result, we use the notation
\mathcal{C}^{\partial=\mathbf{z}}_{G}:=\{[\mathbf{v},\mathbf{R}]\in\mathcal{C}^{m}_{G}:\partial[\mathbf{v},\mathbf{R}]=\mathbf{z}\}
, and the notation
V^{\perp}
for the orthogonal complement of the vector space
V
.

Theorem 6.8
.

Let
G
be a reduced plabic graph of type
(k,n)
.

Fix
m<n-k
and
\mathbf{z}\in\Gr_{m,n}^{\circ}
.
Then there are well-defined
maps
\alpha
and
\perp
which give bijections
in the following diagram:
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{C}^{\partial=\mathbf{z}}_{G}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\{V\in T_{G^{op}}:\mathbf{z}\subset V\}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\{C\in T_{G}:C\subset\mathbf{z}^{\perp}\}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{[\mathbf{v},\mathbf{R}]}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\partial[\mathbf{v}^{\prime},\mathbf{R}]}=V}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces V^{\perp}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\alpha}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\sim}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Omega}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\sim}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\perp}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\sim}

In particular,
\alpha
sends
[\mathbf{v},\mathbf{R}]
to the boundary of the unique non-degenerate
[\mathbf{v}^{\prime},\mathbf{R}^{\prime}]\in\mathcal{C}^{n-k}_{G}
with
\mathbf{R}^{\prime}=\mathbf{R}
.

Proof.

We focus first on the map

\alpha:\mathcal{C}^{\partial=\mathbf{z}}_{G}\to\{V\in T_{G^{op}}:\mathbf{z}\subset V\}.

Let
[\mathbf{v},\mathbf{R}]\in\mathcal{C}^{\partial=\mathbf{z}}_{G}
. By
Theorem

6.7
, there is a unique nondegenerate
[\mathbf{v}^{\prime},\mathbf{R}^{\prime}]\in\mathcal{C}^{n-k}_{G}
with
\mathbf{R}^{\prime}=\mathbf{R}
. The map
\alpha
sends
[\mathbf{v},\mathbf{R}]\mapsto V=\partial[\mathbf{v}^{\prime},\mathbf{R}]
.
Theorem

6.7
shows
V\in T_{G^{op}}
, so to show
\alpha
is well-defined, we need to show
\mathbf{z}\subset V
.

Fix a representative
A
of
\mathbf{z}
and fix a representative
(\mathbf{v},\mathbf{R})
of
[\mathbf{v},\mathbf{R}]
with boundary
A
. Fix a representative
(\mathbf{v}^{\prime},\mathbf{R})
of
[\mathbf{v}^{\prime},\mathbf{R}]
with exactly the same relations as
(\mathbf{v},\mathbf{R})
. Choose
I\in\binom{[n]}{n-k}
such that
G
has an acyclic reverse perfect orientation with sources
I
. By assumption, the
m\times(n-k)
matrix
[z_{i_{1}}\dots z_{i_{n-k}}]
is full rank, where
I=\{i_{1},\dots,i_{n-k}\}
. So we may extend
[z_{i_{1}}\dots z_{i_{n-k}}]
to a full-rank
(n-k)\times(n-k)
matrix
M^{\prime}
, by adding some rows to the bottom. Since
V\in T_{G^{op}}
,
\langle I\rangle_{V}\neq 0
; in other words, the vectors
v^{\prime}_{i_{1}},\dots,v^{\prime}_{i_{n-k}}
are a basis of
\mathbb{C}^{n-k}
. Using the
GL_{m}
action if necessary, we may assume that
M=[v^{\prime}_{i_{1}}\dots v^{\prime}_{i_{n-k}}]
.

Because
G
is reduced and has an acyclic reverse perfect orientation
\mathcal{O}
with source set
I
,
it satisfies the assumptions of
Lemma

3.6
. Applying (
3
) to both
(\mathbf{v}^{\prime},\mathbf{R})
and
(\mathbf{v},\mathbf{R})
, we obtain that, for
j\notin I
,

(7)

z_{j}=\sum_{i\in I}\left(\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}P:i\to j\\
\text{ path in }\mathcal{O}\end{subarray}}\frac{\prod_{e}r_{e}}{\prod_{e^{\prime}}(-r_{e^{\prime}})}\right)z_{i}\qquad\text{and}\qquad v_{j}^{\prime}=\sum_{i\in I}\left(\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}P:i\to j\\
\text{ path in }\mathcal{O}\end{subarray}}\frac{\prod_{e}r_{e}}{\prod_{e^{\prime}}(-r_{e^{\prime}})}\right)v^{\prime}_{i}.

Projecting
v^{\prime}_{i}
onto the first
m
coordinates gives
z_{i}
for
i\in I
by construction. So this formula shows that projecting
v_{j}^{\prime}
onto the first
m
coordinates gives
z_{j}
. That is, (a representative for)
\mathbf{z}
is the first
m
rows of (a representative for)
V
, and
\mathbf{z}\subset V
as desired.

The inverse map
\beta
of
\alpha
is defined as follows. For
V\in T_{G^{op}}
with
\mathbf{z}\subset V
, let
(\mathbf{v}^{\prime},\mathbf{R}^{\prime})
be a representative of the unique
[\mathbf{v}^{\prime},\mathbf{R}^{\prime}]\in\mathcal{C}^{\partial=V}_{G}
. Use the
GL_{m}
-action to make the top
m
rows of
\partial(\mathbf{v}^{\prime},\mathbf{R}^{\prime})
into a representative of
\mathbf{z}
; this is possible because by assumption,
\mathbf{z}\subset V
. Then we define
(\mathbf{v},\mathbf{R})
by setting
\mathbf{R}:=\mathbf{R}^{\prime}
, and setting
v_{b}
to be the projection of
v_{b}^{\prime}
to the first
m
coordinates. We set
\beta(V)=[\mathbf{v},\mathbf{R}]\in\mathcal{C}^{m}_{G}
.

The argument that
\alpha
is well-defined also shows that
\beta\circ\alpha
is the identity. We note that
V
and
\alpha\circ\beta(V)
are boundaries of
(n-k)
-VRCs with identical relations
\mathbf{R}^{\prime}
.
Theorem

6.7
implies that
\mathbf{R}^{\prime}
determines the boundary, so
V=\alpha\circ\beta(V)
.

We now turn to the map

(8)

\{V\in T_{G^{op}}:\mathbf{z}\subset V\}\xrightarrow{\sim}\{C\in T_{G}:C\subset\mathbf{z}^{\perp}\}

which sends
V
to its orthogonal complement
V^{\perp}=:C
. Clearly
\mathbf{z}\subset V
if and only if
C\subset\mathbf{z}^{\perp}
, so as long as orthogonal complement takes
T_{G^{op}}
to
T_{G}
and vice-versa, the map is well-defined and bijective.

Let
N
be the
n\times n
diagonal matrix with diagonal
1,-1,\dots,(-1)^{n-1}
. The map
V\mapsto VN
is an involution on
T_{G^{op}}
: given a VRC on
G
with boundary
V
, one can produce a VRC with boundary
VN
by changing the sign of
r_{e}
for
e
adjacent to boundary vertices
2,4,6,
etc.

Lemma

A.14
shows that the map
V\mapsto(VN)^{\perp}
is a bijection between
T_{G^{op}}
and
T_{G}
.
Composing these maps shows that
V\mapsto VN\mapsto V^{\perp}
is a bijection from
T_{G^{op}}
to
T_{G}
.
∎

Remark 6.9
.

The proof of
Theorem

6.8
uses only that for some
I\in\binom{[n]}{n-k}
such that
G
has an acyclic reverse perfect orientation with sources
I
, the submatrix of
\mathbf{z}
in columns
I
is full rank. Thus,
Theorem

6.8
holds for
\mathbf{z}
in a slightly larger open set than
\Gr_{m,n}^{\circ}
.

Corollary 6.10
.

Suppose
\Pi_{G}\subset\Gr_{k,n}
has dimension
km
. Then for generic
\mathbf{z}\in\Gr_{m,n}
,

|\mathcal{C}^{\partial=\mathbf{z}}_{G}|=\operatorname{IN}_{m}(G),

that is, the number of
m
-VRC’s with boundary
\mathbf{z}
is equal to the
intersection number of
\Pi_{G}
.

In particular, for generic
\mathbf{z}
,
|\mathcal{C}^{\partial=\mathbf{z}}_{G}|
is finite, does not depend on
\mathbf{z}
, and depends only on the move-equivalence class of
G
.

Proof.

Let
\mathcal{U}\subset\Gr_{m,n}
be the (open dense) subset guaranteed by
Proposition

6.3
where
\{C\in T_{G}:C\subset\mathbf{z}^{\perp}\}
has size
\operatorname{IN}_{m}(G)
.

For
\mathbf{z}\in\Gr_{m,n}^{\circ}\cap\mathcal{U}
, the assumptions of
Theorem

6.8
are satisfied. So
Theorem

6.8
and the choice of
\mathcal{U}
imply that

|\mathcal{C}^{\partial=\mathbf{z}}_{G}|=|\{C\in T_{G}:C\subset\mathbf{z}^{\perp}\}|=\operatorname{IN}_{m}(G).

For the second sentence,
|\mathcal{C}^{\partial=\mathbf{z}}_{G}|
for generic
\mathbf{z}
depends only on the move-equivalence class of
G
because
\Pi_{G}
depends only on the move-equivalence class of
G
, and
\operatorname{IN}_{m}(G)
is defined in terms of
\Pi_{G}
. The other statements are clear.
∎

Recall that
G
is
m
-generically solvable if
|\mathcal{C}^{\partial=\mathbf{z}}_{G}|=1
for generic
\mathbf{z}\in\Gr_{m,n}
. The next proposition shows that a graph
G
is
m
-generically solvable for at most one
m
.

Proposition 6.11
.

Suppose
G
is reduced and of type
(k,n)
. If for generic
\mathbf{z}\in\Gr_{m,n}
,
\mathcal{C}^{\partial=\mathbf{z}}_{G}
is nonempty and finite, then

m=(\#(\text{faces of }G)-1)/(|W|-|B_{int}|)=(1/k)\dim\Pi_{G}.

In particular, if
G
is
m
-generically solvable, then
m=\dim\Pi_{G}/k
.

Proof.

The first statement implies the second, since
G
is
m
-generically solvable if
|\mathcal{C}^{\partial=\mathbf{z}}_{G}|=1
for generic
\mathbf{z}\in\Gr_{m,n}
.

We prove the contrapositive of the first statement: if
\dim\Pi_{G}\neq km
, then for generic
\mathbf{z}
,
\mathcal{C}^{\partial=\mathbf{z}}_{G}
is either empty or infinite.

If
\dim\Pi_{G}<km
, then
Lemma

6.1
implies that for generic
\mathbf{z}\in\Gr_{m,n}
,

\{C\in T_{G}:C\subset\mathbf{z}^{\perp}\}\subset\{C\in\Pi_{G}:C\subset\mathbf{z}^{\perp}\}=\emptyset.

By
Theorem

6.8
, the leftmost set is also equal to
\mathcal{C}^{\partial=\mathbf{z}}_{G}
.

Now suppose
\dim\Pi_{G}>km
. We may assume that for generic
\mathbf{z}\in\Gr_{m,n}
,

\{C\in\Pi_{G}:C\subset\mathbf{z}^{\perp}\}\neq\emptyset,

since otherwise we are done by the reasoning of the previous paragraph. This means that the projection map
\pi
from the incidence variety

X=\{(C,\mathbf{z})\in\Pi_{G}\times\Gr_{m,n}:C\subset\mathbf{z}^{\perp}\}

to
\Gr_{m,n}
is dominant. For generic
\mathbf{z}\in\Gr_{m,n}
, the dimension of the fiber
\pi^{-1}(\mathbf{z})
is
\dim X-\dim\Gr_{m,n}
. Using the computation of
\dim X
from
Lemma

6.1
and the assumption that
\dim\Pi_{G}>km
, we conclude that generic fibers
\pi^{-1}(\mathbf{z})
have positive dimension. Note that
T_{G}
is obtained by removing some divisors from
\Pi_{G}
and so is open and dense. Similarly,
\tilde{T}_{G}:=T_{G}\times\Gr_{m,n}
is open and dense in
X
. By a standard transversality argument, this implies that for generic
\mathbf{z}\in\Gr_{m,n}
, the intersection
\pi^{-1}(\mathbf{z})\cap\tilde{T}_{G}
is positive dimension.

Now, the projection map from
\pi^{-1}(\mathbf{z})\cap\tilde{T}_{G}
to
\Pi_{G}
is a bijection, with image exactly

\{C\in T_{G}:C\subset\mathbf{z}^{\perp}\}

which for generic
\mathbf{z}
is in bijection with
\mathcal{C}^{\partial=\mathbf{z}}_{G}
by
Theorem

6.8
. So for generic
\mathbf{z}
, there are infinitely many
m
-VRC with boundary
\mathbf{z}
.
∎

Corollaries

6.10
and
6.11
together imply the following corollary.

Corollary 6.12
.

A plabic graph
G
of type
(k,n)
is
m
-generically solvable if and only if

\Pi_{G}
has dimension
km
and
m
-intersection number
1
.

Corollary

6.10
shows that if
\Pi_{G}\subset\Gr_{k,n}^{\geq 0}
has dimension
km
, then
m
-VRCs with generic boundary are quite well-behaved. One may also obtain the next two corollaries, which are along similar lines. They show that in this scenario, square moves generically induce a bijection on VRCs and most VRCs are nondegenerate.

Corollary 6.13
.

Suppose
G
is reduced and
\Pi_{G}\subset\Gr_{k,n}
has dimension
km
. If
G
and
G^{\prime}
are related by a square move, then for generic
\mathbf{z}\in\Gr_{m,n}
, the map in
Figure

2
gives a bijection between
\mathcal{C}^{\partial=\mathbf{z}}_{G}
and
\mathcal{C}^{\partial=\mathbf{z}}_{G^{\prime}}
.

Corollary 6.14
.

Suppose
G
is a reduced
(k,n)
-plabic graph and
\Pi_{G}
has dimension
km
. Then for generic
\mathbf{z}\in\Gr_{m,n}
, the set
\mathcal{C}^{\partial=\mathbf{z}}_{G}
does not contain any degenerate VRCs.

Recall from
Definition

4.6
the notion of a
pinning
of
G
. We conclude this section by showing pinnings exist for solvable
G
.

Proposition 6.15
.

Suppose
G
is generically
m
-solvable, or equivalently
\Pi_{G}\subset\Gr_{k,n}
has dimension
km
and
\operatorname{IN}_{m}(G)=1
. Denote by
[\mathbf{v},\mathbf{R}]^{\partial=\mathbf{z}}
the unique
m
-VRC with boundary
\mathbf{z}\in\Gr_{m,n}
, where
\mathbf{z}
is generic. Then there are rational functions
\{f_{e}\}_{e\in E(G)}\subset\mathbb{C}(\widehat{\Gr}_{m,n})
such that for generic
\mathbf{z}
,
[\mathbf{v},\mathbf{R}]^{\partial=\mathbf{z}}
is represented by
(\{v_{b}(\mathbf{z})\},\{f_{e}(\mathbf{z})\})
, where the vectors
v_{b}(\mathbf{z})
are defined using (
3
). In other words, a pinning of
G
exists.

Proof.

Recall that by
Theorem

6.8
and
Proposition

6.3
, for
\mathbf{z}
in some open dense set
\mathcal{U}_{1}\subseteq\Gr_{m,n}
,

|\mathcal{C}^{\partial=\mathbf{z}}_{G}|=|\{V\in T_{G^{op}}:\mathbf{z}\subset V\}|=|\{C\in T_{G}:C\subset\mathbf{z}^{\perp}\}|=|\{C\in\Pi_{G}:C\subset\mathbf{z}^{\perp}\}|=1.

Consider the incidence variety

X=\{(\mathbf{z},V)\in\Gr_{m,n}\times\Pi_{G^{op}}:\mathbf{z}\subset V\}\subset\Gr_{m,n}\times\Gr_{n-k,n}.

The projection
X\to\Gr_{m,n}
onto the first factor is a regular map. By the previous paragraph, this projection is dominant and points in
\mathcal{U}_{1}
have a unique preimage. This implies the projection map is birational, so there is a rational map
F:\Gr_{m,n}\to X
sending
\mathbf{z}\mapsto(\mathbf{z},F(\mathbf{z}))
which is the inverse of the projection map on an open dense set
\mathcal{U}\subseteq\Gr_{m,n}
. We may assume
\mathcal{U}\subset\mathcal{U}_{1}
. The Plücker coordinates of
F(\mathbf{z})
are rational functions in the Plücker coordinates of
\mathbf{z}
. By the choice of
\mathcal{U}
,
F(\mathbf{z})\in T_{G^{op}}
.

For
\mathbf{z}\in\mathcal{U}
, let
[\mathbf{v},\mathbf{R}]
be the unique element of
\mathcal{C}^{\partial=\mathbf{z}}_{G}
. By
Theorem

6.8
,
F(\mathbf{z})=\alpha[\mathbf{v},\mathbf{R}]
. That is,
F(\mathbf{z})
is the boundary of the
(n-k)
-VRC
[\mathbf{v}^{\prime},\mathbf{R}]
.
[
MS17
, Proposition 5.9 and Theorem 7.1]
together with
Theorem

6.7
gives an explicit formula for a rational map
T_{G^{op}}\to(\mathbb{C}^{*})^{E(G)}
sending
V=\partial[\mathbf{v}^{\prime\prime},\mathbf{R}^{\prime\prime}]
to
\widehat{\mathbf{R}^{\prime\prime}}
, where
\widehat{\mathbf{R}^{\prime\prime}}
is a particular distinguished representative of the gauge-equivalence class of
\mathbf{R}^{\prime\prime}
.

So the map
\Gr_{m,n}\to(\mathbb{C}^{*})^{E(G)}
sending

\mathbf{z}\mapsto F(\mathbf{z})=\partial[\mathbf{v}^{\prime},\mathbf{R}]\mapsto\widehat{\mathbf{R}}

is a composition of rational maps and thus is rational. This shows the desired functions
f_{e}
exist, that is, that there is an
m
-VRC with boundary
\mathbf{z}
with
r_{e}=f_{e}(\mathbf{z})
.
Lemma

3.6
implies that for this
m
-VRC, the vectors are given by (
3
).

∎

Remark 6.16
.

While
Proposition

6.15
is not constructive, in practice one may be able to compute the rational functions
f_{e}
using e.g. the Grassmann-Cayley algebra, as we do in
Definition

7.19
when
G
is a tree.
Once one has
\{f_{e}\}_{e\in E(G)}
, one can invert
\Upsilon_{Z,G}
as follows. First, choose Kasteleyn signs on
G
(cf.
[
AGPR24
, Section 7.1]
), and then change the sign on the boundary edges
2,4,6,
etc. Denote these modified Kasteleyn signs by
\{\sigma_{e}\}_{e\in E(G)}
. Then for generic
\mathbf{z}\in\Gr_{m}(W)
, the preimage
C=\Upsilon_{Z,G}^{-1}(\mathbf{z})
equals
\mathbb{B}_{G}(\{\sigma_{e}f_{e}(z)\})
, where
\mathbb{B}_{G}
is the boundary measurement map. That is,
C
is the rowspan of the path matrix
A(G,\mathcal{O})
using edge weights
\{\sigma_{e}f_{e}(\mathbf{z})\}
(see
Definition

A.7
), and its Plücker coordinates are given by
Proposition

A.8
.

Remark 6.17
.

The results in this subsection, connecting intersection number and
m
-VRCs, were in part inspired by various examples in the physics literature of inverting the amplituhedron map by ‘solving
C\cdot\mathbf{z}=0
’
[
MSSV20
,
HL21
]
.
For more on the relationship to the ‘
C\cdot\mathbf{z}=0
’ equations, see
[
ELP+23
, Remark 7.13]
.

6.3.
Proof that dominant and brushable are equivalent

In this section, we use the results relating VRCs and elements of
T_{G}
to prove
Theorem

4.13
, which states that a solvable tangle is dominant if and only if it admits a brushing.

We will need the following combinatorial lemmas. Recall from
Definition

A.12
the definition of a gauge-fix of a plabic graph, and the characterization of gauge-fixes from
Proposition

A.13
.

Lemma 6.18
.

Let
G
be a reduced plabic graph
and
\{P_{j}\}_{j\in J}
a collection of vertex-disjoint paths where
P_{j}
starts at boundary vertex
j
and ends at some vertex
v_{j}
. Then there is a gauge-fix
E(G)=E_{1}\sqcup E_{\neq 1}
such that

•

the edges of the paths
\cup_{j}P_{j}
are contained in
E_{1}
;

•

the only path from a boundary vertex to
v_{j}
which avoids
E_{\neq 1}
is
P_{j}
.

Proof.

Let
G^{\prime}
be the graph
G
together with edges
e_{i}:=(i,i+1)
for
i=1,\dots,n-1
. Note that
G^{\prime}
is connected, since all connected components of
G
contain a boundary vertex. There is a spanning tree
T
of
G^{\prime}
which contains
\cup_{j\in J}P_{j}
and
\cup_{i\in[n-1]}e_{i}
. Removing the edges
e_{i}
from
T
, we obtain a spanning forest
T^{\prime}
of
G
which contains
\cup_{j\in J}P_{j}
. Each connected component
C
of
T^{\prime}
contains exactly one boundary vertex: if
C
contained both
i
and
j
, then
T
would contain a cycle; if
C
contains no boundary vertex, then this implies
T
has more than one connected component. By
Proposition

A.13
, if we set
E_{1}
to be the edges of
T^{\prime}
and
E_{\neq 1}:=E(G)\setminus E_{1}
, then
E_{1}\sqcup E_{\neq 1}
is a gauge-fix.

By construction
\cup_{j}P_{j}
is contained in
E_{1}
. This implies
j
and
v_{j}
are in the same connected component of
T^{\prime}
. Since
T^{\prime}
is a forest, any path from
j
to
v_{j}
which is not
P_{j}
passes through an edge in
E_{\neq 1}
. Consider another boundary vertex
i
. This lies in another connected component of
T^{\prime}
, so every path from
i
to
v_{j}
passes through an edge in
E_{\neq 1}
. This shows the constructed gauge-fix has the desired properties.
∎

Proof of
Theorem

4.13
.

(\Rightarrow):
We show that if
(G,\mathbf{D})
admits a brushing, then it is dominant. Fix a brushing
\mathcal{B}
, and a blob
D\in\mathbf{D}
. Let
\mathcal{O}:=\mathcal{O}^{D}
be the corresponding acyclic reverse perfect orientation and
\{P_{i}\}_{i\in I}
the collection of paths. We index
P_{i}
by its starting point
i\in B_{bd}
rather than its ending point
u_{i}\in D
for convenience. Since
every boundary vertex of
D
is an endpoint of a unique
P_{i}
, there is a natural bijection from
I
to
D
. To ease notation, we will use
I
as an index set throughout rather than
D
.

Choose a gauge-fix
E(G)=E_{1}\cup E_{\neq 1}
having the properties of
Lemma

6.18
. For a VRC
[\mathbf{v},\mathbf{R}]\in\mathcal{C}^{m}_{G}
, we will choose the unique representative
(\mathbf{v},\mathbf{R})
where
r_{e}=1
for
e\in E_{1}
(uniqueness is because
E_{1}\cup E_{\neq 1}
is a gauge-fix).
To ease notation, we denote the vector on
b_{u_{i}}
by
w_{i}
and denote by
\mathbf{w}
the element of
\Gr_{m,I}
with columns
w_{i}
.
This element depends on
(\mathbf{v},\mathbf{R})
, though we omit this dependence from the notation. A priori,
\mathbf{w}
may not be full-rank; however, its maximal minors are rational functions in the Plücker coordinates of
\mathbf{z}
, and the proof below shows that they are not identically zero, so for generic
\mathbf{z}
,
\mathbf{w}
is indeed an element of
\Gr_{m,I}
.

It follows from
Theorem

6.8
if the set

\{\mathbf{w}:\exists V\in T_{G^{op}}~\text{with}~\partial[\mathbf{v},\mathbf{R}]\subset V\}

is dense in
\Gr_{m,I}
, then
(G,\mathbf{D})
is dominant
6
6

6

The definition of dominance involves density in
\Conf^{\circ}_{m,I}
, but this is implied by density in
\Gr_{m,I}
.
.

Fix
\mathbf{p}=(p_{i})_{i\in I}\in\Gr_{m,I}
. It is enough to find
V\in T_{G^{op}}
and
[\mathbf{v},\mathbf{R}]\in\mathcal{C}^{m}_{G}
such that
\partial[\mathbf{v},\mathbf{R}]\subset V
and
\mathbf{w}
is arbitrarily close to
\mathbf{p}.

We will show that we can find
V\in T_{G^{op}}
and
\mathbf{z}\subset V
such that
z_{i}
is arbitrarily close to
p_{i}
for all
i\in I
. We then use
Theorem

6.8
and the gauge-fix to obtain a VRC
(\mathbf{v},\mathbf{R})
with boundary
\mathbf{z}
, and show that in this scenario,
z_{i}
is arbitrarily close to
w_{i}
.

Let
G^{\prime}
be the plabic graph obtained from
G^{op}
by erasing the edges of
E_{\neq 1}
. Write
E_{\neq 1}^{+}
for the edges of
E_{\neq 1}
oriented black-to-white, and
E_{\neq 1}^{-}
for those oriented white-to-black. Note that
T_{G^{\prime}}
is in the boundary of
T_{G^{op}}
and consists of a single point
V^{\prime}
. Indeed, points in
T_{G^{op}}
have a unique representative
A
with the identity matrix in columns indexed by the source set
J
of
\mathcal{O}
, and are the boundary of a unique
(n-k)
-VRC on
G
with
r_{e}=1
for
e\in E_{1}
. Then
T_{G^{\prime}}
is obtained by letting
r_{e}\to 0
for
e\in E_{\neq 1}^{+}
and
r_{e}\to\infty
for
e\in E_{\neq 1}^{-}
. Every path between boundary vertices in
\mathcal{O}
involves an edge of
E_{\neq 1}
, so the boundary of the resulting VRC is the matrix
M
with the identity in columns
J
and zero columns elsewhere. The point
V^{\prime}
is represented by
M
and has a single nonzero Plücker coordinate,
\langle J\rangle
.

Let
\mathbf{p}^{\prime}\in\Gr_{m,n}
be obtained from
\mathbf{p}
by adding zero columns in indices
[n]\setminus I
. Since
I\subset J
,
V^{\prime}
also has a representative matrix
M^{\prime}=gM
whose top
m
rows are (a representative for)
\mathbf{p}^{\prime}
.

Define
B=gA
. As
r_{e}\to 0
for
e\in E_{\neq 1}^{+}
and
r_{e}\to\infty
for
e\in E_{\neq 1}^{-}
,
A\to M
and so
B\to M^{\prime}
. If we take the top
m
rows of
B
, we obtain an element
\mathbf{z}\in\Gr_{m,n}
which is contained in
B
and which approaches
\mathbf{p}^{\prime}
. In particular,
z_{i}\to p_{i}
, as desired.

Let
(\mathbf{v},\mathbf{R})
be the
m
-VRC with boundary
\mathbf{z}
and coefficients
r_{e}
equal to those of the VRC with boundary
B
(in particular, with
r_{e}=1
for
e\in E_{1}
). This VRC exists by
Theorem

6.8
. If we express
w_{i}
using (
3
), we have

w_{u}=z_{i}+\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}{P:j\to u}\\
{P\neq P_{i}}\end{subarray}}\wt(P)z_{j}

where the sum is over paths
P\neq P_{i}
in
\mathcal{O}^{D}
from a boundary vertex to
u
. By
Lemma

6.18
, for all terms in the right sum,
\wt(P)\neq 1
. In particular,
\wt(P)
has in the numerator
r_{e}
for some
e\in E_{\neq 1}^{+}
, or has in the denominator
r_{e}
for some
e\in E_{\neq 1}^{+}
. So as
x_{e}
become arbitrarily small (or arbitrarily large, as appropriate),
w_{i}
approaches
z_{i}
, which in turn approaches
p_{i}
, as desired.

(\Leftarrow):
We now show that if
(G,\mathbf{D})
is solvable and does not admit a brushing, then it is not dominant.

Suppose for
D\in\mathbf{D}
, there is no acyclic reverse perfect orientation with vertex-disjoint paths from boundary vertices to
D
. Let
\mathcal{O}
be any acyclic reverse perfect orientation. A max-flow/min-cut argument shows that there is a number
l<|D|
such that

•

there is a vertex-disjoint path collection from
B_{bd}
to
D
consisting of
l
paths. Say the set of sources in these paths is
I\subset B_{bd}
.

•

there is a set of
l
vertices
X
such that all vertex-disjoint path collections from
B_{bd}
to
D
pass through
X
. We may assume
X=\{b_{1},\dots,b_{l}\}
consists of black vertices.

Let
(G^{\prime},D)
be the subtangle obtained by restricting to vertices and edges that can be reached from
X
via a directed path in
\mathcal{O}
; note this includes all boundary vectors
b_{u}
of the blob
D
. Let
B_{bd}^{\prime}
denote the boundary vertices of
G^{\prime}
. The orientation
\mathcal{O}
restricts to an acyclic reverse perfect orientation of
G^{\prime}
, with source set exactly
X.

The restriction of a VRC of
G
to
G^{\prime}
is a VRC for
G^{\prime}
, by definition.
Moreover, the restriction of the VRC
(\mathbf{v},\mathbf{R})
to
G^{\prime}
depends only on the vectors
\{v_{b}\}_{b\in B_{bd}^{\prime}}
, again by definition of the VRC.
Alternately, using
Lemma

3.6
, the restriction to
G^{\prime}
depends only on
\mathbf{R}|_{G^{\prime}}
and
\{v_{b}\}_{b\in X}
.

Additionally, for
\mathbf{z}
coming from a dense open set, the vectors
(v_{b}(\mathbf{z}))_{b\in B_{bd}^{\prime}}
in the VRC
[\mathbf{v},\mathbf{R}]^{\partial=\mathbf{z}}
are the boundary of a unique VRC on
G^{\prime}
. Indeed, otherwise we could change the VRC on
G^{\prime}
to another without changing
(v_{b}(\mathbf{z}))_{b\in B_{bd}^{\prime}}
, and obtain another VRC on
G
with boundary
\mathbf{z}
, contradicting solvability of
G
. So the tangle
(G^{\prime},D)
gives a map
f:\Conf^{\circ}_{m,B_{bd}(G^{\prime})}\dashrightarrow\Conf_{m,D}
.

Fix now a pinning of
(G,\mathbf{D})
. There is some gauge fix such that vertex-disjoint paths from
I
to
X
are in
E_{1}
; we may assume that the coefficients on these edges are 1. Note that by the argument of the ‘if’ direction above, the set of obtainable
\mathbf{v}_{X}:=(v_{b}(\mathbf{z}))_{b\in X}
is dense in
\Gr_{m,l}.
Thus, we can write the blob’s boundary vectors
\{v_{b_{u}}(\mathbf{z})\}_{u\in D}
as functions on a dense subset of
\Gr_{m,l}.

Write
\mathbf{w}(\mathbf{z})=(w_{u})_{u\in D}
for the blob vectors as functions of
\mathbf{z}.
Then
\mathbf{w}(\mathbf{z})
is a rational function on
\mathbf{z},
by
Proposition

6.15
. On the other hand, by the above discussion, we can also write
\mathbf{w}
as a function of
\mathbf{v}_{X},
that is, we can write

F(\mathbf{v}_{X}(\mathbf{z}))=\mathbf{w}(\mathbf{z}),

where
F
is defined on a dense subset of
\Gr_{m,l}
, and also
\mathbf{v}_{X}(\mathbf{z})
is a rational function of
\mathbf{z}.
Note that
F
is a lift of
f
to the Grassmannian.

It suffices to show that
F
is a rational map from
\Gr_{m,l}
to
\Gr_{m,D}
, and hence
f
is a rational map from
\Conf^{\circ}_{m,l}
to
\Conf_{m,D}
. Since
l<|D|
,
f
cannot be dominant. This implies that the blob boundary lines
\{\mathbb{C}\mathbf{w}(\mathbf{z}):\mathbf{z}\in\Gr_{m,n}\}
are not dense in
\Conf^{\circ}_{m,D}.

We now show that
F
is rational. Let
\mathcal{U}\subseteq\Gr_{m,n}
be an open dense set on which there is a unique and non-degenerate VRC with boundary
\mathbf{z}
, which exists by
Proposition

6.15
and
Corollary

6.14
. Let
S
be
the (Zariski dense) image of the map
\mathcal{U}\to\Gr_{m,l}
which sends
\mathbf{z}\mapsto\mathbf{v}_{X}(\mathbf{z})
. Note that when
\mathbf{z}\in\mathcal{U},

\mathbf{w}(\mathbf{z})
is well-defined, hence for
\mathbf{x}\in S
,
F(\mathbf{x})
is well-defined. For
\mathbf{x}\in S
, write
\mathbf{x}^{\prime}\in\Gr_{m,n}
as the vector space obtained from
\mathbf{x}
by adding zero columns in columns
N\setminus I
.
As in the ‘if’ direction, we can take the limit
\mathbf{z}\to\mathbf{x}^{\prime}
by letting
r_{e}\to 0
(
r_{e}\to\infty
) for black-to-white (white-to-black) edges of
G\setminus G^{\prime}
with
r_{e}\nequiv 1
. Then

\lim_{\mathcal{U}\ni\mathbf{z}\to\mathbf{x}^{\prime}}\mathbf{v}_{X}(\mathbf{z})=\mathbf{x}.

Also we have that

\lim_{\mathbf{z}\to\mathbf{x}^{\prime}}\mathbf{w}(\mathbf{z})=F(\mathbf{x})
. Indeed,
\mathbf{x}\in S,
hence
F(\mathbf{x})
is well-defined and equals
\mathbf{w}(\mathbf{z})
for every
\mathbf{z}\in\mathcal{U}
for which
\mathbf{v}_{X}(\mathbf{z})=\mathbf{x}.

Thus, the rational function
\mathbf{w}(-)
extends to
\mathbf{x}^{\prime}
and we can write

F(\mathbf{x})=\mathbf{w}(\mathbf{x}^{\prime}).

Since
\mathbf{w}
is rational, this shows that
F
is a rational mapping from
S,
hence from
\Gr_{m,l}
, to
\Gr_{m,D}.

∎

7.
Characterizing intersection number one trees

In this section we focus on plabic trees. In particular,
we will show in
Theorem

7.23
that if
G
is a plabic tree, it is solvable (equivalently,
\dim\Pi_{G}=km
and
\operatorname{IN}_{m}(G)=1
) if and only if it is
m
-balanced
, see
Definition

7.2
.
We also provide an explicit
m
-VRC on amplitrees with fixed boundary vectors via the Grassmann-Cayley algebra (
Proposition

7.21
).

We will assume throughout this section that our plabic trees are
bipartite, i.e.
G=((B,W),E)
, with
B=B_{int}\sqcup B_{bd}

as in
3.1
.

7.1.
Plabic trees and the
m
-balanced property

In this section we introduce the notion of
m
-balanced
plabic trees. We will show in

Corollary

7.7
that if a plabic tree
G
of type
(k,km+1)

fails to be
m
-balanced, then

\operatorname{IN}_{m}(G)=0
.

For convenience, we record in
Lemma

7.1
some well-known (and easy to prove) facts about plabic trees that we will use later.

Lemma 7.1
.

Let
G
be a plabic tree of type
(k,n)
.

(1)

Then
G
is reduced, and
\dim\Pi_{G}=n-1
.

(2)

We have

k=1+\sum_{b\in B_{int}}(\deg b-2).

(3)

We can apply moves to
G
to make
G
bipartite with trivalent internal black vertices.

(4)

We have that

k=|W|-|B_{int}|
.

(5)

If
G
is bipartite with black vertices trivalent, then
k=|B_{int}|+1
.

Definition 7.2
.

Let
G
be a bipartite plabic tree of type
(k,km+1)
.
We say that
G
is
m
-balanced
if
for each edge
e
of
G
, if we write

G\setminus\{e\}=G_{1}\sqcup G_{2}
(giving “half” the edge
e
to each
G_{i}
), then for each
i=1,2
, we have

m(k_{i}-1)<\dim\Pi_{G_{i}}\leq mk_{i},

where
k_{i}
is the
k
-statistic of the tree
G_{i}
.
A
(k,m)
-amplitree
is a bipartite plabic tree
G

of type
(k,km+1)
which is
m
-balanced.

Remark 7.3
.

We will show in
Theorem

7.22
that ‘amplitrees’ have intersection number one and, by
Remark

2.29
, they correspond to a new infinite class of rational Yangian invariants for scattering amplitudes in
\mathcal{N}=4
SYM (hence the use of ‘ampli’ in ‘amplitree’).

We start by proving a characterization
of the
m
-balanced property plus some helpful lemmas.

Lemma 7.4
.

Let
G
be a bipartite plabic tree of type
(k,km+1)
. Then the
m
-balanced property says that
for each
i=1,2
, we have

(9)

1\leq|B_{bd}\cap G_{i}|-m\sum_{b\in G_{i}\cap B_{int}}(\deg b-2)\leq m.

Proof.

Let us rewrite (
9
) as

1+m\sum_{b\in G_{i}\cap B_{int}}(\deg b-2)\leq|B_{bd}\cap G_{i}|\leq m+m\sum_{b\in G_{i}\cap B_{int}}(\deg b-2).

Using
Lemma

7.1
(1) and (2), we then get

1+m(k_{i}-1)\leq\dim\Pi_{G_{i}}\leq mk_{i}.

The result follows.
∎

The following observations are easy to verify.

Lemma 7.5
.

Use the notation of
Definition

7.2
.

•

The
m
-balanced property is preserved under bipartite expand and contract moves.

•

If (
9
) holds for
G_{1}
,
then it also holds for
G_{2}.

The next proposition shows the first connection between the
m
-balanced property and intersection number.

Proposition 7.6
.

Let
G
be a bipartite plabic tree of type

(k,km+1)
. Suppose that the
m
-balanced property fails.
Then we can find a subtree
G^{\prime}
of
G
and
a perfect orientation
\mathcal{O}
of
G

which satisfy
the conditions of
Proposition

6.5
.

Proof.

By
Lemma

7.1
we can assume without loss of generality
that the internal black vertices of
G
are trivalent.
Since the
m
-balanced property fails, we know that
there is an edge
e
such that
G\setminus\{e\}=G_{1}\sqcup G_{2}

such that without loss of generality,

\dim\Pi_{G_{1}}\leq m(k_{1}-1)
(and correspondingly

\dim\Pi_{G_{2}}>mk_{2}
). Let
v_{i}
denote the vertex of
e
in
G_{i}
.

Choose a perfect orientation
\mathcal{O}
of
G
.
We first claim that without loss of generality, we can
assume that
e
is directed from
G_{2}
to
G_{1}
. To see this,
note that each edge
e
is part of (at least one) directed
path from a boundary vertex of
G
to another boundary vertex.
(This is because no internal vertex of
G
is a source or
sink, so we will never get “stuck” when we try to produce such
a path.) Thus, if
e
is not directed from
G_{2}
to
G_{1}

in
\mathcal{O}
, we can choose a directed path
p
passing through
e

which goes from one boundary vertex to another, and reverse all
arrows along
p
, obtaining a new perfect orientation in which

e
is oriented from
G_{2}
to
G_{1}
.

Note that
G_{1}
must contain at least two boundary vertices from
B_{bd}
. Otherwise
\dim\Pi_{G_{1}}=1
,
k_{1}=1
,
and it’s not true that
\dim\Pi_{G_{1}}\leq m(k_{1}-1).

Let
S
be the set of sources of
\mathcal{O}
which lie in

B_{bd}\cap G_{1}
. Let
k^{\prime}=|S|
. Note
that
k_{1}=1+|S|=1+k^{\prime}
(since
G_{1}
also has a source at
v_{2}
).

Suppose -vertex
v_{1}
is black.
It is trivalent, so it must have another edge
e^{\prime}
directed
towards it, and thus there must be a path from some
vertex of
B_{bd}\cap G_{1}
to
v_{1}
.
Thus
1\leq|S|<k
.
Let
G^{\prime}
be the graph
G_{1}
with the edge
e
removed.
Clearly
\mathcal{O}
restricts to a perfect orientation of
G^{\prime}
with sources
S
, so

G^{\prime}
has type
(k^{\prime},n^{\prime})
for some
n^{\prime}
.
Also

\dim\Pi_{G^{\prime}}<\dim\Pi_{G_{1}}\leq m(k_{1}-1)=mk^{\prime}.

Thus
the conditions of
Proposition

6.5
are satisfied.

Now suppose vertex
v_{1}
is white.
First we claim that
there’s at least one source in
G_{1}\cap B_{bd}
.
If not,
G_{1}
has
k_{1}=1
, and so it’s not true
that
\dim\Pi_{G_{1}}\leq m(k_{1}-1).

Let
e_{1},\dots,e_{\ell}
be the edges besides
e
which are incident to
v_{1}
.

Let
G^{\prime}
be
G_{1}
with edges
e,e_{1},\dots,e_{\ell}
and the vertex
v_{1}
removed.
Clearly
\mathcal{O}
restricts to a perfect orientation of
G^{\prime}

(this would not have been true if we only removed edge
e
) with sources
S
, so
G^{\prime}
has type
(k^{\prime},n^{\prime})
for some
n^{\prime}
.
We have
\dim\Pi_{G^{\prime}}<\dim\Pi_{G_{1}}\leq m(k_{1}-1)=mk^{\prime}.

Thus we found a perfect orientation
\mathcal{O}
and a subtree
G^{\prime}

such that
the conditions of
Proposition

6.5
are satisfied.
∎

By
Proposition

7.6
and
Proposition

6.5
, we obtain the following result.

Corollary 7.7
.

Let
G
be a bipartite plabic tree of type

(k,km+1)
, such that the
m
-balanced property fails.
Then
\Pi_{G}
has
m
-intersection number 0.

7.2.
Amplitrees have unique
m
-VRCs and intersection number
1

In this subsection, we show in
Theorem

7.22
that amplitrees are solvable.
We will also show in
Theorem

7.23
that a bipartite plabic tree
is solvable if and only if it is an
amplitree.

To show solvability, we need to construct VRCs on plabic trees. To help us do this, we will decorate the vertices with subspaces of various dimensions.

Definition 7.8
.

Let
G
be a bipartite plabic tree with
B_{bd}=[n]
. Let
r
be an internal vertex, and fix
\mathbf{z}\in\Mat_{m,n}
with columns
z_{1},\dots,z_{n}
. Orient the edges of
G
towards
r
. Going from leaves to root, we associate to each vertex
x
of
G
a subspace
V_{x}^{\rt=r}=V_{x}
of
\mathbb{C}^{m}
as follows:

•

If
x=i
is a boundary vertex,
V_{i}=\spn({z_{i}})
.

•

If
x
is an internal white vertex with children
b_{1},\dots,b_{p}
, then
V_{x}=V_{b_{1}}+\cdots+V_{b_{p}}
.

•

If
x
is an internal black vertex with children
w_{1},\dots,w_{p}
, then
V_{x}=V_{w_{1}}\cap\cdots\cap V_{w_{p}}
.

We often omit the superscript
\rt=r
if the root is clear from context. The subspace
V_{x}^{\rt=r}
clearly depends on
\mathbf{z}
, though we omit this dependence from the notation. Note that if we replace
\mathbf{z}
with
g\mathbf{z}
, for
g\in GL_{m}
, the subspace
V_{x}
becomes
gV_{x}:=\{gv:v\in V_{x}\}.
The following lemma shows the relationship between these vector spaces and VRCs.

Lemma 7.9
.

Suppose
(\mathbf{v},\mathbf{R})
is an
m
-VRC on
G
with boundary
\mathbf{z}\in\Mat_{m,n}
. Let
r
be an internal vertex of
G
. Then for every black vertex
b
of T, the vector
v_{b}
is in the subspace
V_{b}^{\rt=r}
.

Proof.

We prove this going from leaves to root. It is true for the leaves by construction. Choose a non-leaf black vertex
b
, and suppose the lemma is true for all grandchildren of
b
. The children of
b
are white vertices
w_{1},\dots,w_{p}
. Say the children of
w_{i}
are black vertices
b_{1},\dots,b_{j}
. By assumption
v_{b_{\ell}}\in V_{b_{\ell}}
. The definition of
m
-VRCs and the presence of the white vertex
w_{i}
implies that vectors
v_{b},v_{b_{1}},\dots,v_{b_{j}}
satisfy a relation with all coefficients nonzero. In particular,
v_{b}
is in the span of
v_{b_{1}},\dots,v_{b_{j}}
, which is contained in
V_{b_{1}}+\dots+V_{b_{j}}=V_{w_{i}}
. So
v_{b}
is in
V_{w_{1}}\cap\dots\cap V_{w_{p}}=V_{b}
.
∎

We will now restrict our attention to those
\mathbf{z}
where the dimension of the subspaces
V_{x}
are predictable. This will turn out to be a dense open subset of
\Gr_{m,n}
.

The next definition will use the following notion. If
G
is a plabic graph, we denote by
G^{{\operatorname{ext}}}
the plabic graph obtained by adding a bivalent white vertex and bivalent black vertex next to each boundary vertex as in
Figure

9
.

Figure 9.
Adding bivalent white and black vertices to
G
to obtain
G^{{\operatorname{ext}}}

Definition 7.10
.

Let
G,r,\mathbf{z}
and the subspaces
V_{x}^{\rt=r}=V_{x}
be as in
Definition

7.8
. We call
\mathbf{z}

generic for
(G,r)
if for all vertices
x
of
G
,

•

if
x
is a boundary vertex
i
, then
\dim V_{x}=\dim\spn(z_{i})=1

•

if
x
is white with children
b_{1},\dots,b_{p}
, then
\dim V_{x}=\min(\dim V_{b_{1}}+\dots+\dim V_{b_{p}},m)
.

•

if
x
is black with children
w_{1},\dots,w_{p}
, then
\dim V_{x}=\max(\dim V_{w_{1}}+\dots+\dim V_{w_{p}}-(p-1)m,0)
.

We call the right hand sides of the above equations the
expected dimension of
V_{x}
, and denote it by
d_{x}
.
Genericity is preserved under left-multiplication by an element of
GL_{m}
, so if
\mathbf{z}
is generic for
(G,r)
, we also say
\rowsp\mathbf{z}\in\Gr_{m,n}
is generic for
(G,r)
.

We say
\mathbf{z}\in\Mat_{m,n}
and
\rowsp\mathbf{z}\in\Gr_{m,n}
are
generic for
G
if they are generic for
(G^{\operatorname{ext}},r)
for all choices of internal vertex
r
of
G^{\operatorname{ext}}
.

We note that if
\mathbf{z}
is generic for
G
, it is also generic for
(G,r)
for all choices of internal vertex
r
; the extra vertices in
G^{\operatorname{ext}}
do not change the dimension conditions when
r
is a vertex of
G
. They impose additional dimension conditions when the root is chosen to be one of the added vertices, which will be useful in
Lemma

7.18
.

The use of the term “generic” above is justified by the following proposition and corollary.

Proposition 7.11
.

Let
G
be a bipartite plabic tree with
B_{bd}=[n]
and let
r
be an internal vertex. Then there is an open dense
subset
\mathcal{U}_{G,r}
of
\Gr_{m,n}
such that
\mathbf{z}\in\mathcal{U}_{G,r}
is generic for
(G,r)
.

Proof.

We will show, going from leaves to root, that for every vertex
x
there is a Zariski-open dense subset of
\mathcal{U}_{x}\subseteq\Gr_{m,n}
such that
V_{y}
has the expected dimension
d_{y}
for all
y
which are descendants of
x
(including
x
itself). Then
\mathcal{U}_{G,r}
will be equal to
\mathcal{U}_{r}
.

Consider
z_{1},\ldots,z_{n}
as elements of
\bigwedge\mathbb{C}^{m}
. We will also represent the vector spaces
V_{x}
by elements of
\bigwedge\mathbb{C}^{m}
which we will denote by
F_{x}(\mathbf{z})
.

As long as
x
and all of its descendants have the expected dimension, we can use
Lemma

2.17
to express
F_{x}
algebraically using
\wedge
and
*
as follows. We use the notation of
Definition

7.10
, and also write
e_{1},\ldots,e_{m}
for the standard basis of
\mathbb{C}^{m}.

•

If
x=i
,
F_{x}=z_{i}.

•

If
x
is black and
d_{x}=0
then
F_{x}=1,
which represents the trivial vector space. Otherwise

F_{x}=F_{w_{1}}*\dots*F_{w_{p}}.

•

If
x
is white and
d_{x}=d_{b_{1}}+\dots+d_{b_{p}}
, then
F_{x}=F_{b_{1}}\wedge F_{b_{2}}\wedge\cdots\wedge F_{b_{p}}.
Otherwise,
F_{x}=e_{1}\wedge\ldots\wedge e_{m},
which represents the vector space
\mathbb{C}^{m}
.

We will use induction on the number of descendants of
x
. If
x
has no descendants, then
x=i,

d_{x}=1
and
V_{x}=\spn(z_{i})
has expected dimension on the Zariski-open dense subset
\mathcal{U}_{x}
where
z_{i}\neq 0
.

Assume
x
is white. The treatment in black vertices is almost the same, and we omit it.
We assume that the claim holds for
x
’s descendants
b_{1},\ldots,b_{p}
in the rooted tree, meaning that for each
i
there is a Zariski open set
\mathcal{U}_{b_{i}}\in\Gr_{m,n}
in which
\dim V_{y}=d_{y}
for all descendants of
b_{i}
.

Assume first
d_{x}=d_{b_{1}}+\dots+d_{b_{p}}.

Write

\mathcal{U}_{x}:=\left\{\mathbf{z}\in\Gr_{m,n}|\bigwedge_{i=1}^{p}F_{b_{i}}(\mathbf{z})\neq 0\right\}\cap\bigcap_{i=1}^{p}\mathcal{U}_{b_{i}}.

On this set,
\bigwedge_{i=1}^{p}F_{b_{i}}(\mathbf{z})=F_{x}
represents
V_{x}
by
Lemma

2.17
and
\dim V_{x}=d_{x}
. Also, for all descendants
y
of
x
,
\dim V_{y}=d_{y}
.

We now show
\bigwedge_{i=1}^{p}F_{b_{i}}(\mathbf{z})
is not identically
0
as a function from
\Gr_{m,n}
to
\GC(m)
, hence the complement of its zero locus is Zariski dense and
\mathcal{U}_{x}
is open and dense. Pick vector spaces
W_{1},\dots W_{p}
so that
\dim W_{i}=d_{i}
and

\dim(W_{1}+\dots+W_{p})=d_{x}.

We can find
\mathbf{z}\in\Gr_{m,n}
for which
V_{b_{i}}=W_{i}
for
i=1,\ldots,p.
First, let
L_{i}
denote the leaves which are descendants of
b_{i}
, and let
\mathbf{z}_{L_{i}}
denote the submatrix using just columns
L_{i}
. If we act by
g\in GL_{m}
just on
\mathbf{z}_{L_{i}}
and leave the remaining columns untouched to obtain
\mathbf{z}^{\prime}
, then
F_{b_{i}}(\mathbf{z}^{\prime})
represents
gV_{b_{i}}(\mathbf{z})
and
F_{b_{j}}(\mathbf{z}^{\prime})
represents
V_{b_{j}}(\mathbf{z})
for
i\neq j
.

Now, pick an arbitrary
\mathbf{z}\in\bigcap_{i=1}^{p}\mathcal{U}_{b_{i}}.
For
i=1,\ldots,p
, pick
g_{i}\in GL_{m}
with
g_{i}V_{b_{i}}(\mathbf{z})=W_{i}.
Then for each
i
, we replace
\mathbf{z}_{L_{i}}
with
g_{i}\mathbf{z}_{L_{i}}
. Call the resulting element
\mathbf{z}^{\prime}
. We have
V_{b_{i}}(\mathbf{z}^{\prime})=W_{i}
and
W_{i}
is represented by
F_{b_{i}}(\mathbf{z}^{\prime})
. For this
\mathbf{z}^{\prime}
,
\bigwedge_{i=1}^{p}F_{b_{i}}(\mathbf{z}^{\prime})
is nonzero. If
\mathbf{z}^{\prime}
is of full rank, we are done. Otherwise, note that for an open dense subset of
\mathbf{h}=(h_{1},\ldots,h_{p})\in GL_{m}^{p}
it holds that
\dim(h_{1}W_{1}+\dots+h_{p}W_{p})=d_{x}
and
\mathbf{h}\cdot\mathbf{z}^{\prime}
, defined by replacing
\mathbf{z}^{\prime}_{L_{i}}
for
i=1,\ldots,p
in
h_{i}\mathbf{z}^{\prime}_{L_{i}}
, is of full rank. Hence the same argument shows that
\bigwedge_{i=1}^{p}F_{b_{i}}(\mathbf{h}\cdot\mathbf{z}^{\prime})\neq 0.

The case
d_{x}=m<d_{b_{1}}+\dots+d_{b_{p}}
is proven similarly. In this case we put

\mathcal{U}_{x}=\{\mathbf{z}\in\Gr_{m,n}|F_{b_{1}}*(F_{b_{2}}\wedge\dots\wedge F_{b_{p}})\neq 0\}\cap\bigcap_{i=1}^{p}\mathcal{U}_{b_{i}}.

For
\mathbf{z}\in\mathcal{U}_{x}
,
Lemma

2.16
implies that
V_{x}=V_{b_{1}}+\dots+V_{b_{p}}=\mathbb{C}^{m}
and so
V_{x}
has expected dimension. A similar argument as above shows that
F_{b_{1}}*(F_{b_{2}}\wedge\dots\wedge F_{b_{p}})
is not identically zero on
\Gr_{m,n}
, so
\mathcal{U}_{x}
is indeed open and dense.
∎

Corollary 7.12
.

Let
G
be a bipartite plabic tree with boundary
[n]
and black leaves. Then there is an open dense subset
\mathcal{U}_{G}
of
\Gr_{m,n}
such that
\mathbf{z}\in\mathcal{U}_{{G}}
is generic for
G
.

Proof.

Consider the intersection

\bigcap_{r\text{ internal vertex of }G^{\operatorname{ext}}}\mathcal{U}_{G^{\operatorname{ext}},r}

where
\mathcal{U}_{G^{\operatorname{ext}},r}
is the open dense set consisting of
\mathbf{z}
which are generic for
(G^{\operatorname{ext}},r)
, guaranteed to exist by
Proposition

7.11
. This intersection consists of
\mathbf{z}
that are generic for
G
, by definition, and is a finite intersection of open dense subsets, so is open and dense.
∎

We now turn to
(k,m)
-amplitrees, and show that in this case, the dimensions of the subspaces
V_{x}^{\rt=r}
for generic
\mathbf{z}
can be read off of the tree. We will use this to produce VRCs for amplitrees using the Grassmann-Cayley algebra.

Lemma 7.13
.

Let
G
be a
(k,m)
-amplitree,
r
an internal vertex, and let
\mathbf{z}\in\Gr_{m,n}
be generic for
(G,r)
. For a vertex
x\neq r
, let
e
be the edge from
x
to its parent, and let
G^{\prime}
be the connected component of
G\setminus e
containing
x
. Then

(10)

\dim V_{x}=|B_{bd}\cap G^{\prime}|-m\sum_{b\in B_{int}\cap G^{\prime}}(\deg b-2).

Further,
1\leq\dim V_{x}\leq m
and

•

if
x
is white with children
b_{1},\dots,b_{p}
, then
\dim V_{x}=\dim V_{b_{1}}+\dots+\dim V_{b_{p}}
.

•

if
x
is black with children
w_{1},\dots,w_{p}
, then
\dim V_{x}=\dim V_{w_{1}}+\dots+\dim V_{w_{p}}-(p-1)m
.

Proof.

We proceed from leaves to root. Equation (
10
) is true for boundary vertices
x=i
, since in that case
\dim V_{i}=1
and
G^{\prime}
consists of just the boundary vertex
i
.

Now, assume (
10
) holds for the children
u_{1},\dots,u_{p}
of
x
. Say that, if you remove the edge from
u_{i}
to
x
from
G
, the connected component
G^{\prime}_{i}
containing
u_{i}
has
L_{i}
leaves and

\sum_{b\in B_{int}\cap G^{\prime}_{i}}(\deg b-2)=:D_{i}.

We have

\dim V_{u_{1}}+\dots+\dim V_{u_{p}}=L_{1}+\dots+L_{p}-m(D_{1}+\dots+D_{p}).

Suppose
x
is a white vertex. Then
V_{x}=V_{u_{1}}+\dots+V_{u_{p}}
. Since
\mathbf{z}
is generic for
(G,r)
, we have

\dim V_{x}=\min(\dim V_{u_{1}}+\dots+\dim V_{u_{p}},m)=\min(L_{1}+\dots+L_{p}-m(D_{1}+\dots+D_{p}),m).

Now, since
x
is white, we have

L_{1}+\dots+L_{p}-m(D_{1}+\dots+D_{p})=|B_{bd}\cap G^{\prime}|-m\sum_{b\in B_{int}\cap G^{\prime}}(\deg b-2)\leq m

where the final inequality is because
G
is
m
-balanced.
This implies

\dim V_{x}=L_{1}+\dots+L_{p}-m(D_{1}+\dots+D_{p})=|B_{bd}\cap G^{\prime}|-m\sum_{b\in B_{int}\cap G^{\prime}}(\deg b-2)

as desired.

If instead
x
is black, the argument is very similar. We have
V_{x}=V_{u_{1}}\cap\dots\cap V_{u_{p}}
and because of genericity,

\dim V_{x}=\max(0,\dim V_{u_{1}}+\dots+\dim V_{u_{p}}-(p-1)m).

We have

\displaystyle\dim V_{u_{1}}+\dots+\dim V_{u_{p}}-(r-1)m

\displaystyle=L_{1}+\dots+L_{p}-m(D_{1}+\dots+D_{p})-m(\deg x-2)

\displaystyle=|B_{bd}\cap G^{\prime}|-m\sum_{b\in B_{int}\cap G^{\prime}}(\deg b-2)\geq 1

where the last inequality is because
G
is
m
-balanced. So again, (
10
) holds.

The argument above also shows the bound
1\leq\dim V_{x}\leq m
and the equalities

\dim V_{x}=\sum_{i=1}^{p}\dim V_{u_{i}}
if
x
is white and

\dim V_{x}=\sum_{i=1}^{p}\dim V_{u_{i}}-(p-1)m

if
x
is black.
∎

Lemma

7.13
gives the following corollary.

Corollary 7.14
.

Let
G
be a
(k,m)
-amplitree,
r
an internal vertex, and let
\mathbf{z}\in\Gr_{m,n}
be generic for
(G,r)
. If
x\neq r
is white and has children
b_{1},\dots,b_{p}
, then
V_{b_{1}}+\dots+V_{b_{p}}=V_{b_{1}}\oplus\dots\oplus V_{b_{p}}
.

The next lemma shows that for amplitrees, there is only one choice (up to gauge) for the vectors in a VRC.

Lemma 7.15
.

Let
G
be a
(k,m)
-amplitree, let
r
be an internal black vertex, and let
\mathbf{z}\in\Gr_{m,n}
be generic for
(G,r)
. Then the subspace
V_{r}
has dimension
1
.

Proof.

Similar to the proof of
Lemma

7.13
, say the children of
r
are
u_{1},\dots,u_{p}
and let
L_{i},D_{i}
be as in that proof. We have that

\dim V_{r}=\max(0,\dim V_{u_{1}}+\dots+\dim V_{u_{p}}-(p-1)m).

Using
Lemma

7.13
,

\displaystyle\dim V_{u_{1}}+\dots+\dim V_{u_{p}}-(r-1)m

\displaystyle=L_{1}+\dots+L_{p}-m(D_{1}+\dots+D_{p})-m(\deg x-2+1)

\displaystyle=|B_{bd}|-m\sum_{b\in B_{int}}(\deg b-2)-m

\displaystyle=km+1-m(k-1)-m=1

where the second-to-last equality uses
Lemma

7.1
(2) and the fact that a
(k,m)
-amplitree by definition has
km+1
boundary vertices.
∎

With this information about the subspaces
V_{x}
, we can give an explicit
m
-VRC on amplitree
G
with boundary
\mathbf{z}
. The first step is to define elements of the Grassmann-Cayley algebra which will represent the subspaces
V_{x}
.

Definition 7.16
.

Let
G
be a
(k,m)
-amplitree. Choose
r\in B_{int}\cup W
. Orient all edges towards
r
. Moving from leaves to
r
, define a function
F^{\rt=r}_{x}:\Mat_{m,n}\to\GC(m)
for each vertex by the following formulas:

•

if
x
is a boundary vertex
i
,
F^{\rt=r}_{x}(\mathbf{z}):=z_{i}
;

•

if
x
is white with children
b_{1},\dots,b_{p}
reading clockwise from the parent of
x
,
F^{\rt=r}_{x}(\mathbf{z}):=\bigwedge_{i=1}^{p}F_{b_{i}}(\mathbf{z})
;

•

if
x
is black with children
w_{1},\dots,w_{p}
reading clockwise from the parent of
x
,
F^{\rt=r}_{x}(\mathbf{z}):=F_{w_{1}}(\mathbf{z})*\dots*F_{w_{p}}(\mathbf{z})
.

As usual, we drop “
\rt=r
” if the choice of root is clear. We note changing the ordering of the children of
x
will change the sign of
F_{x}(\mathbf{z})
but has no other effect.

Lemma 7.17
.

Let
G
be a
(k,m)
-amplitree and
r\in W\cup B_{int}
be an internal vertex. If
\mathbf{z}\in\Mat_{m,n}
is generic for
(G,r)
, then
F_{x}(\mathbf{z})
represents the subspace
V_{x}
.

In particular, if
r\in B_{int}
, then
F_{r}(\mathbf{z})
is a nonzero vector which can be expressed as a linear combination of
\mathbf{z}
’s columns, whose coefficients are polynomials in the Plücker coordinates of
\mathbf{z}
.

Proof.

For the first statement, we move from leaves to root. For
x
a vertex with children
u_{1},\dots,u_{p}
, assume
F_{u_{i}}
represents
V_{u_{i}}
.
Lemma

7.13
shows that the hypotheses of
Lemma

2.17
hold for the
F_{u_{i}}
. Applying
Lemma

2.17
shows that
F_{x}
represents
V_{x}.

The second statement follows from
Lemma

7.15
and expanding out the shuffles and wedges.
∎

The vectors
F^{\rt=b}_{b}(\mathbf{z})
will be the vectors of the VRC. To define the coefficients, we need the following lemma.

Lemma 7.18
.

Let
\mathbf{z}\in\Mat_{m,n}
be generic for
(k,m)
-amplitree
G
and let
i\in B_{bd}
. Suppose the adjacent white vertex is
w
, and
w
has neighbors
i,b_{1},\dots,b_{p}
. Choose
w
as the root. Then
\bigwedge_{j=1}^{p}F_{b_{j}}(\mathbf{z})\in\bigwedge^{m}\mathbb{C}^{m}\setminus\{0\}
and
\langle\bigwedge_{j=1}^{p}F_{b_{j}}(\mathbf{z})\rangle\in\mathbb{C}^{*}
.

Proof.

Consider
G^{{\operatorname{ext}}}
and choose as the root the black vertex
b
placed between
w
and
i
. Then
F^{\rt=b}_{b_{j}}(\mathbf{z})=F^{\rt=w}_{b_{j}}(\mathbf{z})=F_{b_{j}}(\mathbf{z}).
We also have that
F_{w}^{\rt=b}(\mathbf{z})
is equal to
\bigwedge_{j=1}^{p}F_{b_{j}}(\mathbf{z})
. Because
\mathbf{z}
is generic, the dimension of the subspace represented by
F_{w}^{\rt=b}(\mathbf{z})
is given by (
10
), which is equal to
m
in this case. This implies that
F_{w}^{\rt=b}(\mathbf{z})\in\bigwedge^{m}\mathbb{C}^{m}
and is nonzero. By definition (see
2.19
),
\langle\bigwedge_{j=1}^{p}F_{b_{j}}(\mathbf{z})\rangle
is a scalar, and is nonzero because the wedge is nonzero.
∎

Definition 7.19
.

Let
G
be a
(k,m)
-amplitree and let
\mathbf{z}\in\Mat_{m,n}
. For an edge
e
of
G
, define
f_{e}(\mathbf{z}):=1
if
e
is not adjacent to a boundary vertex. If
e
is adjacent to boundary vertex
i
and white vertex
w
, use
w
as the root and define

f_{e}(\mathbf{z}):=\langle\bigwedge_{j=1}^{p}F_{b_{j}}(\mathbf{z})\rangle

where
b_{1},\dots,b_{j}
are the other neighbors of
w
, reading clockwise from
i
. If
w
is degree at least three, then

f_{e}(\mathbf{z})=F_{b_{1}}*\left(\bigwedge_{j=2}^{p}F_{b_{j}}(\mathbf{z})\right).

Remark 7.20
.

Let
G
be a
(k,m)
-amplitree. Suppose
i\in B_{bd}
is adjacent to a white vertex
w
, and
\deg(w)\geq 3
.
Let
T_{i}
be
G
with the edge
e=(i,w)
removed. Then
T_{i}
is an
s\ell_{m}
-
tensor diagram

[
CKM14
,
FLL19
]
and the associated tensor invariant is, up to sign, equal to
f_{e}(\mathbf{z})
. If
\deg(w)=2
, then
f_{e}(\mathbf{z})
factors into a product of tensor invariants, whose corresponding tensor diagrams are smaller subtrees of
G
.

The next proposition shows that the functions
F^{\rt=b}_{b}(\mathbf{z})
and
f_{e}(\mathbf{z})
give us (up to sign) the vectors and relations of a VRC with boundary
\mathbf{z}
.

Proposition 7.21
.

Suppose
\mathbf{z}=[z_{1}\dots z_{n}]\in\Mat_{m,n}
is generic for
(k,m)
-amplitree
G
. For
i\in B_{bd}
, set
v_{i}:=z_{i}
. For
b\in B_{int}
, set
v_{b}(\mathbf{z}):=F^{\rt=b}_{b}(\mathbf{z})
. For
e
an edge of
G
, set
r_{e}:=f_{e}(\mathbf{z})
. Then there is a choice of signs
\{s_{e}\}\subset\{\pm 1\}^{E(G)}
so that
(\{v_{b}\},\{s_{e}r_{e}\})
is a non-degenerate
m
-VRC with boundary
\mathbf{z}
.

Proof.

Fix a white vertex
w
with neighbors
b_{1},\dots b_{p}
and set
e_{i}=(w,b_{i})
. We check that

\sum_{i=1}^{p}\pm f_{e_{i}}(\mathbf{z})v_{b_{i}}(\mathbf{z})=0.

For the remainder of the proof, we suppress the dependence on
\mathbf{z}
.

Notice that if
b_{i}\in B_{int}
, then

v_{b_{i}}=F^{\rt=b_{i}}_{b_{i}}=(-1)^{c_{i}}F^{\rt=w}_{b_{i}}*(F^{\rt=w}_{b_{1}}\wedge\cdots\widehat{F^{\rt=w}_{b_{i}}}\wedge\cdots\wedge F^{\rt=w}_{b_{p}})

for some integer
c_{i}
. Indeed,
F^{\rt=w}_{b_{i}}
is the shuffle of all
F^{\rt=b_{i}}_{w^{\prime}}
where
w^{\prime}
is a neighbor of
b_{i}
and is not equal to
w.
The other term
\bigwedge_{j\neq i}F^{\rt=w}_{b_{j}}
is equal to
\pm F^{\rt=b_{i}}_{w}
. So the expression above is, up to sign, the shuffle of all
F^{\rt=b_{i}}_{w^{\prime}}
where
w^{\prime}
is a neighbor of
b_{i}
. By definition this is equal to
\pm F^{\rt=b_{i}}_{b_{i}}.

Let
d_{i}
be the integer such that
F^{\rt=w}_{b_{i}}\in\bigwedge^{d_{i}}\mathbb{C}^{m}
. We have

d_{1}+\dots+d_{p}=|B_{bd}|-m\sum_{b\in B_{int}}(\deg b-2)=m+1

where the first equality is from (
10
), and the second is from
Lemma

7.1
(2). So we may apply
Lemma

2.18
to
F^{\rt=w}_{b_{1}},\dots,F^{\rt=w}_{b_{p}}
and obtain

\sum_{i=1}^{p}(-1)^{d_{i}(d_{i}+\dots+d_{p})}(F^{\rt=w}_{b_{1}}\wedge\cdots\widehat{F^{\rt=w}_{b_{i}}}\wedge\cdots\wedge F^{\rt=w}_{b_{p}})*F^{\rt=w}_{b_{i}}=0.

The
i
th term in this relation is exactly
(-1)^{d_{i}(d_{i}+\dots+d_{p})+c_{i}}v_{b_{i}}
if
b_{i}\in B_{int}
. If instead
b_{i}\in B_{bd}
, then
F^{\rt=w}_{b_{i}}=z_{b_{i}}
and

(F^{\rt=w}_{b_{1}}\wedge\cdots\widehat{F^{\rt=w}_{b_{i}}}\wedge\cdots\wedge F^{\rt=w}_{b_{p}})*F^{\rt=w}_{b_{i}}=(F^{\rt=w}_{b_{1}}\wedge\cdots\widehat{F^{\rt=w}_{b_{i}}}\wedge\cdots\wedge F^{\rt=w}_{b_{p}})^{*}~z_{b_{i}}=(-1)^{c_{i}}f_{e_{i}}z_{b_{i}}

for some integer
c_{i}
. In either case, we set
s_{e_{i}}:=(-1)^{{d_{i}(d_{i}+\dots+d_{p})+c_{i}}}
.

In summary, we have

0=\sum_{i=1}^{p}(-1)^{d_{i}(d_{i}+\dots+d_{p})}(F^{\rt=w}_{b_{1}}\wedge\cdots\widehat{F^{\rt=w}_{b_{i}}}\wedge\cdots\wedge F^{\rt=w}_{b_{p}})*F^{\rt=w}_{b_{i}}=\sum_{i=1}^{p}s_{e_{i}}f_{e_{i}}(\mathbf{z})v_{b_{i}}(\mathbf{z})=0

as desired.
∎

The next theorem shows that the
m
-VRC of
Proposition

7.21
represents the unique element of
\mathcal{C}^{\partial=\mathbf{z}}_{G}
.

Theorem 7.22
.

Let
G
be a
(k,m)
-amplitree and let
\mathbf{z}\in\Gr_{m,n}
be generic for
G
. Then there is a unique element of
\mathcal{C}^{m}_{G}
with boundary
\mathbf{z}
.

Proof.

We fix a representative
[z_{1}\dots z_{n}]
of
\mathbf{z}
which we denote also by
\mathbf{z}
. By
Proposition

7.21
, there exists a non-degenerate
m
-VRC
(\mathbf{v},\mathbf{R})
with boundary
\mathbf{z}
. We first show that all other non-degenerate
m
-VRCs with boundary
\mathbf{z}
are related to
(\mathbf{v},\mathbf{R})
by gauge transformations. We then show that there are no degenerate
m
-VRCs with boundary
\mathbf{z}
.

Suppose
(\mathbf{v}^{\prime},\mathbf{R}^{\prime})
is a non-degenerate
m
-VRC with boundary
\mathbf{z}
. By
Lemma

7.9
,
v_{b}^{\prime}
and
v_{b}
lie in the subspace
V_{b}^{\rt=b}
, which is a line by
Lemma

7.15
. So
v_{b}^{\prime}=c_{b}v_{b}
for some nonzero constant
c_{b}
. Using gauge, we may assume
c_{b}=1
for all
b
. Choose a white vertex
w\in W
. Say its neighbors are
b_{1},\dots,b_{p}
, and let
e_{i}
denote the edge
(w,b_{i})
. We have

v_{b_{1}}=-\frac{1}{r_{e_{1}}}\left(\sum_{i=2}^{p}r_{e_{i}}v_{b_{i}}\right)=v_{b_{1}}^{\prime}=-\frac{1}{r^{\prime}_{e_{1}}}\left(\sum_{i=2}^{p}r^{\prime}_{e_{i}}v^{\prime}_{b_{i}}\right)=-\frac{1}{r^{\prime}_{e_{1}}}\left(\sum_{i=2}^{p}r^{\prime}_{e_{i}}v_{b_{i}}\right)

and so

0=\sum_{i=2}^{p}\left(\frac{r_{e_{i}}}{r_{e_{1}}}-\frac{r^{\prime}_{e_{i}}}{r^{\prime}_{e_{1}}}\right)v_{b_{i}}.

Notice that, by
Lemma

7.9
,
v_{b_{i}}\in V_{b_{i}}^{\rt=w}
, and by definition,
V_{b_{i}}^{\rt=w}=V_{b_{i}}^{\rt=b_{1}}
. By
Corollary

7.14
,

V_{b_{2}}^{\rt=b_{1}}+\dots+V_{b_{p}}^{\rt=b_{1}}=V_{b_{2}}^{\rt=b_{1}}\oplus\dots\oplus V_{b_{p}}^{\rt=b_{1}}

and so in particular,
v_{b_{2}},\dots,v_{b_{p}}
are linearly independent. This implies that for
i=2,\dots,p

\frac{r_{e_{i}}}{r_{e_{1}}}=\frac{r^{\prime}_{e_{i}}}{r^{\prime}_{e_{1}}}

which implies that the coefficients around
w
are related by a gauge transformation.

Now suppose
(\mathbf{v}^{\prime},\mathbf{R}^{\prime})
is a degenerate
m
-VRC with boundary
\mathbf{z}
; we will arrive at a contradiction. There is a vector
v_{b_{1}}
which is equal to zero. Say it is adjacent to white vertex
w
, whose other neighbors are
b_{2},\dots,b_{p}
. As above, the vector
v_{b_{i}}\in V_{b_{i}}^{\rt=b_{1}}
and

V_{b_{2}}^{\rt=b_{1}}+\dots+V_{b_{p}}^{\rt=b_{1}}=V_{b_{2}}^{\rt=b_{1}}\oplus\dots\oplus V_{b_{p}}^{\rt=b_{1}}

so if they are nonzero, they are linearly independent. Since a linear combination of the
v_{b_{i}}
with positive coefficients is equal to
v_{b_{1}}=0
, the only possibility is that
v_{b_{i}}=0
for
i=2,\dots,p
as well. Continuing this argument, we eventually obtain that some boundary vector is
0
, a contradiction of
\mathbf{z}
being generic.

∎

Theorem 7.23
.

Let
G
be a bipartite plabic tree of type
(k,km+1)
. Then

\operatorname{IN}_{m}(G)=1

if and only if
G
is
m
-balanced. If
G
is not
m
-balanced, then
\operatorname{IN}_{m}(G)=0
.

Proof of
Theorem

7.23
.

Suppose that
G
is a bipartite plabic tree of type

(k,km+1)
but it does not satisfy the
m
-balanced
property. Then by
Corollary

7.7
,

\Pi_{G}
has
m
-intersection number
0
.

Conversely, suppose that
G
is a bipartite plabic tree of
type
(k,km+1)
which satisfies the
m
-balanced property.
Then by
Theorem

7.22
,
for generic
\mathbf{z}\in\Gr_{m,n}
, there is a unique element of
\mathcal{C}^{m}_{G}
with boundary
\mathbf{z}
.
But now by
Corollary

6.10
,

\Pi_{G}
has intersection number
1
.
∎

Theorem

7.23
shows that in the case of trees,
6.6
holds: that is, the intersection number is
0
if and only if we can find a
subgraph as in
Proposition

6.5
.

7.3.
Enumeration of amplitrees

Recall from
Definition

7.2

that a
(k,m)
-amplitree is a bipartite plabic tree
G

of type
(k,km+1)
which is
m
-balanced.
In this section we give some enumerative results about move-equivalence classes of amplitrees. Since an amplitree is in particular a tree, the only moves that we can apply to an amplitree are the expand and contract moves, as in
Figure

1
.

Table

1
gives computational data about the enumeration of amplitrees of type
(k,m)
.

1

2

3

4

5

1

1

1

1

1

1

2

1

5

35

285

2530

3

1

14

280

6565

160916

4

1

30

1274

63410

5

1

55

4228

380310

6

1

91

11438

Table 1.
The number of (move-equivalence classes of) amplitrees of type
(k,m)
.

We also have the following results.

Theorem 7.24
.

When
k=2
, the number
c_{k,m}
of move-equivalence classes of amplitrees of type
(k,m)
is

c_{2,m}=1^{2}+2^{2}+\dots+m^{2}=\frac{m(m+1)(2m+1)}{6}.

This is the sequence of
square pyramidal numbers
, and it appears as sequence A000330 in
[
S+
]
. We can write the generating function as

(11)

\sum_{m\geq 0}c_{2,m}x^{m}=\frac{1+x}{(1-x)^{4}}.

Proof.

Each move-equivalence class of a
(2,m)
-amplitree can be represented by a tree with
2m+1
boundary vertices (leaves), with a single trivalent internal black vertex connected to three internal white vertices, where each white vertex is connected to
\ell
boundary vertices for some
1\leq\ell\leq m
.

Let
d_{0}+d_{1}+1
be the number of boundary vertices adjacent to the white vertex which is closest to the boundary vertex
1
; let
d_{0}
and
d_{1}
denote the number of boundary vertices which are counterclockwise and clockwise of the
1
. Let
d_{2}
and
d_{3}
denote the number of boundary vertices which are incident to the other two white vertices.
Thus we must have that
1\leq d_{0}+d_{1}\leq m
,
1\leq d_{2}\leq m
, and
1\leq d_{3}\leq m
, and
1+d_{0}+d_{1}+d_{2}+d_{3}=2m+1
.

If we let
f_{m}:=c_{2,m}
be the number of move-equivalence classes of
(2,m)
-amplitrees, then
we claim that

(12)

f_{m}=5f_{m-1}-10f_{m-2}+10f_{m-3}-5f_{m-4}+f_{m-5}.

To prove the claim, note that to get such an amplitree on
2m+1
leaves from an amplitree on
2m-1
leaves, we can increase
d_{i}
and
d_{j}
by 1 where
(i,j)\in\{(0,2),(0,3),(1,2),(1,3),(2,3)\}
.
However, any two of these operations commute, so e.g.
increasing
d_{0}
and
d_{2}
then increasing
d_{1}
and
d_{3}
results in the same tree as
increasing
d_{1}
and
d_{3}
, and then increasing
d_{0}
and
d_{2}
.
Thus by inclusion-exclusion, (
12
) holds.

Since (
12
) is a linear recurrence, it follows that
f_{m}
is a polynomial in
m
of degree at most
4
. Computation of the initial terms results in the formula (
11
).
∎

The same style of argument can be used to prove the following result.

Theorem 7.25
.

When
k=3
, the number
c_{k,m}
of move-equivalence classes of amplitrees of type
(k,m)
has generating function given by

\sum_{m\geq 0}c_{3,m}x^{m}=\frac{1+28x+56x^{2}+14x^{3}}{(1-x)^{7}}.

Similarly, for each fixed
k
we expect the number of
(k,m)
-amplitrees to satisfy a generating function of the form
p(x)/(1-x)^{3k-2}
where
p(x)
is a polynomial of degree at most
3k-2
. This implies that
c_{k,m}=\Theta(m^{3k-3})
as
m
tends to infinity, where the implied constants depend on
k
. If
m\geq 2
is fixed and
k
tends to infinity, then the number of
(k,m)
-amplitrees is exponential in
k
. Indeed, an exponential lower bound can be shown by considering different plane embeddings of trees isomorphic to chain trees, cf
Definition

5.7
, while an exponential upper bound holds for all trees in the plane in general. It remains an intriguing problem to determine how the basis of the exponent depends on
m
, or any further information about the joint asymptotic behavior of
c_{k,m}
when both
k
and
m
are large. One could also hope to compute an explicit two-variable generating function keeping track of both
k
and
m
.

8.
Proofs of the quasi-cluster homomorphism property

In this section, we provide the proofs that the promotions from
Section

5
are quasi-cluster homomorphisms.

8.1.
Star promotion

Recall
Definition

5.3
of unary star promotion.

Theorem 8.1
.

Unary star promotion
\Psi_{m}
is a quasi-homomorphism of cluster algebras from
\mathcal{A}(\Sigma)
to
\mathcal{A}(\overline{\Sigma})
, where
\Sigma
is the rectangles seed for
{\Gr}_{4,N^{\prime}}
and
\overline{\Sigma}
is the rectangles seed for
{\Gr}_{4,n}
with the cluster variables in the leftmost column frozen, as shown in
8.2
.

Notation 8.2
.

The initial seed
\Sigma
is the rectangles seed
for
\Gr_{m,\{1,3,\dots,n\}}
(see
Section

2.2
), shown below for
m=5
:

\langle 13456\rangle
\langle 13457\rangle
\langle 13458\rangle
\langle 13459\rangle
\cdots
\langle 1345n\rangle
\langle 13467\rangle
\langle 13478\rangle
\langle 13489\rangle
\cdots
\langle 134(n{-}1)n\rangle
\langle 13567\rangle
\langle 13678\rangle
\langle 13789\rangle
\cdots
\langle 13(n{-}2)(n{-}1)n\rangle
\langle 14567\rangle
\langle 15678\rangle
\langle 16789\rangle
\cdots
\langle 1(n{-}3)(n{-}2)(n{-}1)n\rangle
\langle 34567\rangle
\langle 45678\rangle
\langle 56789\rangle
\cdots
\langle(n{-}4)(n{-}3)(n{-}2)(n{-}1)n\rangle
\Sigma

The seed
\overline{\Sigma}
is the following rectangles seed for
\Gr_{m,n}
with the cluster variables in the leftmost column frozen, also shown for
m=5
:

\langle 12345\rangle

\langle 12346\rangle

\langle 12347\rangle
\langle 12348\rangle
\langle 12349\rangle
\cdots
\langle 1234n\rangle

\langle 12356\rangle

\langle 12367\rangle
\langle 12378\rangle
\langle 12389\rangle
\cdots
\langle 123(n{-}1)n\rangle

\langle 12456\rangle

\langle 12567\rangle
\langle 12678\rangle
\langle 12789\rangle
\cdots
\langle 12(n{-}2)(n{-}1)n\rangle

\langle 13456\rangle

\langle 14567\rangle
\langle 15678\rangle
\langle 16789\rangle
\cdots
\langle 1(n{-}3)(n{-}2)(n{-}1)n\rangle
\langle 23456\rangle
\langle 34567\rangle
\langle 45678\rangle
\langle 56789\rangle
\cdots
\langle(n{-}4)(n{-}3)(n{-}2)(n{-}1)n\rangle
\overline{\Sigma}

In both seeds, we label the rows
1
to
m
from top to bottom, and label the columns
m+1,\dots,n
from left to right, such that each column is labeled by the largest index that appears in it.
We let
x_{rc}
, respectively
\overline{x}_{rc}
denote
the mutable cluster variable of
\Sigma
, respectively
\overline{\Sigma}
in row
r
and column
c
.

We note the following corollary of
Theorem

8.1
.

Corollary 8.3
.

Let
A,B,C
be disjoint ordered sets in
[n]
,
i.e.
a<b<c
for each
a\in A
,
b\in B
, and
c\in C.

Suppose moreover that
|C|=m-1
, and that

|A|+|B|+|C|=2m
. Then the chain polynomial

\langle A*B*C\rangle
is a cluster variable for
\Gr_{m,n}
.

Proof of
Corollary

8.3
.

Let
\overline{\Psi}_{m}
be the
reduced unary star promotion map
, defined the same way as

\Psi_{m}
in
Definition

5.3
but without a denominator. First let
C=\{n-m+2,n-m+3,\dots,n\}.
Then it follows from
Remark

8.5
below that for
3\leq j\leq m
,

\displaystyle\overline{\Psi}_{m}(\langle j,C\rangle)

\displaystyle\;=\;\langle 1,\dots,\widehat{j{-}1},\dots,m{+}1\rangle\langle j{-}1,C\rangle-\langle 1,\dots,\widehat{j{-}2},\dots,m{+}1\rangle\langle j{-}2,C\rangle

\displaystyle\quad\quad\quad\quad+\langle 1,\dots,\widehat{j{-}3},\dots,m{+}1\rangle\langle j{-}3,C\rangle-\;\dots\;\pm\langle 2,\dots,m{+}1\rangle\langle 1,C\rangle

\displaystyle\;=\;\langle(1,2,\dots,j{-}1)*(j,j{+}1,\dots,m{+}1)*C\rangle

Now we know that “spreading out indices”
[
ELP+23
, Lemma 4.20]
preserves the property of being a cluster variable, so the corollary follows.
∎

Remark 8.4
.

Also applying a cyclic shift
[
ELP+23
, Corollary 4.18]
preserves the property of being a cluster variable, up to a sign. Hence, for any cyclically ordered disjoint sets
A,B,C
with
|C|=m-1
and
|A|+|B|=m+1
,
we have that
(-1)^{r(m-r)}\langle A*B*C\rangle
is a cluster variable, where
r
is the number of indices in
A,B,C
which appear to the left of 1.

Remark 8.5
.

In what follows, we use the fact that
for
3\leq j\leq m
, the substitution in
Definition

5.3
of
unary star promotion
can also be written via Plücker relations in the form

j\;\mapsto\;\frac{\langle 1,\dots,\widehat{j{-}1},\dots,m{+}1\rangle}{\langle 1,\dots,\widehat{j},\dots,m{+}1\rangle}(j-1)+\diamond(j-2)+\diamond(j-3)+\dots+\diamond(1),

where
\diamond
represents similar rational expressions in Plücker coordinates.

Proof of
Theorem

8.1
.

For concreteness we explain the proof in detail for
m=5
, then indicate how the formulas generalize for arbitrary
m
.

We first compute the images of the cluster variables in the initial seed under
\Psi_{5}
. It is not hard to see that
\Psi_{5}
fixes the cluster variables in rows
4
and
5
of the seed
\Sigma
in
8.2
, as well as the cluster variable
\langle 13456\rangle
in the top-left corner of
\Sigma
. In order to apply
\Psi_{5}
to the remaining cluster variables, we use
Remark

8.5
, which in this case
says that

\displaystyle 3

\displaystyle\;\mapsto\;\frac{\langle 13456\rangle}{\langle 12456\rangle}(2)+\diamond(1)

\displaystyle 4

\displaystyle\;\mapsto\;\frac{\langle 12456\rangle}{\langle 12356\rangle}(3)+\diamond(2)+\diamond(1)

\displaystyle 5

\displaystyle\;\mapsto\;\frac{\langle 12356\rangle}{\langle 12346\rangle}(4)+\diamond(3)+\diamond(2)+\diamond(1)

Applying the above formulas to the cluster variables in rows
3
,
2
, and
1
, respectively,
we get

\displaystyle\Psi_{5}(\langle 1,3,i,i+1,i+2\rangle)\;=\;\frac{\langle 13456\rangle}{\langle 12456\rangle}\cdot\langle 1,2,i,i+1,i+2\rangle\;=\;F_{3}\cdot\langle 1,2,i,i+1,i+2\rangle

\displaystyle\Psi_{5}(\langle 1,3,4,i,i+1\rangle)\;=\;\frac{\langle 13456\rangle}{\langle 12356\rangle}\cdot\langle 1,2,3,i,i+1\rangle\;=\;F_{2}\cdot\langle 1,2,3,i,i+1\rangle

\displaystyle\Psi_{5}(\langle 1,3,4,5,i\rangle)\;=\;\frac{\langle 13456\rangle}{\langle 12346\rangle}\cdot\langle 1,2,3,4,i\rangle\;=\;F_{1}\cdot\langle 1,2,3,4,i\rangle

where

F_{1}\;=\;\frac{\langle 13456\rangle}{\langle 12346\rangle}\;\;\;\;\;\;\;\;F_{2}\;=\;\frac{\langle 13456\rangle}{\langle 12356\rangle}\;\;\;\;\;\;\;\;F_{3}\;=\;\frac{\langle 13456\rangle}{\langle 12456\rangle}\;\;\;\;\;\;\;\;F_{4}\;=\;1

are the “frozen factors” corresponding to the four rows. To summarize, for
x_{rc}
and
\bar{x}_{rc}
, respectively the cluster variable of
\Sigma
and
\overline{\Sigma}
as in
8.2
, our calculations show that

\Psi_{5}(x_{rc})=F_{r}\cdot\bar{x}_{rc}
and hence
\Psi_{5}(x_{rc})\propto\bar{x}_{rc}
.

Now we check exchange ratios. The exchange ratio for the mutable cluster variable
x_{1i}=\langle 1345i\rangle
in row
1
of
\Sigma
is

\hat{y}_{\Sigma}(x_{1i})\;=\;\frac{\langle 1345(i{+}1)\rangle\,\langle 134(i{-}1)i\rangle}{\langle 1345(i{-}1)\rangle\,\langle 134i(i{+}1)\rangle}

and its image under
\Psi_{5}
is

\Psi_{5}\left(\hat{y}_{\Sigma}(x_{1i})\right)\;=\;\frac{\langle 1234(i{+}1)\rangle\,\langle 123(i{-}1)i\rangle}{\langle 1234(i{-}1)\rangle\,\langle 123i(i{+}1)\rangle}

But now observe that
\Psi(\hat{y}_{\Sigma}(x_{1i}))
is exactly the exchange ratio

\hat{y}_{\overline{\Sigma}}(\bar{x}_{1i})
. Similarly, the exchange ratio for the mutable cluster variable

x_{2i}=\langle 134(i{-}1)i\rangle
in row
2
of
\Sigma
is

\hat{y}_{\Sigma}(x_{2i})\;=\;\frac{\langle 1345(i{-}1)\rangle\,\langle 134i(i{+}1)\rangle\,\langle 13(i{-}2)(i{-}1)i\rangle}{\langle 1345i\rangle\,\langle 134(i{-}2)(i{-}1)\rangle\,\langle 13(i{-}1)i(i{+}1)\rangle}

and its image under
\Psi_{5}
is

\Psi_{5}\left(\hat{y}_{\Sigma}(x_{2i})\right)\;=\;\frac{\langle 1234(i{-}1)\rangle\,\langle 123i(i{+}1)\rangle\,\langle 12(i{-}2)(i{-}1)i\rangle}{\langle 1234i\rangle\,\langle 123(i{-}2)(i{-}1)\rangle\,\langle 12(i{-}1)i(i{+}1)\rangle}

which equals the exchange ratio
\hat{y}_{\overline{\Sigma}}(\bar{x}_{2i})
just as before. The computations of the exchange ratios and their images for the mutable cluster variables in row
3
and row
4
are similar.
We have now verified that properties (1) and (2) in
Definition

2.11
are satisfied by our map
\Psi_{5}
.

The proof in the general case is a straightforward extension of the
m=5
case. We
again use for
\Sigma
the rectangles seed, where the rows are labeled from
1
to
m
from top to bottom, and the columns are labeled from
m+1
to
n
from left to right, and the cluster variable
x_{rc}
in row
r
and column
c
is given by

x_{rc}\;=\;\begin{cases}\langle 1,3,4,\dots,m{+}1{-}r,\;c{-}r{+}1,\dots,c{-}1,c\rangle&\text{ for }1\leq r\leq m-1\\
\langle c{-}m{+}1,c{-}m{+}2,\dots,c{-}1,c\rangle&\text{ for }r=m\end{cases}

We again find that
\Psi_{m}
fixes the cluster variables in rows
m-1
and
m
,
and that

\Psi_{m}(\langle 1,3,4,\dots,m{+}1{-}r,\;c{-}r{+}1,\dots,c{-}1,c\rangle\;\propto\;\langle 1,2,3,4,\dots,m{-}r,\;c{-}r{+}1,\dots,c{-}1,c\rangle

It is straightforward to check that the exchange ratios satisfy
property (2) of
Definition

2.11
.
∎

Remark 8.6
.

Our unary star promotion map is essentially
the same as the
a=n-k
“splicing” map from
[
GS24
]
, which was developed independently. The splicing map is also shown there to be a quasi-cluster homomorphism.

8.2.
Spurion promotion

In this section we show that the map
\Psi_{sp}
from
Definition

5.5
is a quasi-cluster homomorphism. Recall that
N^{\prime}=\{1,2,7,8,\dots,n\}
.

Theorem 8.7
.

Unary spurion promotion
\Psi_{sp}

is a quasi-cluster homomorphism
from

\mathcal{A}(\Sigma)
to

\mathcal{A}(\overline{\Sigma})
, where
\Sigma
is a seed for
{\Gr}_{4,N^{\prime}}
and
\overline{\Sigma}
is the seed for
{\Gr}_{4,n}
with some variables frozen, shown in
8.8
.

Notation 8.8
.

In the proof of
Theorem

8.7
we derive the following seed
\overline{\Sigma}
, a freezing of a seed for
\Gr_{4,n}
. The boxed variables are frozen; those in dashed boxes are mutable in the
{\Gr}_{4,n}
seed. As in
Definition

5.5
, the chain polynomial
F_{i}
is obtained by formally deleting the index
i
from the expression
\langle 123*456*789\rangle
. For indices after
9
we use
\mathrm{A},\mathrm{B},\mathrm{C},\dots
as shorthand for
10,11,12,\dots
respectively.

\langle 1237\rangle

\langle 1239\rangle
\langle 123\mathrm{A}\rangle
\langle 123\mathrm{B}\rangle
\cdots
\langle 123n\rangle
\langle 1289\rangle
\langle 129\mathrm{A}\rangle
\langle 12\mathrm{A}\mathrm{B}\rangle
\cdots
\langle 12(n{-}1)n\rangle
\langle 1789\rangle
\langle 189\mathrm{A}\rangle
\langle 19\mathrm{A}\mathrm{B}\rangle
\cdots
\langle 1(n{-}2)(n{-}1)n\rangle

\langle 3789\rangle

\langle 789\mathrm{A}\rangle
\langle 89\mathrm{A}\mathrm{B}\rangle
\cdots
\langle(n{-}3)(n{-}2)(n{-}1)n\rangle

F_{8}

F_{7}

F_{3}

F_{2}

\langle 1234\rangle
\langle 2345\rangle
