\overline{\Sigma}

Notation 8.9
.

[Seed
\Sigma
for
\Gr_{4,N^{\prime}}
]
Let
\Sigma
be the following rectangles seed for
{\Gr}_{4,N^{\prime}}
. This is similar to the rectangles seeds appearing in proof for the star promotion.
\langle 1278\rangle
\langle 1279\rangle
\langle 127\mathrm{A}\rangle
\langle 127\mathrm{B}\rangle
\langle 127\mathrm{C}\rangle
\cdots
\langle 127n\rangle
\langle 1289\rangle
\langle 129\mathrm{A}\rangle
\langle 12\mathrm{A}\mathrm{B}\rangle
\langle 12\mathrm{B}\mathrm{C}\rangle
\cdots
\langle 12(n{-}1)n\rangle
\langle 1789\rangle
\langle 189\mathrm{A}\rangle
\langle 19\mathrm{A}\mathrm{B}\rangle
\langle 1\mathrm{A}\mathrm{B}\mathrm{C}\rangle
\cdots
\langle 1(n{-}2)(n{-}1)n\rangle
\langle 2789\rangle
\langle 789\mathrm{A}\rangle
\langle 89\mathrm{A}\mathrm{B}\rangle
\langle 9\mathrm{A}\mathrm{B}\mathrm{C}\rangle
\cdots
\langle(n{-}3)(n{-}2)(n{-}1)n\rangle
\Sigma

Note that the mutable variables of both
\overline{\Sigma}
and
\Sigma
are in a
3\times(n-9)
grid. This gives a natural bijection between the mutable variables of the two seeds, which we use in the next proposition.

Lemma 8.10
.

The mutable variables in
\overline{\Sigma}
are proportional to the corresponding mutable variables in
\Psi_{sp}(\Sigma)
.

Proof.

We apply the map
\Psi_{sp}
to the variables in
\Sigma
, and substitute the vectors
2
,
7
, and
8
using the formulas in
Remark

5.6
.

The variables
\langle 19\mathrm{A}\mathrm{B}\rangle
,
\langle 9\mathrm{A}\mathrm{B}\mathrm{C}\rangle
and everything to their right in the third and fourth rows of both seeds are clearly fixed by
\Psi_{sp}
. The variable
\langle 89\mathrm{A}\mathrm{B}\rangle
is also fixed, since
8\mapsto 8-\tfrac{F_{9}}{F_{8}}9
but
\langle 99\mathrm{A}\mathrm{B}\rangle=0
, and similarly
\langle 189\mathrm{A}\rangle
is fixed as well. Also
\langle 129\mathrm{A}\rangle
is fixed since
2\mapsto 2-\tfrac{F_{1}}{F_{2}}1
and
\langle 119\mathrm{A}\rangle=0
, and same for all variables to its right of the form
\langle 12ij\rangle
in the second row of both seeds. The variable
\langle 1289\rangle
is also fixed by combining the two previous arguments for
12
and
89
. Similarly, the variables
\langle 789\mathrm{A}\rangle
and
\langle 1789\rangle
are fixed by combining the substitution rules
7\mapsto 7-\tfrac{F_{8}}{F_{7}}8+\tfrac{F_{9}}{F_{7}}9
and
8\mapsto 8-\tfrac{F_{9}}{F_{8}}9
.

The remaining variables are
\langle 1278\rangle
,
\langle 2789\rangle
, and all variables of the form
\langle 127i\rangle
for
i\geq 9
in the first row of
\Sigma
. We compute the images of these variables under
\Psi_{sp}
using
Remark

5.6
:

\displaystyle\Psi_{sp}\big(\langle 127i\rangle\big)\;

\displaystyle=\;\left\langle 1\left(2-\tfrac{F_{1}}{F_{2}}1\right)\left(\tfrac{F_{1}}{F_{7}}1-\tfrac{F_{2}}{F_{7}}2+\tfrac{F_{3}}{F_{7}}3\right)i\right\rangle\;=\;\frac{F_{3}}{F_{7}}\langle 123i\rangle

\displaystyle\Psi_{sp}\big(\langle 1278\rangle\big)\;

\displaystyle=\;\left\langle 1\left(2-\tfrac{F_{1}}{F_{2}}1\right)\left(\tfrac{F_{1}}{F_{7}}1-\tfrac{F_{2}}{F_{7}}2+\tfrac{F_{3}}{F_{7}}3\right)\left(\tfrac{F_{7}}{F_{8}}7-\tfrac{F_{3}}{F_{8}}3+\tfrac{F_{2}}{F_{8}}2-\tfrac{F_{1}}{F_{8}}1\right)\right\rangle\;=\;\frac{F_{3}}{F_{8}}\langle 1237\rangle

\displaystyle\Psi_{sp}\big(\langle 2789\rangle\big)\;

\displaystyle=\;\left\langle\left(\tfrac{F_{3}}{F_{2}}3-\tfrac{F_{7}}{F_{2}}7+\tfrac{F_{8}}{F_{2}}8-\tfrac{F_{9}}{F_{2}}9\right)\left(7-\tfrac{F_{8}}{F_{7}}8+\tfrac{F_{9}}{F_{7}}9\right)\left(8-\tfrac{F_{9}}{F_{8}}9\right)9\right\rangle\;=\;\frac{F_{3}}{F_{2}}\langle 3789\rangle

∎

Lemma 8.11
.

Applying
\Psi_{sp}
to the exchange ratio of a mutable variable in
\Sigma
gives the exchange ratio of the corresponding mutable variables in
\overline{\Sigma}
.

Proof.

If
n=9
then there are no mutable variables in
\Sigma
, so suppose
n\geq 10
. By the proof of the previous lemma, the images of mutable variables of the form
\langle 127i\rangle
in
\Sigma
are
\Psi_{sp}(\langle 127i\rangle)=\tfrac{F_{3}}{F_{7}}\langle 123i\rangle\propto\langle 123i\rangle
in
\overline{\Sigma}
, since
F_{3}
and
F_{7}
are frozen. The other mutable variables in
\Sigma
map under
\Psi_{sp}
to identical mutable variables in
\overline{\Sigma}
without frozen factors.

We verify that the exchange ratios of mutable variables in
\Sigma
map to the exchange ratio of their proportional mutable images. For the three variables in the leftmost column, applying
Definition

2.10
and using the computations from the proof of the previous lemma, we obtain:

\displaystyle\Psi_{sp}\left(\hat{y}_{\Sigma}\big(\langle 1279\rangle\big)\right)\;

\displaystyle=\;\Psi_{sp}\left(\frac{\langle 127\mathrm{A}\rangle\langle 1289\rangle}{\langle 1278\rangle\langle 129\mathrm{A}\rangle}\right)\;=\;\frac{\tfrac{F_{3}}{F_{7}}\langle 123\mathrm{A}\rangle\langle 1289\rangle}{\tfrac{F_{3}}{F_{8}}\langle 1237\rangle\langle 129A\rangle}\;=\;\hat{y}_{\overline{\Sigma}}\big(\langle 1239\rangle\big)

\displaystyle\Psi_{sp}\left(\hat{y}_{\Sigma}\big(\langle 1289\rangle\big)\right)\;

\displaystyle=\;\Psi_{sp}\left(\frac{\langle 129\mathrm{A}\rangle\langle 1789\rangle}{\langle 1279\rangle\langle 189\mathrm{A}\rangle}\right)\;=\;\frac{\langle 129\mathrm{A}\rangle\langle 1789\rangle}{\tfrac{F_{3}}{F_{7}}\langle 1239\rangle\langle 189A\rangle}\;=\;\hat{y}_{\overline{\Sigma}}\big(\langle 1289\rangle\big)

\displaystyle\Psi_{sp}\left(\hat{y}_{\Sigma}\big(\langle 1789\rangle\big)\right)\;

\displaystyle=\;\Psi_{sp}\left(\frac{\langle 189\mathrm{A}\rangle\langle 2789\rangle}{\langle 1289\rangle\langle 789\mathrm{A}\rangle}\right)\;=\;\frac{\langle 189\mathrm{A}\rangle\tfrac{F_{3}}{F_{2}}\langle 3789\rangle}{\langle 1289\rangle\langle 789A\rangle}\;=\;\hat{y}_{\overline{\Sigma}}\big(\langle 1789\rangle\big)

For the remaining mutable variables of
\Sigma
in the first row,
\langle 127i\rangle
for
9<i<n
, we similarly verify:

\Psi_{sp}\left(\hat{y}_{\Sigma}\big(\langle 127i\rangle\big)\right)\;=\;\Psi_{sp}\left(\frac{\langle 127(i{+}1)\rangle\langle 12(i{-}1)i\rangle}{\langle 127(i{-}1)\rangle\langle 12i(i{+}1)\rangle}\right)\;=\;\frac{\tfrac{F_{3}}{F_{7}}\langle 123(i{+}1)\rangle\langle 12(i{-}1)i\rangle}{\tfrac{F_{3}}{F_{7}}\langle 123(i{-}1)\rangle\langle 12i(i{+}1)\rangle}\;=\;\hat{y}_{\overline{\Sigma}}\big(\langle 123i\rangle\big)

Also for the remaining mutables in the second row of both quivers,
\langle 12(i{-}1)i\rangle
for
9<i<n
:

\displaystyle\Psi_{sp}\left(\hat{y}_{\Sigma}\big(\langle 12(i{-}1)i\rangle\big)\right)\;

\displaystyle=\;\Psi_{sp}\left(\frac{\langle 12i(i{+}1)\rangle\,\langle 1(i{-}2)(i{-}1)i\rangle\,\langle 127(i{-}1)\rangle}{\langle 127i\rangle\,\langle 12(i{-}2)(i{-}1)\rangle\,\langle 1(i{-}1)i(i{+}1)\rangle}\right)

\displaystyle=\;\frac{\langle 12i(i{+}1)\rangle\,\langle 1(i{-}2)(i{-}1)i\rangle\,\tfrac{F_{3}}{F_{7}}\langle 123(i{-}1)\rangle}{\tfrac{F_{3}}{F_{7}}\langle 123i\rangle\,\langle 12(i{-}2)(i{-}1)\rangle\,\langle 1(i{-}1)i(i{+}1)\rangle}\;=\;\hat{y}_{\overline{\Sigma}}\big(\langle 12(i{-}1)i\rangle\big)

Finally, the verification for mutable variables of the form
\langle 1(i{-}2)(i{-}1)i\rangle
for
9<i<n
appearing in the third row of both quivers is omitted, because these variables and their six neighbors are all fixed by
\Psi_{sp}
, as shown in the proof of
Lemma

8.10
.
∎

Lemma 8.12
.

Let
\overline{\overline{\Sigma}}
be the seed obtained from
\overline{\Sigma}
by unfreezing the twelve dashed variables in
8.8
. Then
\overline{\overline{\Sigma}}
is a seed for
{\Gr}_{4,n}
.

Proof.

We present a sequence of mutations that goes from a rectangles seed for
\Gr_{4,n}
to
\overline{\overline{\Sigma}}
. We start from the following rectangles seed, similar the ones used above:
\langle 1234\rangle
\langle 1235\rangle
\langle 1236\rangle
\langle 1237\rangle
\langle 1238\rangle
\langle 1239\rangle
\cdots
\langle 123n\rangle
\langle 1245\rangle
\langle 1256\rangle
\langle 1267\rangle
\langle 1278\rangle
\langle 1289\rangle
\cdots
\langle 12(n{-}1)n\rangle
\langle 1345\rangle
\langle 1456\rangle
\langle 1567\rangle
\langle 1678\rangle
\langle 1789\rangle
\cdots
\langle 1(n{-}2)(n{-}1)n\rangle
\langle 2345\rangle
\langle 3456\rangle
\langle 4567\rangle
\langle 5678\rangle
\langle 6789\rangle
\cdots
\langle(n{-}3)(n{-}2)(n{-}1)n\rangle
\tilde{\Sigma}

We then apply the following sequence of
11
mutations. For each mutation in this list, we give the variable before mutation, the expression for the variable after mutation using the exchange relation in the seed, and a polynomial-in-Plückers expression for the variable after mutation.

(a)

\displaystyle\langle 1678\rangle\;\mapsto\;\frac{\langle 1278\rangle\langle 1567\rangle\langle 6789\rangle+\langle 1267\rangle\langle 1789\rangle\langle 5678\rangle}{\langle 1678\rangle}\;=\;\langle 12*567*789\rangle

(b)

\displaystyle\langle 1567\rangle\;\mapsto\;\frac{\langle 1456\rangle\langle 12*567*789\rangle+\langle 1256\rangle\langle 1789\rangle\langle 4567\rangle}{\langle 1567\rangle}\;=\;\langle 12*456*789\rangle\;=\;F_{3}

(c)

\displaystyle\langle 1267\rangle\;\mapsto\;\frac{\langle 1237\rangle\langle 1256\rangle\langle 6789\rangle+\langle 1236\rangle\langle 12*567*789\rangle}{\langle 1267\rangle}\;=\;\langle 123*56*789\rangle\;=\;F_{4}

(d)

\displaystyle\langle 1256\rangle\;\mapsto\;\frac{\langle 1245\rangle\langle 123*56*789\rangle+\langle 1235\rangle\langle 12*456*789\rangle}{\langle 1256\rangle}\;=\;\langle 123*45*789\rangle\;=\;F_{6}

(e)

\displaystyle\langle 1245\rangle\;\mapsto\;\frac{\langle 123*45*789\rangle\langle 1456\rangle+\langle 1345\rangle\langle 12*456*789\rangle}{\langle 1245\rangle}\;=\;\langle 13*456*789\rangle\;=\;F_{2}

(f)

\displaystyle\langle 1456\rangle\;\mapsto\;\frac{\langle 13*456*789\rangle+\langle 1789\rangle\langle 3456\rangle}{\langle 1456\rangle}\;=\;\langle 3789\rangle

(g)

\displaystyle\langle 1345\rangle\;\mapsto\;\frac{\langle 123*45*789\rangle\langle 3456\rangle+\langle 13*456*789\rangle\langle 2345\rangle}{\langle 1345\rangle}\;=\;\langle 23*456*789\rangle\;=\;F_{1}

(h)

\displaystyle\langle 12*567*789\rangle\;\mapsto\;\frac{\langle 1237\rangle F_{3}\langle 5678\rangle+F_{4}\langle 1278\rangle\langle 4567\rangle}{\langle 12*567*789\rangle}\;=\;\langle 123*456*78\rangle\;=\;F_{9}

(i)

\displaystyle\langle 1278\rangle\;\mapsto\;\;=\;\frac{\langle 1238\rangle\langle 12*456*789\rangle+\langle 1289\rangle\langle 123*456*78\rangle}{\langle 1278\rangle}\;=\;\langle 123*456*89\rangle\;=\;F_{7}

(j)

\displaystyle\langle 1238\rangle\;\mapsto\;\frac{\langle 1237\rangle\langle 123*456*89\rangle+\langle 1239\rangle\langle 123*456*78\rangle}{\langle 1238\rangle}\;=\;\langle 123*456*79\rangle\;=\;F_{8}

(k)

\displaystyle\langle 1235\rangle\;\mapsto\;\frac{\langle 123*56*789\rangle\langle 1234\rangle+\langle 1236\rangle\langle 123*45*789\rangle}{\langle 1235\rangle}\;=\;\langle 123*46*789\rangle\;=\;F_{5}

After applying these mutations, we end up with
\overline{\overline{\Sigma}}
.
∎

Proof of
Theorem

8.7
.

Lemma

8.12
verifies that
\overline{\Sigma}
is a freezing of a seed for
\Gr_{4,n}
.
We verify that
\Psi_{sp}:\mathcal{A}(\Sigma)\to\mathcal{A}(\overline{\Sigma})
is a quasi-cluster homomorphism as in
Definition

2.11
. Our calculations in
Lemma

8.10
show that the image
\Psi_{sp}(x)
of every mutable cluster variable
x
in
\Sigma
is proportional to a mutable variable
\bar{x}
in
\overline{\Sigma}
, up to a Laurent monomial in the frozen variables
\{F_{2},F_{3},F_{7},F_{8}\}
. This verifies condition (1):
\Psi_{sp}(x)\propto\bar{x}
. Our calculations in
Lemma

8.11
verify condition (2), that
\Psi_{sp}
maps the exchange ratio of
x
to the exchange ratio of
\bar{x}
, as required.
∎

8.3.

We show that
\Psi_{ch}:\mathbb{C}(\Gr_{4,N^{\prime}})\;\rightarrow\;\mathbb{C}(\Gr_{4,n})
from
Definition

5.8
is a quasi-cluster homomorphism. Here
N^{\prime}=\{1,2,3,\mathrm{B},\mathrm{C},\mathrm{D},\ldots,n\}
.

Theorem 8.13
.

Chain-tree promotion
\Psi_{ch}
is a quasi-cluster homomorphism
of cluster algebras from

\mathcal{A}(\Sigma)
to

\mathcal{A}(\overline{\Sigma})
, where
\Sigma
is a seed for

and
\overline{\Sigma}
is the seed for

.

Notation 8.15
.

Let
\Sigma
be the following rectangles seed for
{\Gr}_{4,N^{\prime}}
.
\langle 123\mathrm{B}\rangle
\langle 123\mathrm{C}\rangle
\langle 123\mathrm{D}\rangle

Note that the mutable variables of

Lemma 8.16
.

The mutable variables in
\overline{\Sigma}
are proportional to the corresponding mutable variables in
\Psi_{ch}(\Sigma)
.

Proof.

We apply the map
\Psi_{ch}
to the variables in
\Sigma
, and substitute the vectors
2,3,\mathrm{B}
, and
\mathrm{C}
using the formulas in
Definition

5.8
.

The variables
\langle 123i\rangle
, with
i>\mathrm{C}=12
in the first row are fixed by
\Psi_{sp}
as

\Psi_{ch}\big(\langle 123i\rangle\big)\;=\;\left\langle 1\left(2-\tfrac{F_{1}}{F_{2}}1\right)\left(3-\tfrac{F_{1}}{F_{3}}1+\tfrac{F_{2}}{F_{3}}2\right)i\right\rangle=\langle 123i\rangle.

Analogously, the variables
\langle 12\mathrm{C}\mathrm{D}\rangle,\langle 1\mathrm{B}\mathrm{C}\mathrm{D}\rangle
, and
\langle\mathrm{B}\mathrm{C}\mathrm{D}\mathrm{E}\rangle
and all the variables to their right in the respective rows are fixed by
\Psi_{sp}
.
The variables
\langle 123\mathrm{B}\rangle
and
\langle 3\mathrm{B}\mathrm{C}\mathrm{D}\rangle
map under
\Psi_{sp}
to:

\displaystyle\Psi_{ch}\big(\langle 123\mathrm{B}\rangle\big)\;=\;\left\langle 1\left(2-\tfrac{F_{1}}{F_{2}}1\right)\left(3-\tfrac{F_{1}}{F_{3}}1+\tfrac{F_{2}}{F_{3}}2\right)\left(\tfrac{F_{7}}{F_{\mathrm{B}}}7-\tfrac{F_{1}}{F_{\mathrm{B}}}1+\tfrac{F_{2}}{F_{\mathrm{B}}}2-\tfrac{F_{3}}{F_{\mathrm{B}}}3\right)\right\rangle\;=\;\frac{F_{7}}{F_{\mathrm{B}}}\langle 1237\rangle

\displaystyle\Psi_{ch}\big(\langle 3\mathrm{B}\mathrm{C}\mathrm{D}\rangle\big)\;=\;\left\langle\left(\tfrac{F_{7}}{F_{3}}7-\tfrac{F_{\mathrm{B}}}{F_{3}}\mathrm{B}+\tfrac{F_{\mathrm{C}}}{F_{3}}\mathrm{C}-\tfrac{F_{\mathrm{D}}}{F_{3}}\mathrm{D}\right)\left(\mathrm{B}-\tfrac{F_{\mathrm{C}}}{F_{\mathrm{B}}}\mathrm{C}+\tfrac{F_{\mathrm{D}}}{F_{\mathrm{C}}}\mathrm{D}\right)\left(\mathrm{C}-\tfrac{F_{\mathrm{D}}}{F_{\mathrm{C}}}\mathrm{D}\right)\mathrm{D}\right\rangle\;=\;\frac{F_{7}}{F_{3}}\langle 7\mathrm{B}\mathrm{C}\mathrm{D}\rangle

The images of the variables
\langle 123C\rangle,\langle 12BC\rangle,\langle 13BC\rangle
and
\langle 23BC\rangle
under
\Psi_{ch}
involve quartics cluster variables and are:

\displaystyle\Psi_{ch}\big(\langle 123\mathrm{C}\rangle\big)\;

\displaystyle=\;\left\langle 1\left(2-\tfrac{F_{1}}{F_{2}}1\right)\left(3-\tfrac{F_{1}}{F_{3}}1+\tfrac{F_{2}}{F_{3}}2\right)\left(\tfrac{F_{\mathrm{B}}}{F_{C}}\mathrm{B}-\tfrac{F_{7}}{F_{C}}7+\tfrac{F_{1}}{F_{C}}1-\tfrac{F_{2}}{F_{C}}2-\tfrac{F_{3}}{F_{C}}3\right)\right\rangle\;=\;

\displaystyle=\ \left\langle 123\left(\tfrac{F_{\mathrm{B}}}{F_{C}}\mathrm{B}-\tfrac{F_{7}}{F_{C}}7\right)\right\rangle=\frac{\langle 123\mathrm{B}\rangle F_{\mathrm{B}}-\langle 1237\rangle F_{7}}{F_{C}}=\frac{X_{1}}{F_{C}}

\displaystyle\Psi_{ch}\big(\langle 12\mathrm{B}\mathrm{C}\rangle\big)\;

\displaystyle=\;\left\langle 1\left(2-\tfrac{F_{1}}{F_{2}}1\right)\left(\tfrac{F_{7}}{F_{\mathrm{B}}}7-1\tfrac{F_{1}}{F_{\mathrm{B}}}+\tfrac{F_{2}}{F_{\mathrm{B}}}2-\tfrac{F_{3}}{F_{\mathrm{B}}}3\right)\left(\tfrac{F_{\mathrm{B}}}{F_{C}}\mathrm{B}+\tfrac{F_{7}}{F_{C}}7-1\tfrac{F_{1}}{F_{C}}+\tfrac{F_{2}}{F_{C}}2-\tfrac{F_{3}}{F_{C}}3\right)\right\rangle\;=\;

\displaystyle=\;\left\langle 12\left(\tfrac{F_{7}}{F_{\mathrm{B}}}7-\tfrac{F_{3}}{F_{\mathrm{B}}}3\right)\left(\tfrac{F_{\mathrm{B}}}{F_{C}}\mathrm{B}\right)\right\rangle=\frac{\langle 127\mathrm{B}\rangle F_{7}-\langle 123\mathrm{B}\rangle F_{3}}{F_{C}}=\frac{X_{2}}{F_{C}}

\displaystyle\Psi_{ch}\big(\langle 13\mathrm{B}\mathrm{C}\rangle\big)\;

\displaystyle=\;\left\langle 1\left(3-\tfrac{F_{1}}{F_{3}}1+\tfrac{F_{2}}{F_{3}}2\right)\left(\tfrac{F_{7}}{F_{\mathrm{B}}}7-1\tfrac{F_{1}}{F_{\mathrm{B}}}+\tfrac{F_{2}}{F_{\mathrm{B}}}2-\tfrac{F_{3}}{F_{\mathrm{B}}}3\right)\left(\tfrac{F_{\mathrm{B}}}{F_{C}}\mathrm{B}+\tfrac{F_{7}}{F_{C}}7+\dots\right)\right\rangle\;=\;

\displaystyle=\;\left\langle 1\left(3+\tfrac{F_{2}}{F_{3}}2\right)\left(\tfrac{F_{7}}{F_{\mathrm{B}}}7\right)\left(\tfrac{F_{\mathrm{B}}}{F_{C}}\mathrm{B}\right)\right\rangle=\frac{\langle 137\mathrm{B}\rangle F_{3}+\langle 127\mathrm{B}\rangle F_{2}}{F_{3}F_{C}}F_{7}=X_{3}\frac{F_{7}}{F_{3}F_{C}}

\displaystyle\Psi_{ch}\big(\langle 23\mathrm{B}\mathrm{C}\rangle\big)\;

\displaystyle=\;\left\langle\left(2-\tfrac{F_{1}}{F_{2}}1\right)\left(3-\tfrac{F_{1}}{F_{3}}1+\tfrac{F_{2}}{F_{3}}2\right)\left(\tfrac{F_{7}}{F_{\mathrm{B}}}7-1\tfrac{F_{1}}{F_{\mathrm{B}}}+\tfrac{F_{2}}{F_{\mathrm{B}}}2-\tfrac{F_{3}}{F_{\mathrm{B}}}3\right)\left(\tfrac{F_{\mathrm{B}}}{F_{C}}\mathrm{B}+\tfrac{F_{7}}{F_{C}}7+\dots\right)\right\rangle\;=\;

\displaystyle=\;\left\langle\left(2-\tfrac{F_{1}}{F_{2}}1\right)3\left(\tfrac{F_{7}}{F_{\mathrm{B}}}7\right)\left(\tfrac{F_{\mathrm{B}}}{F_{C}}\mathrm{B}\right)\right\rangle=\frac{\langle 237\mathrm{B}\rangle F_{2}-\langle 137\mathrm{B}\rangle F_{1}}{F_{2}F_{C}}F_{7}=X_{4}\frac{F_{7}}{F_{2}F_{C}}

∎

Lemma 8.17
.

Applying
\Psi_{ch}
to the exchange ratio of a mutable variable in
\Sigma
gives the exchange ratio of the corresponding mutable variables in
\overline{\Sigma}
.

Proof.

We verify that the exchange ratios of mutable variables in
\Sigma
map to the exchange ratio of their proportional mutable images. For the three mutable variables in the leftmost column, applying
Definition

2.10
and using the computations from the proof of the previous lemma, we obtain:

\displaystyle\Psi_{ch}\left(\hat{y}_{\Sigma}\big(\langle 123\mathrm{C}\rangle\big)\right)\;=\;\Psi_{ch}\left(\frac{\langle 123\mathrm{B}\rangle\langle 12\mathrm{C}\mathrm{D}\rangle}{\langle 12\mathrm{B}\mathrm{C}\rangle\langle 123\mathrm{D}\rangle}\right)\;=\;\frac{\tfrac{F_{7}}{F_{\mathrm{B}}}\langle 1237\rangle\langle 12\mathrm{C}\mathrm{D}\rangle}{\tfrac{1}{F_{\mathrm{C}}}X_{2}\langle 123\mathrm{D}\rangle}\;=\;\hat{y}_{\overline{\Sigma}}\big(X_{1}\big)

\displaystyle\Psi_{ch}\left(\hat{y}_{\Sigma}\big(\langle 12\mathrm{B}\mathrm{C}\rangle\big)\right)\;=\;\Psi_{ch}\left(\frac{\langle 123\mathrm{C}\rangle\langle 1\mathrm{B}\mathrm{C}\mathrm{D}\rangle}{\langle 12\mathrm{C}\mathrm{D}\rangle\langle 13\mathrm{B}\mathrm{C}\rangle}\right)\;=\;\frac{X_{1}\tfrac{1}{F_{\mathrm{C}}}\langle 1\mathrm{B}\mathrm{C}\mathrm{D}\rangle}{\langle 12\mathrm{C}\mathrm{D}\rangle X_{3}\tfrac{F_{7}}{F_{3}F_{\mathrm{C}}}}\;=\;\hat{y}_{\overline{\Sigma}}\big(X_{2}\big)

\displaystyle\Psi_{ch}\left(\hat{y}_{\Sigma}\big(\langle 13\mathrm{B}\mathrm{C}\rangle\big)\right)\;=\;\Psi_{ch}\left(\frac{\langle 12\mathrm{B}\mathrm{C}\rangle\langle 3\mathrm{B}\mathrm{C}\mathrm{D}\rangle}{\langle 1\mathrm{B}\mathrm{C}\mathrm{D}\rangle\langle 23\mathrm{B}\mathrm{C}\rangle}\right)\;=\;\frac{X_{2}\tfrac{1}{F_{\mathrm{C}}}\langle 7\mathrm{B}\mathrm{C}\mathrm{D}\rangle\tfrac{F_{7}}{F_{3}}}{\langle 1\mathrm{B}\mathrm{C}\mathrm{D}\rangle X_{4}\tfrac{F_{7}}{F_{2}F_{\mathrm{C}}}}\;=\;\hat{y}_{\overline{\Sigma}}\big(X_{3}\big)

For the three mutable variables in the second column from the left:

\displaystyle\Psi_{ch}\left(\hat{y}_{\Sigma}\big(\langle 123\mathrm{D}\rangle\big)\right)\;=\;\Psi_{ch}\left(\frac{\langle 123\mathrm{C}\rangle\langle 12\mathrm{D}\mathrm{E}\rangle}{\langle 12\mathrm{C}\mathrm{D}\rangle\langle 123\mathrm{E}\rangle}\right)\;=\;\frac{\tfrac{1}{F_{\mathrm{C}}}X_{1}\langle 12\mathrm{D}\mathrm{E}\rangle}{\langle 12\mathrm{C}\mathrm{D}\rangle\langle 123\mathrm{E}\rangle}\;=\;\hat{y}_{\overline{\Sigma}}\big(\langle 123\mathrm{E}\rangle\big)

\displaystyle\Psi_{ch}\left(\hat{y}_{\Sigma}\big(\langle 12\mathrm{C}\mathrm{D}\rangle\big)\right)\;=\;\Psi_{ch}\left(\frac{\langle 12\mathrm{B}\mathrm{C}\rangle\langle 123\mathrm{D}\rangle\langle 1\mathrm{C}\mathrm{D}\mathrm{E}\rangle}{\langle 123\mathrm{C}\rangle\langle 12\mathrm{D}\mathrm{E}\rangle\langle 1\mathrm{B}\mathrm{C}\mathrm{D}\rangle}\right)\;=\;\frac{X_{2}\tfrac{1}{F_{\mathrm{C}}}\langle 123\mathrm{D}\rangle\langle 1\mathrm{C}\mathrm{D}\mathrm{E}\rangle}{X_{1}\tfrac{1}{F_{\mathrm{C}}}\langle 12\mathrm{D}\mathrm{E}\rangle\langle 1\mathrm{B}\mathrm{C}\mathrm{D}\rangle}\;=\;\hat{y}_{\overline{\Sigma}}\big(\langle 12\mathrm{C}\mathrm{D}\rangle\big)

\displaystyle\Psi_{ch}\left(\hat{y}_{\Sigma}\big(\langle 1\mathrm{B}\mathrm{C}\mathrm{D}\rangle\big)\right)\;=\;\Psi_{ch}\left(\frac{\langle 12\mathrm{C}\mathrm{D}\rangle\langle 13\mathrm{B}\mathrm{C}\rangle\langle\mathrm{B}\mathrm{C}\mathrm{D}\mathrm{E}\rangle}{\langle 12\mathrm{B}\mathrm{C}\rangle\langle 1\mathrm{C}\mathrm{D}\mathrm{E}\rangle\langle 3\mathrm{B}\mathrm{C}\mathrm{D}\rangle}\right)=\frac{\langle 12\mathrm{C}\mathrm{D}\rangle X_{3}\tfrac{F_{7}}{F_{3}F_{\mathrm{C}}}\langle\mathrm{B}\mathrm{C}\mathrm{D}\mathrm{E}\rangle}{X_{2}\tfrac{1}{F_{\mathrm{C}}}\langle 1\mathrm{C}\mathrm{D}\mathrm{E}\rangle\langle 7\mathrm{B}\mathrm{C}\mathrm{D}\rangle\tfrac{F_{7}}{F_{3}}}=\hat{y}_{\overline{\Sigma}}\big(\langle 1\mathrm{B}\mathrm{C}\mathrm{D}\rangle\big)

Finally, the verification for the remaining mutable variables is omitted, because these variables and their six neighbors are all fixed by
\Psi_{ch}
.
∎

.

Proof.

\displaystyle x_{3\mathrm{C}},x_{3\mathrm{B}},x_{3\mathrm{A}},x_{39},x_{38},x_{37},x_{36},x_{2\mathrm{C}},x_{2\mathrm{B}},x_{2\mathrm{A}},x_{29},x_{28},x_{27},x_{26},\bar{x}_{39},\bar{x}_{38},\bar{x}_{3\mathrm{A}},\bar{\bar{x}}_{39},\bar{x}_{28},\bar{x}_{29},

∎

Lemma 8.21
.

The images under
\Psi_{fo}
of the mutable variables of
\Sigma
are proportional to the mutable variables of
\overline{\Sigma}
.

Proof.

We apply the map
\Psi_{fo}
to the variables in
\Sigma
, and substitute the vectors
2
and
b
, using the formulas in
Definition

5.9
. The variables
\langle 12i\rangle
and
\langle abj\rangle
, with
i\not=b
and
j\not=2
are fixed by
\Psi_{fo}
since

\Psi_{fo}\big(\langle 12i\rangle\big)\;=\;\left\langle 1\left(2-\tfrac{F_{1}}{F_{2}}1\right)i\right\rangle\;=\;\langle 12i\rangle,\;\;\text{ and }\;\;\Psi_{fo}\big(\langle abj\rangle\big)\;=\;\left\langle a\left(b-\tfrac{F_{a}}{F_{b}}a\right)j\right\rangle\;=\;\langle abj\rangle.

Here we have denoted by
F_{i}
the Plücker with labels
\{1,2,3,4\}\setminus\{i\}
and by
F_{x}
the Plücker with labels
\{a,b,c,d\}\setminus\{x\}
.
The only variables that change under
\Psi_{fo}
are the following ones:

\displaystyle\Psi_{fo}\big(\langle 12b\rangle\big)\;=\;\left\langle 1\left(2-\tfrac{F_{1}}{F_{2}}1\right)\left(b-\tfrac{F_{a}}{F_{b}}a\right)\right\rangle\;=\;\frac{\langle 12b\rangle F_{b}-\langle 12a\rangle F_{a}}{F_{b}}\;=\;\frac{\langle 12*ab*cd\rangle}{F_{b}}

\displaystyle\Psi_{fo}\big(\langle 1bd\rangle\big)\;=\;\left\langle 1\left(\tfrac{F_{c}}{F_{b}}c-\tfrac{F_{d}}{F_{b}}d\right)d\right\rangle\;=\;\frac{F_{c}}{F_{b}}\langle 1cd\rangle

\displaystyle\Psi_{fo}\big(\langle bde\rangle\big)\;=\;\left\langle\left(\tfrac{F_{c}}{F_{b}}c-\tfrac{F_{d}}{F_{b}}d\right)de\right\rangle\;=\;\frac{F_{c}}{F_{b}}\langle cde\rangle

\displaystyle\Psi_{fo}\big(\langle 245\rangle\big)\;=\;\left\langle\left(\tfrac{F_{3}}{F_{2}}3-\tfrac{F_{4}}{F_{2}}4\right)45\right\rangle\;=\;\frac{F_{3}}{F_{2}}\langle 345\rangle

Thus, we obtain frozen variables
F_{2},F_{3},F_{b},F_{c}
, the quadratic
\langle 12*ab*cd\rangle
and the new Plückers
\langle 1cd\rangle,\langle cde\rangle
and
\langle 345\rangle
in
\overline{\Sigma}
, as required. Note that the mutable variables in
\Sigma
are in bijection with mutable factors in their images in
\overline{\Sigma}
.
∎

Lemma 8.22
.

The exchange ratios of the mutable variables in
\Sigma
map under
\Psi_{fo}
to the exchange ratios of the corresponding mutable variables in
\overline{\Sigma}
.

Proof.

We verify that the exchange ratios of mutable variables in
\Sigma
map to the exchange ratio of their proportional mutable images. Applying
Definition

2.10
and using the computations from the proof of the previous lemma, we obtain for the following mutable variables of the first row:

\displaystyle\Psi_{fo}\left(\hat{y}_{\Sigma}\big(\langle 12a\rangle\big)\right)\;

\displaystyle=\;\Psi_{fo}\left(\frac{\langle 12a^{\prime}\rangle\langle 1ab\rangle}{\langle 12b\rangle\langle 1a^{\prime}a\rangle}\right)\;=\;\frac{\langle 12a^{\prime}\rangle\langle 1ab\rangle}{\tfrac{1}{F_{b}}\langle 12*ab*cd\rangle\langle 1a^{\prime}a\rangle}\;=\;\hat{y}_{\overline{\Sigma}}\big(\langle 12a\rangle\big)

\displaystyle\Psi_{fo}\left(\hat{y}_{\Sigma}\big(\langle 12b\rangle\big)\right)\;

\displaystyle=\;\Psi_{fo}\left(\frac{\langle 12a\rangle\langle 1bd\rangle}{\langle 12d\rangle\langle 1ab\rangle}\right)\;=\;\frac{\langle 12a\rangle\tfrac{F_{c}}{F_{b}}\langle 1cd\rangle}{\langle 12d\rangle\langle 1ab\rangle}\;=\;\hat{y}_{\overline{\Sigma}}\big(\langle 12*ab*cd\rangle\big)

\displaystyle\Psi_{fo}\left(\hat{y}_{\Sigma}\big(\langle 12d\rangle\big)\right)\;

\displaystyle=\;\Psi_{fo}\left(\frac{\langle 12b\rangle\langle 1de\rangle}{\langle 1bd\rangle\langle 12e\rangle}\right)\;=\;\frac{\tfrac{1}{F_{b}}\langle 12*ab*cd\rangle\langle 1de\rangle}{\tfrac{F_{c}}{F_{b}}\langle 1cd\rangle\langle 12e\rangle}\;=\;\hat{y}_{\overline{\Sigma}}\big(\langle 12d\rangle\big)

For the following mutable variables of the second row we obtain:

\displaystyle\Psi_{fo}\left(\hat{y}_{\Sigma}\big(\langle 145\rangle\big)\right)\;

\displaystyle=\;\Psi_{fo}\left(\frac{\langle 125\rangle\langle 456\rangle}{\langle 156\rangle\langle 245\rangle}\right)\;=\;\frac{\langle 125\rangle\langle 456\rangle}{\langle 156\rangle\tfrac{F_{3}}{F_{2}}\langle 345\rangle}\;=\;\hat{y}_{\overline{\Sigma}}\big(\langle 145\rangle\big)

\displaystyle\Psi_{fo}\left(\hat{y}_{\Sigma}\big(\langle 1ab\rangle\big)\right)\;

\displaystyle=\;\Psi_{fo}\left(\frac{\langle 12b\rangle\langle 1a^{\prime}a\rangle\langle abd\rangle}{\langle 1bd\rangle\langle 12a\rangle\langle a^{\prime}ab\rangle}\right)\;=\;\frac{\tfrac{1}{F_{b}}\langle 12*ab*cd\rangle\langle 1a^{\prime}a\rangle\langle abd\rangle}{\tfrac{F_{c}}{F_{b}}\langle 1cd\rangle\langle 12a\rangle\langle a^{\prime}ab\rangle}\;=\;\hat{y}_{\overline{\Sigma}}\big(\langle 1ab\rangle\big)

\displaystyle\Psi_{fo}\left(\hat{y}_{\Sigma}\big(\langle 1bd\rangle\big)\right)\;

\displaystyle=\;\Psi_{fo}\left(\frac{\langle 12d\rangle\langle bde\rangle\langle 1ab\rangle}{\langle 12b\rangle\langle 1de\rangle\langle abd\rangle}\right)\;=\;\frac{\langle 12d\rangle\tfrac{F_{c}}{F_{b}}\langle cde\rangle\langle 1ab\rangle}{\tfrac{1}{F_{b}}\langle 12*ab*cd\rangle\langle 1de\rangle\langle abd\rangle}\;=\;\hat{y}_{\overline{\Sigma}}\big(\langle 1cd\rangle\big)

\displaystyle\Psi_{fo}\left(\hat{y}_{\Sigma}\big(\langle 1de\rangle\big)\right)\;

\displaystyle=\;\Psi_{fo}\left(\frac{\langle 12e\rangle\langle 1bd\rangle\langle def\rangle}{\langle 12d\rangle\langle 1ef\rangle\langle bde\rangle}\right)\;=\;\frac{\langle 12e\rangle\tfrac{F_{c}}{F_{b}}\langle 1cd\rangle\langle def\rangle}{\langle 12d\rangle\langle 1ef\rangle\tfrac{F_{c}}{F_{b}}\langle cde\rangle}\;=\;\hat{y}_{\overline{\Sigma}}\big(\langle 12e\rangle\big)

Finally, the verification for the remaining mutable variables is omitted, because these variables and their neighbors are all fixed by
\Psi_{fo}
.
∎

Proof of
Theorem

8.19
.

8.20
shows that
\overline{\Sigma}
is a seed for
\Gr_{4,n}
with certain variables frozen. The properties of quasi-cluster homomorphism follow from Lemmas

and
8.22
.
∎

9.
The (colored) operad of plabic tangles

In this section we describe the operad structure which underlies the relation between plabic tangles and promotion maps.

9.1.
The plabic operad

Definition 9.1
.

For
n\in\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 0}
and
\mathbf{d}=(d_{1},\dots,d_{l})\in\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 0}^{l}
, we denote by
\mathcal{P}(n;\mathbf{d})
the set of plabic tangles
(G,\mathbf{D}=(D^{(i)})_{i\in[l]})
where
G
ranges over all possible cores with
n
boundary vertices, and
\mathbf{D}
ranges over all possible blobs where
|D^{(i)}|=d_{i}
. We consider
\mathcal{P}(n;\mathbf{d})
up to local moves which do not touch the boundary vertices of the blobs.

The
(colored) operad of plabic tangles
(or simply the
plabic operad
)
\mathcal{P}
consists of the following:

(1)

the collection
(\mathcal{P}(n;\mathbf{d}))_{n,\mathbf{d}}
, where we refer to the values of
n
and
d_{i}
as the
colors
of
\mathcal{P}
;

(2)

a distinguished element
1=1_{n}
in

\mathcal{P}(n;n)
called the
unit
, which consists of
n
non-intersecting segments connecting the boundary vertices of the inner and outer disks;

(3)

for all positive integers

n,\mathbf{d}=(d_{1},\dots,d_{l}),\mathbf{d}^{\prime}=(d^{\prime}_{1},\dots,d^{\prime}_{l^{\prime}})
and
1\leq i\leq l
, a composition map

\displaystyle\circ_{i}:

\displaystyle\mathcal{P}(n;\mathbf{d})\times\mathcal{P}(d_{i};\mathbf{d}^{\prime})\to\mathcal{P}(n;d_{1},\ldots,d_{i-1},d^{\prime}_{1},\ldots,d^{\prime}_{l^{\prime}},d_{i+1},\dots,d_{l}),

\displaystyle(G,\mathbf{D}),(G^{\prime},\mathbf{E})\mapsto(G^{\prime\prime},(D^{(1)},\ldots,{D^{(i-1)}},E^{(1)},\ldots,E^{(l^{\prime})},{D^{(i+1)}},\ldots,D^{(l)}))

obtained by inserting
(G^{\prime},\mathbf{E})
into the disk
D^{(i)}
and gluing the attaching segments of
D^{(i)}
to boundary vertices of
G^{\prime}
;

(4)

for each positive integer
r
and permutation
\sigma\in S_{l}
, a map

\sigma^{*}:\mathcal{P}(n;d_{1},\ldots,d_{l})\to\mathcal{P}(n;d_{\sigma(1)},\ldots,d_{\sigma(l)})

given by renumbering the inner disks.

Figure 10.
We allow square moves on plabic graphs with a brushing only if the perfect orientation is a rotation of the orientation here and the paths either do not interact with the square or go between diagonally opposite corners.

Remark 9.2
.

Similarly, we can define the
(colored) operad of brushed plabic tangles

\widehat{P}=(\widehat{\mathcal{P}}(n;\mathbf{d})_{n,\mathbf{d}})
as the operad whose underlying set is the set of brushed plabic tangles. We consider brushed tangles up to expand-contract moves and
brushed
square moves (shown in
Figure

10
) that do not touch the boundary vertices of the blobs. The unit
\widehat{1}_{n}
is the tangle
1_{n}
with brushing given by the trivial paths each consisting of one boundary vertex.
Composition is given by inserting the brushed tangle
(G^{\prime},\mathbf{E})
into the blob
D=D^{(i)}
of the brushed tangle
(G,\mathbf{D})
, where the brushing is obtained as follows.

Let
\mathcal{O}=\mathcal{O}^{D}
be the reverse perfect orientation of
G
associated to the blob
D
, let
E

be a blob of
(G^{\prime},\mathbf{E})
, and let
\mathcal{O}^{\prime}
be the reverse perfect orientation of
G^{\prime}
associated to
E
. Let
I\subset D

be the set of sinks of
\mathcal{O}^{\prime}
.
The orientation
\mathcal{O}^{E}
of the composed brushing is obtained by orienting
G^{\prime}
according to
\mathcal{O}^{\prime}
, reversing the orientation of all edges in the paths
\{P_{u}\}_{u\in I}
in
\mathcal{O}
, and orienting all other edges of
G
according to
\mathcal{O}
.
For
u\in E
, the path
P_{u}
of the composed brushing is the concatenation of the path
P^{\prime}_{u}
in
\mathcal{O}^{\prime}
with the path
P_{v}
in
\mathcal{O}
which ends at the starting point of
P^{\prime}_{u}
. For any other blob
D^{\prime}\in\mathbf{D}
, we do not change the paths or the orientation. The symmetric group action is the natural lift of the action on the underlying tangle.

Being an operad means that the following coherence axioms hold; the proof is straightforward.

Observation 9.3
.

Let
(G,\mathbf{D})\in\mathcal{P}(N,\mathbf{d})
and
(G^{\prime},\mathbf{E})\in\mathcal{P}(d_{i},\mathbf{d}^{\prime}),
with
\mathbf{d}=(d_{1},\ldots,d_{\ell})
and
\mathbf{d}^{\prime}=(d^{\prime}_{1},\ldots,d^{\prime}_{\ell^{\prime}})
. Then we have the following.

•

identity:

(G,\mathbf{D})\circ_{i}1_{d_{i}}=(G,\mathbf{D}).

•

commutativity
:
For
j\neq i\leq\ell
and
(G^{\prime\prime},\mathbf{H})\in\mathcal{P}(d_{j},\mathbf{d}^{\prime\prime})

[(G,\mathbf{D})\circ_{i}(G^{\prime},\mathbf{E})]\circ_{j}(G^{\prime\prime},\mathbf{H})=[(G,\mathbf{D})\circ_{j}(G^{\prime\prime},\mathbf{H})]\circ_{i}(G^{\prime},\mathbf{E}).

•

associativity
:
For
i\leq\ell,j\leq\ell^{\prime},
and
(G^{\prime\prime},\mathbf{H})\in\mathcal{P}(d^{\prime}_{j},\mathbf{d}^{\prime\prime})

[(G,\mathbf{D})\circ_{i}(G^{\prime},\mathbf{E})]\circ_{j}(G^{\prime\prime},\mathbf{H})=(G,\mathbf{D})\circ_{i}[(G^{\prime},\mathbf{E})\circ_{j}(G^{\prime\prime},\mathbf{H})].

•

group action:
The maps
\sigma^{\star}
for
\sigma\in S_{\ell}
act on
\bigsqcup_{\mathbf{d}=(d_{1},\ldots,d_{\ell})}\mathcal{P}(N,\mathbf{d}).

•

equivariance of compositions
: For
i\leq\ell
and
\sigma\in S_{\ell},

(G,(D^{(\sigma(1))},\ldots,D^{(\sigma(\ell))})\circ_{\sigma(i)}(G^{\prime},\mathbf{E})=\hat{\sigma}^{*}((G,\mathbf{D})\circ_{i}(G^{\prime},\mathbf{E})),

where
\hat{\sigma}
is the permutation on
([\ell]\setminus\{i\})\sqcup[\ell^{\prime}]
, identified with the total order obtained from
\{\sigma(1),\dots,\sigma(\ell)\}
by
removing
i
and inserting the total order on
\{1,2,\dots,\ell^{\prime}\}
in its place.
An analogous statement holds for
\sigma\in S_{\ell^{\prime}}
acting on
\mathbf{E}
.

These properties also hold for the operad of brushed plabic tangles.

A
suboperad
is a subset of an operad which contains the units and is closed under composition and permutation maps
\sigma^{*}
.

Proposition 9.4
.

The collection of dominant (
m
-generically) solvable plabic tangles forms a suboperad
\mathcal{P}^{\mathrm{ds}}_{m}
of
\mathcal{P}.
In particular the composition of dominant solvable tangles is dominant solvable.
The same holds for the suboperad
\widehat{\mathcal{P}^{\mathrm{ds}}_{m}}
of brushed dominant solvable tangles.

Proof.

It is straightforward that
1_{n}
is solvable and dominant, and it is also immediate that the action of the symmetric group restricts to an action on
\mathcal{P}^{\mathrm{ds}}_{m}.
We are left to show that compositions of dominant solvable tangles are dominant solvable tangles.

Consider the composed tangle
{(G,\mathbf{D})\circ_{i}(G^{\prime},\mathbf{E})}
where both constituent tangles are dominant solvable. The proof of
Corollary

4.14
shows that the core of the composed tangle is solvable. The fact that the composed tangle is dominant follows from
Theorem

4.13
and

Remark

9.2
.

The proof for brushed tangles is a straightforward extension of the above argument.
∎

9.2.
Representations of plabic operads

In this section we explain that the promotion maps from
Section

4
form
representations
of the operad
\mathcal{P}^{\mathrm{ds}}_{m}
of dominant solvable tangles.

Definition 9.5
.

An
algebra
\mathcal{R}
over an operad of plabic tangles
consists of:

•

a suboperad
\mathcal{P}^{\prime}
of
\mathcal{P}
;

•

a collection of
\mathbb{C}
-algebras
\mathcal{R}(n)
for every color which may appear in
\mathcal{P}^{\prime}
, where

\mathcal{R}(0)=\mathbb{C}
;

•

for every
(G,\mathbf{D})\in\mathcal{P}^{\prime}
, a
promotion map

(13)

\mathcal{R}(G,\mathbf{D}):\bigotimes_{D\in\mathbf{D}}\mathcal{R}(|D|)\to\mathcal{R}(n)

The spaces
\mathcal{R}(|D|)
for
D\in\mathbf{D}
are the
input spaces
or
input components
of
\mathcal{R}(G,\mathbf{D}),
while
\mathcal{R}(n)
is the output space.

This data is required to satisfy:

•

normalization
: when
\ell=0
the domain of the map (
13
) is
\mathbb{C}
, and the map is the natural embedding
\mathbb{C}\to\mathcal{R}(n)
which sends
1\mapsto 1
.

•

identity
:
\mathcal{R}(1_{n})
is the identity map
\mathcal{R}(n)\to\mathcal{R}(n).

•

composition
:

\mathcal{R}\left((G,\mathbf{D})\circ_{i}(G^{\prime},\mathbf{E})\right)=\mathcal{R}(G,\mathbf{D})\circ_{i}\mathcal{R}(G^{\prime},\mathbf{E})
, where the
\circ_{i}
on the right corresponds to substituting the output of
\mathcal{R}(G^{\prime},\mathbf{E})
into the
i
th component of the domain of
\mathcal{R}(G,\mathbf{D})
.

•

symmetric group action
: for
\sigma\in S_{|\mathbf{D}|}
, we have

\sigma\cdot\mathcal{R}(G,\mathbf{D})=\mathcal{R}(\sigma^{*}(G,\mathbf{D}))
,
where
\sigma\cdot
permutes the components of the domain
\bigotimes_{D\in\mathbf{D}}\mathcal{R}(|D|)
of
\mathcal{R}(G,\mathbf{D})
according to the permutation
\sigma.

One can similarly define an algebra
\widehat{\mathcal{R}}
over a suboperad of brushed plabic tangles.
An important example is the following.

Definition 9.6
.

The
algebra
\mathcal{A}^{\mathrm{ds}}_{m}
of dominant
(
m
-generically) solvable plabic tangles

is the collection
\mathcal{R}(n)=\mathbb{C}(\Conf^{\circ}_{m,n}),
for
n\geq m
, together with the promotion maps
(
4
)

\psi=\psi_{(G,\mathbf{D})}:\bigotimes_{D\in\mathbf{D}}\mathbb{C}(\Conf^{\circ}_{m,D})\to\mathbb{C}(\Conf^{\circ}_{m,n})

associated to each dominant (
m
-generically) solvable plabic tangle
(G,\mathbf{D}).

The
algebra
\widehat{\mathcal{A}^{\mathrm{ds}}_{m}}
of dominant
(
m
-generically) solvable brushed plabic tangles

is the collection
\widehat{\mathcal{R}(n)}=\mathbb{C}(\Gr_{m,n}),
together with a promotion map (
6
) associated to each brushed dominant solvable plabic tangle
(G,\mathbf{D}).

Observation 9.7
.

The algebra
\mathcal{A}^{\mathrm{ds}}_{m}

is an algebra over the suboperad
\mathcal{P}^{\mathrm{ds}}_{m}
. The algebra
\widehat{\mathcal{A}^{\mathrm{ds}}_{m}}

is an algebra over the suboperad
\widehat{\mathcal{P}^{\mathrm{ds}}_{m}}
.
The only non-trivial property to verify is the behaviour under composition, which follows from
Proposition

9.4
.

We now sketch how to extend the above definitions to the operad of rank-
m
regular tangles (which we do not require to be dominant or solvable).
It will be more convenient to focus on geometric promotion rather than algebraic promotion.

Definition 9.8
.

For a rank
-m
regular plabic tangle
(G,\mathbf{D})
with

\mathbf{D}=(D^{(1)},\dots,D^{(\ell)})

we define
boundary
m
-VRCs
for
(G,\mathbf{D})
as collections of elements

(\mathbf{z},\mathbf{w}^{(1)},\ldots,\mathbf{w}^{(\ell)})=(z_{1},\ldots,z_{n};w^{(1)}_{1},\ldots,w^{(\ell)}_{|D^{(\ell)}|})\in\Conf^{\circ}_{m,n}\times\Conf^{\circ}_{m,D^{(1)}}\times\dots\times\Conf^{\circ}_{m,D^{(\ell)}}

for which there exists an
m
-VRC
[\mathbf{v},\mathbf{R}]\in\mathcal{C}^{m}_{G}
which restricts to

(\mathbf{z},\mathbf{w}^{(1)},\ldots,\mathbf{w}^{(\ell)})
on the boundaries of the inner and outer disks.

We similarly define
boundary
m
-VRCs
for a brushed tangle as collections

(\mathbf{z},\mathbf{w}^{(1)},\ldots,\mathbf{w}^{(\ell)})\in\Gr_{m,n}\times\Gr_{m,D^{(1)}}\times\dots\times\Gr_{m,D^{(\ell)}}

where we use the brushing to lift the points to the Grassmannian.

Definition 9.9
.

We associate to the tangle
(G,\mathbf{D})=(G,(D^{(1)},\dots,D^{(\ell)}))
the space

F_{(G,\mathbf{D})}\hookrightarrow\Conf^{\circ}_{m,n}\times\Conf^{\circ}_{m,D^{(1)}}\times\dots\times\Conf^{\circ}_{m,D^{(\ell)}},

of all boundary
m
-VRCs for
(G,\mathbf{D}).

We similarly define
{F}_{\widehat{(G,\mathbf{D})}}
for a brushed tangle by replacing everywhere
\Conf^{\circ}
by
\Gr.

Note that
F_{1_{n}}
is just the diagonal in
\Conf^{\circ}_{m,n}\times\Conf^{\circ}_{m,n}.

When the tangle is generically solvable,
F_{(G,\mathbf{D})}
is the graph of the
geometric promotion map from (
5
).
However,
F_{(G,\mathbf{D})}
makes sense more generally, e.g. when we have
intersection number greater than
1
. In
Section

10
we will give an example of a promotion map corresponding to the
4
-mass box
, which has intersection number
2
. Such a promotion can be thought of as a multivalued function or a function from some Galois cover of the Grassmannian.

In this more general setting we can define an analogue of the algebraic promotion map from
Equation

6
, where the domain
is the coordinate ring of
F_{(G,\mathbf{D})}
, which
can usually be written in the form

R_{(G,\mathbf{D})}:=\left(\mathbb{C}(\Conf^{\circ}_{m,D^{(1)}})\otimes_{\mathbb{C}}\dots\otimes_{\mathbb{C}}\mathbb{C}(\Conf^{\circ}_{m,D^{(\ell)}})\otimes_{\mathbb{C}}\mathbb{C}(\Conf^{\circ}_{m,n})\right)/\mathcal{I}_{(G,\mathbf{D})}

where
\mathcal{I}_{(G,\mathbf{D})}
is the ideal defining (the closure of)
F_{(G,\mathbf{D})}.

In this language, the composition of tangles corresponds geometrically to a projection of the fibered product.
Let the
fibered product

F_{(G,\mathbf{D})}\times_{\Conf^{\circ}_{m,D^{(i)}}}F_{(G^{\prime},\mathbf{E})}
denote the space of pairs of boundary
m
-VRCs of the form

\left((\mathbf{z};\mathbf{w}^{(1)},\ldots,\mathbf{w}^{(i)},\ldots,\mathbf{w}^{(\ell)}),(\mathbf{w}^{(i)};\mathbf{u}^{(1)},\dots,\mathbf{u}^{(r)})\right)\in F_{(G,\mathbf{D})}\times F_{(G^{\prime},\mathbf{E})}.

Let

\mathrm{Pr}_{\Conf^{\circ}_{m,n}\times\prod_{j=1,j\neq i}^{\ell}\Conf^{\circ}_{m,D^{(j)}}\times\prod_{h=1}^{\ell}\Conf^{\circ}_{m,E^{(h)}}}
be the projection map which maps this element to

(\mathbf{z};\mathbf{w}^{(1)},\ldots,\widehat{\mathbf{w}^{(i)}},\mathbf{u}^{(1)},\ldots\mathbf{u}^{(r)},\ldots,\mathbf{w}^{(\ell)}).

The following observation generalizes the composability part of
Proposition

9.4
, and
9.7
.

Observation 9.10
.

We have

F_{(G,\mathbf{D})\circ_{i}(G^{\prime},\mathbf{E})}=\mathrm{Pr}_{\Conf^{\circ}_{m,n}\times\prod_{j=1,j\neq i}^{\ell}\Conf^{\circ}_{m,D^{(j)}}\times\prod_{h=1}^{\ell}\Conf^{\circ}_{m,E^{(h)}}}\left(F_{(G,\mathbf{D})}\times_{\Conf^{\circ}_{m,D^{(i)}}}F_{(G^{\prime},\mathbf{E})}\right).

(The statement for brushed tangles is analogous, but with Grassmannians replacing configuration spaces everywhere.)

The symmetric group
S_{\ell}
acts on the
\ell
input spaces in the fibered product by permuting them. It holds that for all
\mathbf{d}=(d_{1},\ldots,d_{\ell})
,
n
,
(G,\mathbf{D})\in\mathcal{P}(n,\mathbf{d}),
and
\sigma\in S_{\ell}
,

F_{\sigma^{\star}(G,\mathbf{D})}=\sigma\cdot F_{(G,\mathbf{D})},

where on the right hand side,

\sigma
acts by permuting the corresponding elements of the product.
This leads to the following definition.

Definition 9.11
.

Let
\mathcal{P}^{\prime}
be a suboperad of the plabic operad
\mathcal{P}
or the brushed plabic operad
\widehat{\mathcal{P}}
.
A
generalized topological representation
of
\mathcal{P}^{\prime}
is a collection of topological spaces
\mathcal{F}(n)
for
n\geq 0
, as well as
a topological space
\mathcal{F}{(G,\mathbf{D})}\subseteq\mathcal{F}(n)\times\bigtimes_{i=1}^{\ell}\mathcal{F}(|D^{(i)}|)
for every
(G,\mathbf{D})=(G,(D^{(1)},\dots,D^{(\ell)}))\in\mathcal{P}^{\prime},
7
7

7

When
\ell=0
, we have
\mathcal{F}(G)\subseteq\mathcal{F}(n).
satisfying the following properties:

•

identity
:
\mathcal{F}(1_{n})
is the diagonal of
\mathcal{F}(n)\times\mathcal{F}(n)
;

•

composition
:

\mathcal{F}\Big((G,\mathbf{D})\circ_{i}(G^{\prime},\mathbf{E})\Big)=\mathrm{Pr}_{\mathcal{F}(n)\times\prod_{j=1,j\neq i}^{\ell}\mathcal{F}(|D^{(j)}|)\times\prod_{h=1}^{\ell}\mathcal{F}(|E^{(h)}|}\mathcal{F}{(G,\mathbf{D})}\times_{\mathcal{F}(|D^{(i)}|)}\mathcal{F}{(G^{\prime},\mathbf{E})}.

•

symmetric group action
: for
\sigma\in S_{\ell}

\sigma\cdot\mathcal{F}(G,\mathbf{D})=\mathcal{F}(\sigma^{*}(G,\mathbf{D})),

where
\sigma\cdot
permutes the components
\mathcal{F}(|D^{(i)}|)

and affects the projection accordingly.

The topological representation obtained by setting
\mathcal{F}(n)=\Conf^{\circ}_{m,n}
and
\mathcal{F}{(G,\mathbf{D})}={F}_{(G,\mathbf{D})}
is called the

\Amp_{m}
-topological
representation of the plabic operad. The topological representation obtained by setting
{\mathcal{F}}(n)=\Gr_{m,n}
and
{\mathcal{F}}{\widehat{(G,\mathbf{D})}}={F}_{\widehat{(G,\mathbf{D})}}
is called the
\Amp_{m}
-topological representation of the brushed plabic operad
.

We can obtain an
algebraic representation
from the
\Amp_{m}-
topological representation of the brushed plabic operad one by moving to coordinate rings.

For fixed
m
, an interesting suboperad is the suboperad of (brushed) dominant tangles whose core
G
has rank
k
and dimension
km
. One can show that the intersection number is multiplicative under composition. In
Section

10
we give discuss the induced map in the special case of the
4
-mass box.

The following conjecture generalizes the first item of
4.16
to higher intersection number.

Conjecture 9.12
.

Let
(G,\mathbf{D})
be a plabic tangle of dimension at most
km
which admits a brushing. Then there exists a brushing
\mathcal{B}
and a choice of signs
\boldsymbol{\sigma}
such that the following holds for the associate space
F_{\widehat{(G,\mathbf{D})}}
:

F_{\widehat{(G,\mathbf{D})}}\cap\Gr^{\geq 0}_{m,n}\times\Gr_{m,D^{(1)}}\times\dots\times\Gr_{m,D^{(\ell)}}\subseteq\Gr^{\geq 0}_{m,n}\times\Gr^{\geq 0}_{m,D^{(1)}}\times\dots\times\Gr^{\geq 0}_{m,D^{(\ell)}}

Similar operadic frameworks can be associated to the

momentum amplituhedron

[
DFLP19
]
and the
ABJM amplituhedra

[
HHK23
]
. In fact the
arc moves
of
[
PT25
]
are a special case of promotions in the ABJM amplituhedron setting.

Remark 9.13
.

The plabic operad has additional structures, such as boundary maps which are obtained by erasing an edge of the core
G
to obtain a new core
G^{\prime}\subset G
. These boundary maps are compatible with representations of the operad: the space
F_{(G^{\prime},\mathbf{D})}
is a subspace of the closure
\overline{F_{(G,\mathbf{D})}}
of
F_{(G,\mathbf{D})}
in
the Hausdorff topology.
We leave the study of this object to a future work.

9.3.
Modules

We now briefly remark on modules associated to operads of plabic tangles.

Definition 9.14
.

Let
\mathcal{R}
be an algebra over a suboperad
\mathcal{P}^{\prime}
plabic tangles. A
module
over
\mathcal{R}
is an assignment of an
\mathcal{R}(n)
-module

\mathcal{M}(n)
for every color of
\mathcal{P}^{\prime}
, as well as maps

\mathcal{M}(G,\mathbf{D}):\bigotimes_{D\in\mathbf{D}}\mathcal{M}(|D|)\to\mathcal{M}(n).

These maps satisfy analogous normalization, identity, composition and symmetric group action conditions as in
Definition

9.5
, and in addition,
for all
f_{i}\in\mathcal{R}(|D^{(i)}|)
and
m_{i}\in\mathcal{M}(|D^{(i)}|),
with
i=1,\ldots,\ell,

(14)

\mathcal{M}(G,\mathbf{D})(f_{1}m_{1}\otimes\cdots\otimes f_{\ell}m_{\ell})=\mathcal{R}(G,\mathbf{D})(f_{1}\otimes\cdots\otimes f_{\ell})\ \mathcal{M}(G,\mathbf{D})(m_{1}\otimes\cdots\otimes m_{\ell}),

compatible with (
13
). We can similarly define a brushed version.

The next definition is motivated by the
canonical form
of amplituhedron cells
[
AHBC+16b
,
Lam16b
]
.

Definition 9.15
.

Let
\mathcal{M}(n)
to be the module of meromorphic forms on
\Gr_{m,n}.

The
canonical form
of a plabic graph is the meromorphic form
\bigwedge_{i=1}^{d}\frac{dx_{i}}{x_{i}},
where
(x_{i})_{i=1}^{d}
is any coordinate system associated to a plabic graph, such as the edge weights (modulo gauge). Let
\widehat{(G,\mathbf{D})}
be a brushed
m
-generically solvable plabic tangle, where
G
is of type
(k,n)
.
We define

\displaystyle\mathcal{M}(\widehat{G,\mathbf{D}}):\mathcal{M}(|D^{(1)}|)\otimes\dots\otimes

\displaystyle\mathcal{M}(|D^{(\ell)}|)\to\mathcal{M}(n)

by

\displaystyle(\omega_{1}\otimes\cdots\otimes\omega_{\ell})\mapsto\Omega_{G}\wedge\psi^{*}\omega_{1}\wedge\cdots\wedge\psi^{*}\omega_{\ell},

where
\Omega_{G}
is the push-forward to
\Gr_{m,n}
of the canonical form of
G
via the map
\Upsilon_{Z,G}
from (
2
) which associates a point of
\Pi_{G}\subset\Gr_{k,n}
to a point of
\Gr_{m,n}
, and
\psi^{*}=\psi^{*}_{\widehat{(G,\mathbf{D})}}
is the pull-back of the forms by the geometric promotion defined in
Definition

4.8
.

If
\ell=0
then
\mathcal{M}(\widehat{G,\emptyset})
is a meromorphic form on
\Gr_{m,n}
which agrees with the canonical form of the positroid cell
S_{G}

[
AHBC+16b
,
Lam16b
]
. This definition can be extended beyond the dominant solvable case using the
\Amp_{m}
-topological
representation of the brushed plabic operad.

Example 9.16
.

Consider the BCFW tangle described in
Definition

5.4
. The map it induces is

\displaystyle\omega_{L}\otimes\omega_{R}\mapsto\frac{d\langle bcdn\rangle}{d\langle abcd\rangle}\wedge\frac{d\langle acdn\rangle}{d\langle abcd\rangle}\wedge\frac{d\langle abdn\rangle}{d\langle abcd\rangle}\wedge\frac{d\langle abcn\rangle}{d\langle abcd\rangle}\wedge\psi^{*}_{BCFW}\omega_{L}\wedge\psi^{*}_{BCFW}\omega_{R},

where
\psi^{*}_{BCFW}
is the pullback along the map
\psi_{BCFW}
associated to the map
\Psi_{BCFW}
of
Definition

5.4
. This map is well known from the study of the BCFW recursion
[
BH15
]
, where if
\omega_{L},\omega_{R}
are the canonical forms of the
\tilde{Z}
-images of the left and right blobs, then the left hand side of the above equation is the canonical form of the
\tilde{Z}
-image of the composed cell.

The above definitions extend to e.g. the momentum and ABJM amplituhedron.

10.
Beyond intersection number one and cluster algebras

While so far we have considered mainly the case of intersection number
1
positroid varieties, one can also define promotions for more general plabic tangles, as was mentioned in
Section

9
. In this section we provide an example of a plabic tangle whose core is the
4
-mass box (see
Figure

11
) which indexes an intersection number
2
positroid variety. While the promotion will not yield a quasi-cluster homomorphism between the coordinate rings of the corresponding Grassmannians, we will prove that this promotion has remarkable positivity properties (
Theorem

10.10
).

10.1.
4
-Mass Box Promotion

We describe unary promotion by a
4
-mass box plabic graph as in
Figure

11
. The core is a
4-
mass box cell with inner disks. Since the cell has
m
-intersection number
2
, where
m=4
, there are generically
2
solutions for the vector configuration of the core.

Remark 10.1
.

Note that vector-relation configurations for the
4
-mass box plabic graph, with
m=4
, encodes the following classical Schubert problem:

how many lines in
\mathbb{P}^{3}
meet four general lines?
Here e.g. the vectors at
1
and
2
span a line, as do the vectors at
3
and
4
, the vectors at
5
and
6
, and the vectors at
7
and
8
.

In the following proposition, we describe how these
2
solutions give rise to two promotion maps.

1
2
3
4
5
6
7
8
n
\star
w
u
v
1
2
7
8
\star

Figure 11.
A brushed plabic tangle whose core is the 4-mass box plabic graph, which has
4
-intersection number 2. As usual, the brushing is indicated by the labels of the blob’s vertices.

Proposition 10.2
.

The plabic tangle from
Figure

11
gives rise to two corresponding promotion maps
\Psi_{\pm}:\mathbb{C}(\widehat{\Gr}_{4,N^{\prime}})\to\mathbb{C}(\widehat{\Gr}_{4,n})[\sqrt{\Delta}],

where
N^{\prime}=\{1,2,7,8,\ldots,n\}
and

(15)

z_{2}\mapsto\frac{12*56X_{\pm}}{\langle 156X_{\pm}\rangle},\quad z_{7}\mapsto X_{\pm}:=z_{7}+\alpha_{\pm}z_{8},

(16)

\alpha_{\pm}=\frac{-B\pm\sqrt{\Delta}}{2A},\quad\Delta:=B^{2}-4AC

(17)

A=Y_{88},\,\,B=Y_{78}+Y_{87},\,\,C=Y_{77},

where
Y_{ij}:=\langle 12i*43*56j\rangle
are cluster variables for
\Gr_{4,n}
when
i,j\in\{7,\ldots,n\}
.

Remark 10.3
.

Geometrically, referring to the terminology of
Section

9
, if we denote the brushed tangle inducing this promotion by
\widehat{(G,D)},
then
{F}_{\widehat{(G,D)}}
can be identified with the graph of a map from the Galois double cover of
\Gr_{4,n}
defined by extending
\mathbb{C}(\widehat{\Gr_{4,n}})
by
\sqrt{\Delta},
to
\Gr_{4,N^{\prime}},
and we may view the promotion maps representing that graph.
Interestingly, this map from the Galois cover can be presented as different global branches,
\Psi_{\pm}.
We conjecture that this phenomenon of different well-defined global branches on the positive Grassmannian, holds for all cells of intersection number greater than one, which is equivalent to saying that certain expressions which describe branching loci of the promotion map from the Galois cover are positive on the positive Grassmannian.

Proof of
Proposition

10.2
.

The white vertex attached to
7,8
enforces, up to gauge, a linear relation
v=z_{7}+\alpha z_{8}
, with
\alpha
a coefficient to be determined. Analogously, we have
u=z_{3}+\beta z_{4}
. The vector
w
associated to the black vertex between the two white vertices close to
1,2
has to belong to
(12)
and
(uv)
. These two subspaces intersect trivially if they are generic, therefore they need to satisfy:
\langle 12uv\rangle=0
. Analogously, considering the vector
w
on the black vertex between the two white vertices close to
5,6
, we need
\langle 56uv\rangle=0
. The two conditions give the following constraints on
\alpha,\beta
:

(18)

\langle ij37\rangle+\alpha\langle ij38\rangle+\beta\langle ij47\rangle+\alpha\beta\langle ij48\rangle=0,\quad\text{ for }\{i,j\}\in\{\{1,2\},\{5,6\}\}.

If we solve the equation where
\{i,j\}=\{1,2\}
for
\beta
and substitute it in the other equation we get the following quadratic equation for
\alpha
:

(19)

A\alpha^{2}+B\alpha+C=0,

where
A,B,C
are as in
Equation

17
. Hence there are
2
solutions for
v
, which we denote as
X_{\pm}=z_{7}+\alpha_{\pm}z_{8}
, with
\alpha_{\pm}
as in
Equation

16
.

Since
\langle 12uv\rangle=0
, the vectors satisfy a linear relation of the kind:
\gamma z_{1}+z_{2}+\delta u+\epsilon v=0
, where the coefficients are non-zero. Moreover, a vector
w
in the intersection of
(12)
and
(uv)
is
w=\gamma z_{1}+z_{2}=-\delta u-\epsilon v
. By wedging the linear relation with
5\wedge 6\wedge v
we get:

(20)

0=\gamma\langle 156v\rangle+\langle 256v\rangle+\delta\langle u56v\rangle+\epsilon\langle v56v\rangle=\gamma\langle 156v\rangle+\langle 256v\rangle,

because
\langle 56uv\rangle=0
and
v\wedge v=0
. Therefore, we can solve for
\gamma
and we can express
w
as:

(21)

w=-\frac{\langle 256v\rangle}{\langle 156v\rangle}z_{1}+z_{2}=\frac{12*56v}{\langle 156v\rangle}.

∎

Proposition 10.4

(
[
AHLS21
]
)
.

The function
\Delta
in
Equation

16
is positive on
\Gr^{>0}_{4,n}
.

Remark 10.5
.

The positivity of
\Delta
in
Proposition

10.4
is related to the existence of real solutions to the Schubert problem in
Remark

10.1
, which have been the subject of recent study, see e.g.
[
KP23
]
.

Remark 10.6
.

\Delta
is
nontrivially positive
, according to the definition in
[
AHLS21
]
. Whereas, all the following proofs will rely on sums or products of functions which are
Laurent positive
.

Lemma 10.7
.

Let
i\in\{9,\ldots,n\}
. Then
2A\langle 127i\rangle-B\langle 128i\rangle
is positive on
\Gr^{>0}_{4,n}
, where
A
and
B
are the functions in
Equation

17
.

Proof.

Let us write
2A\langle 127i\rangle-B\langle 128i\rangle
as

\displaystyle\big(\langle 1283\rangle\langle 4568\rangle-\langle 1284\rangle\langle 3568\rangle\big)\langle 127i\rangle+\big(\langle 1283\rangle\langle 4568\rangle-\langle 1284\rangle\langle 3568\rangle\big)\langle 127i\rangle-

\displaystyle\big(\langle 1273\rangle\langle 4568\rangle-\langle 1274\rangle\langle 3568\rangle+\langle 1283\rangle\langle 4567\rangle-\langle 1284\rangle\langle 3567\rangle\big)\langle 128i\rangle=

\displaystyle\langle 4568\rangle\big(\langle 1283\rangle\langle 127i\rangle-\langle 1273\rangle\langle 128i\rangle\big)+\langle 1283\rangle\big(\langle 4568\rangle\langle 127i\rangle-\langle 4567\rangle\langle 128i\rangle\big)+

\displaystyle\langle 3568\rangle\big(\langle 1274\rangle\langle 128i\rangle-\langle 1284\rangle\langle 127i\rangle\big)+\langle 1284\rangle\big(\langle 3567\rangle\langle 128i\rangle-\langle 3568\rangle\langle 127i\rangle\big)=

\displaystyle-\langle 4568\rangle\langle 1278\rangle\langle 123i\rangle+\langle 1283\rangle\langle 456*78*12i\rangle+\langle 3568\rangle\langle 1278\rangle\langle 124i\rangle+\langle 1284\rangle\langle 356*87*12i\rangle=

\displaystyle\langle 1278\rangle\langle 12i*43*568\rangle+\langle(128*34)65(78*12i)\rangle

The claim follows from the fact that
\langle 12i*43*568\rangle
and
\langle(128*34)65(78*12i)\rangle
are positive on
\Gr^{>0}_{4,n}
.
∎

Proposition 10.8
.

Let
X_{\pm}
be as in
Equation

15
. Then on the positive Grassmannian
\Gr^{>0}_{4,n}
, the following holds:

i)

\langle 12X_{\pm}i\rangle
is positive, for all
i\in\{9,\ldots,n\}
;

ii)

\langle 789*21*56X_{s}\rangle
and
\langle 156X_{s}\rangle
are both positive (negative), if
s=+
(
s=-
).

Proof.

Proof of i).

\displaystyle\langle 12X_{\pm}i\rangle=\langle 127i\rangle+\alpha_{\pm}\langle 128i\rangle=\langle 127i\rangle+\frac{-B\pm\sqrt{\Delta}}{2A}\langle 128i\rangle=\frac{1}{2A}\left(2A\langle 127i\rangle+(-B\pm\sqrt{\Delta})\langle 128i\rangle\right)

since
A>0
and
\langle 12X_{+}i\rangle>\langle 12X_{-}i\rangle
, it is enough to show that
2A\langle 12X_{-}i\rangle>0
:

\displaystyle 2A\langle 127i\rangle-(B+\sqrt{\Delta})\langle 128i\rangle>0\Leftrightarrow 2A\langle 127i\rangle-B\langle 128i\rangle>\sqrt{\Delta}\langle 128i\rangle

By
Lemma

10.7

2A\langle 127i\rangle-B\langle 128i\rangle>0
, we can square both sides and we obtain:

\displaystyle 4A^{2}\langle 127i\rangle^{2}+B^{2}\langle 128i\rangle^{2}-4AB\langle 127i\rangle\langle 128i\rangle>(B^{2}-4AC)\langle 128i\rangle^{2}\Leftrightarrow

\displaystyle A\langle 127i\rangle^{2}-B\langle 127i\rangle\langle 128i\rangle+C\langle 128i\rangle^{2}>0

\displaystyle A\langle 127i\rangle^{2}-B\langle 127i\rangle\langle 128i\rangle+C\langle 128i\rangle^{2}=\big(\langle 1283\rangle\langle 4568\rangle-\langle 1284\rangle\langle 3568\rangle\big)\langle 127i\rangle^{2}-

\displaystyle\big(\langle 1273\rangle\langle 4568\rangle-\langle 1274\rangle\langle 3568\rangle+\langle 1283\rangle\langle 4567\rangle-\langle 1284\rangle\langle 3567\rangle\big)\langle 127i\rangle\langle 128i\rangle+

\displaystyle\big(\langle 1273\rangle\langle 4567\rangle-\langle 1274\rangle\langle 3567\rangle\big)\langle 128i\rangle^{2}=

\displaystyle\langle 3568\rangle\langle 127i\rangle\big(\langle 1274\rangle\langle 128i\rangle-\langle 1284\rangle\langle 127i\rangle\big)+\langle 3567\rangle\langle 128i\rangle\big(\langle 1284\rangle\langle 127i\rangle-\langle 1274\rangle\langle 128i\rangle\big)+

\displaystyle\langle 1283\rangle\langle 127i\rangle\big(\langle 4568\rangle\langle 127i\rangle-\langle 4567\rangle\langle 128i\rangle\big)+\langle 1273\rangle\langle 128i\rangle\big(\langle 4567\rangle\langle 128i\rangle-\langle 4568\rangle\langle 127i\rangle\big)=

\displaystyle\langle 1278\rangle\langle 124i\rangle\big(\langle 3568\rangle\langle 127i\rangle-\langle 3567\rangle\langle 128i\rangle\big)+\langle 456*87*12i\rangle\big(\langle 1283\rangle\langle 127i\rangle-\langle 1273\rangle\langle 128i\rangle\big)=

\displaystyle\langle 1278\rangle\langle 124i\rangle\langle 356*78*12i\rangle-\langle 456*87*12i\rangle\langle 1278\rangle\langle 123i\rangle=

\displaystyle\langle 1278\rangle\langle(12i*34)65(78*12i)\rangle

Since
\langle(12i*34)65(78*12i)\rangle
is positive on
\Gr^{>0}_{4,n}
, this completes the proof.

Proof of ii)

We now prove that
\langle 156X_{s}\rangle
is positive if
s=+
and negative if
s=-
. The two cases, once multiplied by
2A>0
and rearranged read:

\displaystyle\sqrt{\Delta}\langle 1568\rangle>y\quad\mbox{and}\quad\sqrt{\Delta}\langle 1568\rangle>-y,\quad y:=B\langle 1568\rangle-2A\langle 1567\rangle.

Therefore we can square both sides to obtain:

\displaystyle\Delta\langle 1568\rangle^{2}>y^{2}\Leftrightarrow A\langle 1567\rangle^{2}-B\langle 1567\rangle\langle 1568\rangle+C\langle 1568\rangle^{2}<0.

By using steps similar to the ones in the proof of i) we can show that

\displaystyle A\langle 1567\rangle^{2}-B\langle 1567\rangle\langle 1568\rangle+C\langle 1568\rangle^{2}=-\langle 5678\rangle\langle(156*34)21(78*156)\rangle

where
\langle(156*34)21(78*156)\rangle
can be checked to be positive on
\Gr^{>0}_{4,n}
.

We now conclude the proof by showing that
\langle 789*21*56X_{s}\rangle
is positive for
s=+
and negative for
s=-
. Let use define
Y_{i}:=\langle 789*21*56i\rangle
, then
\langle 789*21*56X_{s}\rangle=Y_{7}+\alpha_{s}Y_{8}
. Following the same reasoning for the proof above for
\langle 156X_{s}\rangle
, we arrive that the statement to prove is equivalent to:

\displaystyle AY_{7}^{2}-BY_{7}Y_{8}+CY_{8}^{2}<0.

By using similar steps as in the previous proof and identities of the type

\displaystyle\langle 127i\rangle Y_{8}-\langle 128i\rangle Y_{7}=\langle 1278\rangle\langle 12i*65*789\rangle,\quad\mbox{and}\quad\langle 567i\rangle Y_{8}-\langle 568i\rangle Y_{7}=\langle 5678\rangle Y_{i},

one can show that

\displaystyle AY_{7}^{2}-BY_{7}Y_{8}+CY_{8}^{2}=-\langle 1278\rangle\langle 5678\rangle\langle(789*12)56*34*12(56*789)\rangle

where the quartic polynomial
\langle(789*12)56*34*12(56*789)\rangle
is positive on
\Gr^{>0}_{4,n}
.
∎

Lemma 10.9
.

Consider the rectangle seed
\Sigma
for
\Gr_{4,N^{\prime}}
in
8.9
. Let
x
be a cluster variable in
\Sigma
, then its image
\Psi_{\pm}(x)
under the
4
-mass box promotion is a positive function on
\Gr^{>0}_{4,n}
.

Proof.

Let us denote
F_{2}:=\langle 156X_{\pm}\rangle
and
F_{1}:=\langle 256X_{\pm}\rangle
. Then:

\displaystyle\Psi_{\pm}\big(\langle 1278\rangle\big)\;

\displaystyle=\;\left\langle 1\left(2-\tfrac{F_{1}}{F_{2}}1\right)\left(7+\alpha_{\pm}8\right)8\right\rangle\;=\;\langle 1278\rangle

\displaystyle\Psi_{\pm}\big(\langle 127i\rangle\big)\;

\displaystyle=\;\left\langle 12\left(7+\alpha_{\pm}8\right)i\right\rangle\;=\;\langle 12X_{\pm}i\rangle,\quad i\in\{9,\ldots,n\}

\displaystyle\Psi_{\pm}\big(\langle 78ij\rangle\big)\;

\displaystyle=\;\left\langle\left(7+\alpha_{\pm}8\right)8ij\right\rangle\;=\;\langle 78ij\rangle,\quad 2\not\in\{i,j\}

\displaystyle\Psi_{\pm}\big(\langle 2789\rangle\big)\;

\displaystyle=\;\left\langle\left(2-\tfrac{F_{1}}{F_{2}}1\right)\left(7+\alpha_{\pm}8\right)89\right\rangle\;=\;\frac{F_{2}\langle 2789\rangle-F_{1}\langle 1789\rangle}{F_{2}}=\frac{\langle 789*21*56X_{\pm}\rangle}{\langle 156X_{\pm}\rangle}

The rest of the variables in
\Sigma
are fixed by
\Psi_{\pm}
. It then follows from
Proposition

10.8
that
\Psi_{\pm}(x)
is positive on
\Gr^{>0}_{4,n}
for every variable
x
in
\Sigma
.
∎

Theorem 10.10

(
4
-Mass Box Cluster Positivity)
.

The
4
-mass box promotion map
\Psi_{\pm}
preserves positivity of cluster variables for
\Gr_{4,N^{\prime}}
, i.e. if
x
is a cluster variable for
\Gr_{4,N^{\prime}}
, then
\Psi_{\pm}(x)
is a positive function on
\Gr^{>0}_{4,n}
.

Proof.

If
x
is a cluster variable for
\Gr_{4,N^{\prime}}
, then by Laurent positivity of cluster algebras,
x
can be expressed as a Laurent polynomial of the cluster variables in the initial seed
\Sigma
with positive coefficients. By
Lemma

10.9
, the images under
\Psi_{\pm}
of all such cluster variables in the initial seed
\Sigma
will be positive functions on
\Gr^{>0}_{4,n}
, hence
\Psi_{\pm}(x)
will also be a positive function on
\Gr^{>0}_{4,n}
.
∎

Remark 10.11
.

Positivity is non-trivial already for Plücker coordinates. If
x=\langle 127n\rangle
, then

\Psi_{\pm}(x)=\langle 127n\rangle+\alpha_{\pm}\langle 128n\rangle,

is not a sum of positive terms, as
\langle 127n\rangle
and
\langle 128n\rangle
are positive and
\alpha_{\pm}
is negative on the positive Grassmannian.

We obtain the following as a corollary of
Theorem

10.10
.

Corollary 10.12
.

Let us consider the geometric promotion
\psi_{\pm}:\Gr_{4,n}\dashedrightarrow\Gr_{4,N^{\prime}}
, of which
\Psi_{\pm}
is the pullback. Then
\psi(\Gr^{>0}_{4,n})\subseteq\Gr^{>0}_{4,N^{\prime}}
.

In general, we make the following conjecture for positivity properties of higher intersection number tangles.

Conjecture 10.13
.

Recall
9.12
and
Remark

10.3
. Suppose
(G,\mathbf{D})
is a tangle which admits a brushing, and suppose that
\Pi_{G}
has dimension
km
and
\operatorname{IN}_{m}(G)>0
. Then we can find a brushing and signs for which
9.12
holds.

Moreover,
F_{\widehat{(G,\mathbf{D})}}\cap\Gr^{>0}_{m,n}\times\Gr^{>0}_{m,D^{(1)}}\times\dots\times\Gr^{>0}_{m,D^{(\ell)}}
is the disjoint union of a fixed number graphs of functions from
\Gr_{m,n}^{>0}
to
\prod_{D\in\mathbf{D}}\Gr^{>0}_{m,D}
.

Remark 10.14

(Positivity Phenomenon in Scattering Amplitudes)
.

Scattering amplitudes in planar
\mathcal{N}=4
SYM are conjectured to be regular on
\Gr^{>0}_{4,n}
. In other words, they have singularities on hypersurfaces
f(\textbf{z})=0
, where
f(\textbf{z})>0
when
\textbf{z}\in\Gr^{>0}_{4,n}
. We call this the ‘positivity phenomenon’ in planar
\mathcal{N}=4
SYM. When
f(\textbf{z})
is a rational function,
f(\textbf{z})
is conjectured to be a cluster variable for
\Gr_{4,n}
, hence
it is positive on
\Gr^{>0}_{4,n}
.
In our framework, we expect
f(\textbf{z})
to be the image of a cluster promotion map, and hence a cluster variable and positive on the positive Grassmannian.
This was proved when
f(\textbf{z})
is a singularity of a Yangian invariant
\mathcal{Y}_{G}
associated to a BCFW cell
S_{G}

[
ELP+23
]
in relation to
cluster adjacency conjecture
.

When
f(\textbf{z})
is an algebraic function, there is no general mechanism that would explain positivity. In special cases, there are some results in the frameworks of
limiting rays
and
cluster algebraic functions

[
cCcS18
,
DFGK21
,
AHLS21
]
. We expect that algebraic singularities of Yangian invariants
\mathcal{Y}_{G}
, with
\mbox{IN}_{4}(G)>1
, are
also
images of promotion maps. In the case of the four mass box promotion,
Theorem

10.10
shows that the map preserves positivity. We expect this to hold in general for promotion maps coming from a plabic graph
G
with
\mbox{IN}_{m}(G)>1
(
9.12
). Hence promotion maps should give a new mechanism to generate algebraic functions that are positive on
\Gr^{\geq 0}_{m,n}
and explain positivity of algebraic singularities of Yangian invariants. Noting that all algebraic singularities of scattering amplitudes in planar
\mathcal{N}=4
SYM that have been found so far are singularities of Yangian invariants, understanding promotion maps should be a stepping-stone towards proving the positivity phenomenon.

Remark 10.15
.

While the promotion in
Proposition

10.2
is not a quasi-cluster homomorphism between Grassmannian cluster algebras, it does take relations between cluster variables, e.g. the 3-term exchange relations, into highly non trivial relations between functions involving
\sqrt{\Delta}.

This hints at a more general algebraic structure governing the singularities of scattering amplitudes, beyond cluster algebras, where algebraic promotions would serve as morphisms of the associated category.

Appendix A
Background on plabic graphs

In
[
Pos06
]
,
Postnikov
classified the cells of the positive Grassmannian,
using equivalence classes of
reduced plabic graphs
,
and
decorated (or affine) permutations
.
Here we review some of this technology, following
[
FWZb
]
,

[
Pos06
]
, and
[
PSW07
]
.

Definition A.1

(Plabic graphs)
.

A
planar bicolored graph
(or “plabic graph”)
is a planar graph
G
properly embedded into a closed disk, such that
each internal vertex is colored black or white;
each internal vertex is connected by
a path to some boundary vertex;
there are vertices lying on the
boundary of the disk labeled
1,\dots,n

for some positive
n
;
and each of the boundary vertices is incident to a single
edge.
See Figure
12
for an example.

We note that segments of the boundary of the disk between boundary vertices of
G
are not edges of
G
. The faces of
G
are the connected components of the complement of
G
in the disk. We use the notation
E(G)
for the edges of
G
and
F(G)
for the faces.

Figure 12.
A plabic graph.

If
G
has an internal leaf which is incident to a boundary
vertex,
we call this a

lollipop
.

We will require that our plabic graphs are
leafless
, i.e. that
they have no internal leaves
except for lollipops.

There is a natural set of local transformations (moves) of plabic graphs:

(M1)
Square move
(or
urban renewal
). If a plabic graph has a square formed by
four trivalent vertices whose colors alternate,
then we can switch the
colors of these four vertices.

(M2)
Contracting/expanding a vertex
.
Two adjacent internal vertices of the same color can be merged or unmerged.

(M3)
Middle vertex insertion/removal
.
We can remove/add degree
2
vertices.

See
Figure

13
for depictions of these three moves.

Figure 13.
Local moves (M1), (M2), (M3) on plabic graphs.

Definition A.2
.

Two plabic graphs are
move-equivalent
if they can be obtained
from each other by moves (M1)-(M3). The
move-equivalence class

of a given plabic graph
G
is the set of all plabic graphs which are move-equivalent
to
G
.
A plabic graph
is called
reduced
if there is no graph in its move-equivalence
in which there is a
bubble
, that is,
two adjacent vertices
u
and
v

which are connected by
more than one edge.

Remark A.3
.

It is sometimes convenient to regard the boundary vertices of a plabic graph as being black. Note that
given any plabic graph
G
, with boundary vertices regarded as black vertices, we can always apply moves (M1)-(M3) to find a move-equivalent graph
G^{\prime}
which is
bipartite.

Definition A.4

(Perfect orientation)
.

A
perfect orientation
(respectively, a
reverse perfect orientation
)
\mathcal{O}
of a plabic graph
G
is a
choice of orientation of each edge such that each
internal black (respectively, white) vertex
u
is incident to exactly one edge
directed away from
u
; and each internal white (respectively, black) vertex
v
is incident
to exactly one edge directed towards
v
.
A plabic graph
is called
perfectly orientable
if it has a perfect orientation.
Let
G_{\mathcal{O}}
denote the directed graph associated with a perfect orientation
\mathcal{O}
of
G
. The
source set

I_{\mathcal{O}}\subset[n]
of a perfect orientation
\mathcal{O}
is the set of boundary vertices
i
for which
i

is a source of the directed graph
G_{\mathcal{O}}
. A
flow

F
from
I_{\mathcal{O}}
to
J
is a collection of vertex-disjoint directed paths in
G_{\mathcal{O}}
whose starting points are
I_{\mathcal{O}}
and whose ending points are
J
.

If
G
has
n
boundary vertices, and
k:=I_{\mathcal{O}}
is the size of a (equivalently, any) perfect orientation, then we say that
G

has type
(k,n)
.

Proposition A.5
.

[
Pos06
, Proposition 11.7, Lemma 11.10]

Let
G
be a plabic graph. Then there is a matroid

\mathcal{M}_{G}
called a
positroid
whose bases
are precisely the subsets

\{I\ |\ I=I_{\mathcal{O}}\text{ for some perfect
orientation }\mathcal{O}\text{ of }G\}.

Lemma A.6
.

[
PSW07
, Lemma 3.2]

Each reduced plabic graph
G
on
[n]
has an acyclic perfect orientation. Moreover,
for each total order
<_{i}
on
[n]
defined by

i<_{i}i+1<_{i}\dots<_{i}n<_{i}1<_{i}\dots<_{i}i-1
,

G
has an acyclic perfect orientation
\mathcal{O}
whose source set
I_{\mathcal{O}}

is the lexicographically minimal basis with respect to
<_{i}
.

Given a perfect orientation
\mathcal{O}
of a plabic graph
G
of type
(k,n)
,
one can describe explicitly an associated cell
S_{G}
of
\Gr_{k,n}^{\geq 0}
; we therefore say that

G
and
S_{G}
have
rank
k
. It turns out that the cell depends only
on the move-equivalence class of
G
, not on
G
or the choice of perfect orientation.
The description of the cell is particularly simple
when
\mathcal{O}
is acyclic.

Definition A.7

(Path matrix and boundary measurement map)
.

Let
G
be a
plabic graph of type
(k,n)

with an acyclic perfect orientation
\mathcal{O}
of
G
.
Let us associate a variable
x_{e}
with each edge
e\in E(G)
of
G
.
For
i\in I_{\mathcal{O}}
and
j\notin I_{\mathcal{O}}
,
define the
boundary measurement

M_{ij}
as the following expression:

M_{ij}:=\sum_{P}\left(\prod_{e\in P}x_{e}/\prod_{e^{\prime}\in P}x_{e^{\prime}}\right),

where the sum is over all directed paths in
G_{\mathcal{O}}
that start
and end at the boundary vertices
i
and
i
,
the first product
is over all edges
e
in
P
which are oriented white-to-black, and the second product is over all edges
e^{\prime}
in
P
which are oriented black-to-white.
The
path matrix

A=A(G,\mathcal{O})=(a_{ij})

is the unique
k\times n
matrix with rows indexed in increasing order by

I_{\mathcal{O}}
such that

•

The
k\times k
submatrix of
A
in the column set
I_{\mathcal{O}}
is the identity matrix.

•

For any

i\in I_{\mathcal{O}}
and
j\notin I_{\mathcal{O}}
, the entry
a_{ij}
equals

\pm M_{ij}
.

•

All Plücker coordinates of
A
are subtraction-free expressions in the variables
x_{e}
.

Proposition A.8
.

Let
G,\mathcal{O}
be as above. The path matrix
A(G,\mathcal{O})
has Plücker coordinates

\langle J\rangle_{A}=\sum_{F}\left(\prod_{e\in F}x_{e}/\prod_{e^{\prime}\in F}x_{e^{\prime}}\right)

where the sum is over flows
F
from the source set
I_{\mathcal{O}}
to
J
, the first product
is over all edges
e
in
F
which are oriented white-to-black, and the second product is over all edges
e^{\prime}
in
F
which are oriented black-to-white.

We can perform certain operations, called
gauge transformations
,
which preserve the boundary measurements
M_{ij}
. The following lemma is
easy to verify.

Lemma A.9

(Gauge transformations)
.

Let
G,\mathcal{O}
be as in
Definition

A.7
and assign to each edge of
G
a variable
x_{e}
. For each internal vertex
v
, choose a number
t_{v}\in\mathbb{C}^{*}
, and for each edge
e=\{u,v\}
, let
x_{e}^{\prime}=x_{e}t_{u}t_{v}
. Then the boundary measurements
M_{ij}
obtained from edge weights
x_{e}
are the same as those obtained from edge weights
x_{e}^{\prime}
.

Definition A.10
.

Let
G,\mathcal{O}
be as in
Definition

A.7
.
The map

\mathbb{B}_{G}:(\mathbb{C}^{*})^{E(G)}/gauge\to\Gr_{k,n}

sending

\{x_{e}\}\mapsto A
is the
boundary measurement map
.

We note that the boundary measurement map can also be defined in terms of a plabic graph and a perfect orientation containing cycles; however, the definition is a little more complicated. One can also equivalently define the boundary measurement map in terms of matchings, see
[
PSW07
, Corollary 4.7]
. The matching formulation shows that while the path matrix
A(G,\mathcal{O})
depends on the choice of perfect orientation
\mathcal{O}
, the resulting point in the Grassmannian does not. This justifies the absence of
\mathcal{O}
from the notation
\mathbb{B}_{G}
for the boundary measurement map.

Theorem A.11

(
[
MS17
, Theorem 7.1]
)
.

Let
G
be a reduced plabic graph. The boundary measurement map
\mathbb{B}_{G}
is an isomorphism onto its image
T_{G}
. Moreover,
T_{G}
is a Zariski-open and dense subset of
\Pi_{G}
.

We call the image
T_{G}
of
\mathbb{B}_{G}
the
boundary measurement torus
. Its dimension is
\dim T_{G}=|F(G)|-1
, where
F(G)
is the set of faces of
G
.

It will sometimes be useful to “use up” the gauge transformations by making some edge weights equal to 1.

Definition A.12
.

A
gauge-fix
of a plabic graph
G
is a partition
E(G)=E_{1}\sqcup E_{\neq 1}
so that, if one uses gauge transformations to set the weight of all edges in
E_{1}
to 1, boundary measurement becomes an isomorphism
\mathbb{B}_{G}:(\mathbb{C}^{*})^{E_{\neq 1}}\to T_{G}.

We have the following characterization of gauge-fixes.

Proposition A.13

(
[
Lam16b
, Lemma 13.1]
)
.

A partition
E(G)=E_{1}\sqcup E_{\neq 1}
is a gauge-fix if and only if
E_{1}
is a spanning forest of
G
where each connected component contains exactly one boundary vertex.

We will need the following lemma relating boundary measurement tori for
G
and
G^{op}
, where
G^{op}
is the graph obtained from
G
by switching the color of every vertex.

Lemma A.14
.

Let
G
be a plabic graph and let
N
be the
n\times n
diagonal matrix with diagonal
1,-1,\dots,(-1)^{n-1}
. Choose
\mathbf{x}\in(\mathbb{C}^{*})^{E(G)}/gauge
and set
V:=\mathbb{B}_{G}(\mathbf{x})
and
W:=\mathbb{B}_{G^{op}}(\mathbf{x})
. Then
W=(VN)^{\perp}=V^{\perp}N
.

Proof.

Consider an acyclic perfect orientation
\mathcal{O}
for
G
with source set
I_{\mathcal{O}}
. We use this orientation to compute
\mathbb{B}_{G}
. For
\mathbb{B}_{G^{op}}
, we use the perfect orientation
\mathcal{O}^{\prime}
obtained by reversing the orientation of each edge. It has sources
I_{\mathcal{O}}^{c}
.

Let
V=\mathbb{B}_{G}(\{x_{e}\})
. Then by
Proposition

A.8
,
\langle J\rangle_{V}
is the generating function for flows in
\mathcal{O}
from
I_{\mathcal{O}}
to
J
. On the other hand, let
W=\mathbb{B}_{G^{op}}(\{x_{e}\})
. The Plücker coordinate
\langle J^{c}\rangle_{W}
is the generating function for flows in
\mathcal{O}^{\prime}
from
I_{\mathcal{O}}^{c}
to
J^{c}
, and is equal to
\langle J\rangle_{V}
. Now
[
Kar17
, Lemma 1.11(2)]
implies that
W=V^{\perp}N
. The equality
V^{\perp}N=(VN)^{\perp}
is straightforward.
∎

Theorem A.15
.

[
Pos06
]

Let
G
and
\mathcal{O}
be as in
Definition

A.7
.
Then for any positive real values of the edge variables
x_{e}
,
the realizable matroid associated to the path matrix
A(G,\mathcal{O})
is the
positroid

\mathcal{M}_{G}
from

Proposition

A.5
.
Let
S_{G}\subset\Gr_{k,n}^{\geq 0}
denote
the set of all
k
-planes in
\mathbb{R}^{n}
spanned by the
path matrices
A=A(G,\mathcal{O})
, as each edge variable
x_{e}
ranges over
\mathbb{R}_{>0}
.
Then
S_{G}
is homeomorphic to an open ball, called a
positroid cell
.
If
G
is reduced, then
S_{G}
has dimension

r(G)-1
, where
r(G)
is the number of regions of
G
.
We have that
\Gr_{k,n}^{\geq 0}
is a disjoint union of positroid cells.

If
G
is a perfectly orientable graph which fails to be reduced,
then the dimension of
S_{G}
will be less than
r(G)
.

Theorem A.16
.

[
Pos06
, Theorem 18.5]

If
S_{G}

is a cell associated to plabic graph
G
,
then
every cell in the closure of
S_{G}
comes from a plabic graph
obtained from
G
by deleting some edges, and
conversely,
if we delete some edges of
G
, obtaining a perfectly orientable graph
H
,
it corresponds
to a cell
S_{H}
in the
closure of
S_{G}
.
